## Note on Accredited Entities on the use of the funding proposal template

- Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
- Accreditations are valid for 5 years from date of issue and must be renewed in accordance with the details provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.
- The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 60. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the usual service time limit.
- The recommended font is Arial, size: 11.

Under the GCF’s Policy on Access to Information, project and programme funding proposals will be disclosed on the GCF website simultaneously with the submission to the Board, subject to the redaction of any information that may be considered commercially sensitive or confidential to the GCF. Accredited Entities are asked to fill in information on disclosure in section G 4.

Please submit the completed proposal to: fundingproposal@unfccc.int

FP [Accredited Entity] [Eston Name] [County/Region] (YYYY/MM/DD)

### A.20. Executing Entity information

- **Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF):** Is a multilateral development bank with headquarters in Lima, Peru and offices in Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Guyana, Paraguay, Suriname, Uruguay, and the Caribbean. Spain and Portugal, as well as 13 private banks in the region. CAF provides financial support to public and private sector entities for projects that promote sustainable development, renewable resources, and support in the technical and financial structuring of sub-regional projects.
- **German Agency for International Cooperation (GIZ):** Is a public-benefit federal enterprise. GIZ is funded by the Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) and the German Federal Foreign Office (AA). GIZ is represented in 150 countries around the globe, with its headquarters located in Bonn, Germany. GIZ operates from about 120 countries around the globe. 70% of its 42,000 employees work abroad.

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation Program (hereinafter referred to as "E-Mobility or Program") aims to enhance the adoption of electric vehicles in Latin America by focusing on renewable energy sources leading to reduced fossil fuel consumption, greenhouse gas emissions and air pollution. E-Mobility will be implemented through two Sub-Programs: Sub-Program 1 (Sub-Prog) covers the four countries of Mexico, Brazil, Chile, and Peru; and one by CAF (Sub-Prog2) to cover the following three countries: Panama (PA), Paraguay (PY) and Uruguay (UY). The Program will also include a fourth sub-program focusing on local initiatives.

### Climate Context

Transport contributes almost one-quarter of the current global energy-related greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and is growing faster than any other energy-use end sector. Latin America already accounts for 10% of global GHG emissions

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 3

As of today, GHG emissions related to transport from many countries in Latin America represent even a higher proportion than global average. Limiting the global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing the way we produce and consume energy. This is especially true for the transport sector, which accounts for about one-third of total global CO2 emissions. The transport sector encompasses various transport modes, coupled with the low-carbon production of electricity and hydrogen (Paris Agreement). According to the International Energy Agency (IEA) modelling says electric vehicles (EVs) need to represent 30% of global sales in 2030. Latin America has not yet reached this level of electrification for passenger cars, but it is on track to reach a similar level as other high-generation power markets in the world (high hydroelectric production and growing deployment of wind and solar power plants). The electrification of transport can be achieved through two main strategies: 1) Electrifying the entire fleet of transport modes can reduce GHG emissions on a whole-to-wheel (on-road+base) by up to 90% (50-90%) compared to fossil fuel vehicles, and 2) Electrification of public transport can also contribute significantly.

**Avoid-Switch/Improve (ASI)** has been an long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in the transport sector. A new dimension needs to be added to this framework: EASD -Electric Avoid-Switch-Improve-. Electrification is a game-changer, and it will also have significant impacts on other dimensions of the ASI framework. The electrification of public transport measures alone will be capable to reach carbon neutrality in the transport sector. Electrification coupled with improvements in vehicle efficiency and operational practices can further reduce GHG emissions by up to 90% compared to fossil fuel vehicles.

Some countries have already started to implement policies that support the transition towards electric vehicles, such as fiscal incentives for electric buses, among others. Sub-program 2 countries have currently their own electrification strategies, but they are not yet fully implemented and need further support from the Program.

Electrification of public transport (PT) buses is an important component to modernize and upgrade the public transport system of countries. This results in new and attractive buses which incentivize passengers to use public transportation. Electrification also has a positive impact on the environment, as it reduces GHG emissions and improves air quality stability and economic efficiency of public transport operations and systems, e.g., through separation of bus ownership and operation, or by reducing maintenance costs.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE A

In LAC, approximately 100 million people are exposed to poor air quality, exceeding WHO guidelines. Despite poor information on air quality provided by environmental authorities, making it difficult to take action for critical air pollution reduction and control measures, the region has been a leader in implementing public transport systems (PT) following Organization Air Quality Guidelines*. Among the most popular cities in LAC are Santiago, Lima, Mexico City, or Curitiba. However, the majority of these cities have not yet implemented PT systems, with the exception of Bogotá (from 2015 to 2000) forcing governments to implement a poorly organized mass transit system transport, however, some of them have been able to implement PT systems in recent years. In addition, there are several initiatives that seek to improve this system as well as the e-buses introduced on its BRT. Chile has more than 800 eBuses through PPP and Medellín has 150.

This development condition with environmental and social pressure on local ecosystems, can get even worse. In particular further migration from rural to urban areas will increase the population density in the areas and the demand for public transport services. The lack of PT systems in LAC is a major problem that exacerbates this situation as it condition go hand in hand with an increase in further pollution of regional eco-system and natural resources. This is due to the fact that most cities are not following the principles of sustainability, towards clean energy, and low-carbon transportation in most countries in Latin America.

The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, from the health and economic impacts on their populations to the need to adapt their public transport systems. The pandemic has exposed weaknesses in the region's energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic recovery. A recent report prepared by the International Labor Organization (ILO) shows that 60% of Latin American countries have not yet implemented a Sustainable Energy Development Strategy (SEDS). Additionally, the pandemic has had an impact on women very hard according to the UN Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) the unemployment rate for women in the region is currently 12%. Many of them have been pushed out of the market. But this figure rises to 22-25% if we assume the same level of labor force participation as men. The pandemic has also had a significant impact on the informal sector, which represents almost half of the economically active population: forced to fully commit to care activities, they plainly aren't seeking new jobs. Moreover, women are more likely than men to be employed in sectors that have been most affected by the crisis and have been after economic crises. Taking into also account that the transportation sector is traditionally very masculinized, this means that women are even more vulnerable during the pandemic.

As well, it has the chance of reducing or broadening (whether gender issues are taken or not into account) the current disparities in access and use of transportations means by women, children, the elderly, people with disabilities and other vulnerable groups.

## Transport Context

Most of the transport sector was deregulated in the region for more than 3 decades until the beginning of the 21st century. The most common public transport systems in a few capital cities, most of the public transport (buses and taxis) and cargo, suffered of atomization, lack of quality service, road insecurity, poor labour conditions, pollution, and congestion, among others. In addition, the region has been characterized by high levels of informal employment in the sector. These changes in transit in Latin America are slow and only occur when technological progress allows them to set up the basis for a more efficient system. The most common public transport systems in LAC are: bus (60% of all trips), taxi (35%), shared taxis (10%). In terms of modal share, the daily trip rates (around 70% on most cities), mostly captive, and in the case of urban logistics the sector is one of the most informal.

Business models in transport in LAC had a major change with de appearance of BRT. Behind its advantages on operation and service driven by a new technology, the BRT system introduced a new conception based on better integration between public and private sectors. The main advantage of this type of system is that it can be partially serve by public buses to still fit in the urban logistics sector. In addition, BRT is one of the pillars on the ASI Framework for Sustainable Urban Transport (2015). However, the number of cities implementing BRT has been decreasing against other modes specially motorbikes, adding more pressure and negative externalities such as congestion, pollution, accidents, among others. The main reason behind this trend is that BRT almost every transport system in Latin America. Electrification in transport represent an opportunity to further plan urban logistics and reduce the use of fossil fuels. However, electrification has not been able to make public transport in effective and efficient, encourage modal shift from private transport means. Thus, modern and improved

## Intervention Strategy

The Program will implement interventions to kick-off EV deployment significantly earlier than under a BAU scenario, and at a much lower cost per vehicle and by comprehensive policy actions.

### Key Interventions of the Program

- **Function as a market accelerator enabling a faster uptake of e-mobility than under a BAU scenario.**
- **Focus on key markets and segments that are critical for achieving long-term targets by addressing comprehensively TA and FA. These interventions are made in a time where e-mobility is already at the tipping point, with significant potential to accelerate the transition towards sustainable transport.**

### Key Interventions

1. **Phase I (2023-2025):** Technical concepts have been tested positively and will be scaled up.
2. **Phase II (2026-2028):** The Program will focus on scaling up the technical concepts, including public-private partnerships (PPPs) for financing (e.g., concessional loans), and thus overcome one of the main barriers which is the EV CAPEX.

### Key Partnerships

The Program will work closely with the governments of the subprogram 2 countries and their main authorities, as it represents an NAF that is aligned to start the energy transition in a sustainable way. The Program also works closely with technical partners from the private sector who can provide added value that E-motion represents to PA, PY, and UY.

### Key Outcomes

The electrification of public transport is coupled with innovative business models, which will modernize the sector simultaneously. This will lead to a significant increase in the number of electric vehicles on the road, accompanied by improved services and new clean buses. This can reverse the trend of decreasing mode shares of public transport and contribute significantly to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The electrification of public transport is coupled with innovative business models that will modernize the sector simultaneously.

### Key Outcomes

- **Increased modal share for public transport:**
  - In Uruguay, the modal share has increased from 10% to 25%.
  - In Paraguay, the modal share has increased from 30% to 40%.

### Key Outcomes

The electrification of private transport is coupled with innovative business models that will modernize the sector simultaneously. This can reverse the trend of decreasing mode shares of public transport and contribute significantly to achieving the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The electrification of private transport is coupled with innovative business models that will modernize the sector simultaneously.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 6

## Country Context and Ownership

Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and e-mobility. The region has one of the highest levels of vehicle ownership per capita in the world due to a high share of private cars, leading to higher than average grid emission factor from the Program countries of this funding proposal (Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama) compared with other regions.

The adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the countries to move towards a decarbonisation pathway.


## The main motivating forces to foster e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are:

(i) reduce GHG emissions; (ii) reduce air pollution, especially PM2.5 and NOx; (iii) reduce dependence on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks; (iv) achieve positive economic impacts related to reduced health care costs and increased productivity; (v) improve road safety by reducing traffic accidents; (vi) address the needs that workers and caretakers face regarding urban transportation; (vii) provide local industries and suppliers in the region with new opportunities for growth, including the manufacturing of electric mass transport systems; and (ix) set the basis to facilitate a holistic change in urban clean mass mobility.

The updated National Determined Contributions (NDCs) of Sub-Program 2 countries all include measures to reduce transport emissions. The NDCs are part of the Paris Agreement and aim at achieving the global objectives until 2030: 10-50% of the total fleet of private vehicles will be electric; 25-45% of private vehicle sales will be electric by 2030; and 70% of new public transport vehicles will be electric. The NDCs also include that fleets will be made up of electric vehicles. Panama's updated NDC (2020) focuses mainly on mitigating emissions from the road sector, with a target to reduce CO2 emissions by 15% in 2030 and 40% in 2050 compared to 2018 levels; this represents the biggest challenge. Paraguay's NDC aims to reduce 20% of the fossil fuel consumption from road transport, which is equivalent to a reduction of 67% of projected emissions by 2030. Uruguay's NDC aims to reduce 10% of projected emissions by 2030. Additionally, a conditional goal of 10% reduction in emissions from road transport is included in the NDCs.

The countries are also working on electric vehicle (EV) infrastructure and charging stations. The main motion categories towards 2025: eBuses (110 units), and electric light commercial vehicles (LCVs) (900 units). A national strategy for EVs is being developed in Uruguay, which includes the installation of 300 charging points and a fast-charging network (no progress yet). However, these goals are part of a scenario conditional to additional means of financing. The countries have also started to develop strategies for electric vehicles and/or energy transition frameworks, roadmaps. Besides having "national EV strategies" or Energy Transition framework (see figure 1 in FP), in the three countries, there is a need to establish regulatory frameworks and policies that will support the development of infrastructure, in order to determine initiatives and actions to be taken; stakeholders and roles, resources and deadlines.

## Political support, governance and law enforcement

As part of the implementation of national e-mobility strategies and achieving countries' NDC targets is political support to induce organizational and/or regulatory changes in the status quo of mass transport systems that will generate a reduction in emissions. The establishment of an enabling environment for the development of the sector: the establishment must be firm, forward-looking, institutionally sound and credible to enact and enforce regulatory changes or other measures that are necessary to achieve the goals set out by the NDCs.

The GCF is supporting the countries with technical assistance (TA) in order to develop a national strategy component the political governance and regulatory enforcement in the countries to ensure long-term sustainability and transformation of mass transport systems. The TA will support the development of a National Strategy for EVs and a TA to kick start its adoption. Local authorities are working in electromobility aligned with their respective policy frameworks, but there is still a need to develop national strategies and regulatory frameworks that are consistent term to achieve 2030 VV goals, thus the need to find resources like GCF. This represents a good level of readiness to provide technical assistance for the development of national strategies and regulatory frameworks relative to the public and private stakeholders interviewed: 19 entities in PA, 14 entities in PY and 22 entities UY.

# Summary of policy framework where E-mobility will take place in Sub-program B countries

## Panama

- **National Energy Policy (NEP):** Promotes renewable energy sources and energy efficiency.
- **NMC (National Motor Cycle Council):** Sets standards for electric vehicles.

## Paraguay

- **National Energy Policy (NEP):** Encourages the use of electricity in public transport.
- **National Electric Mobility Program:** Includes a "electric mobility program in the public sector."
- **Electric Vehicle Charging Infrastructure:** Promotes the installation and maintenance of charging stations.
- **Guide for the establishment of electric mobiles:** Regulates registration standards.

## Uruguay

- **NEP (National Energy Policy):** Encourages renewable energy sources, including solar, wind, biomass, and hydroelectric power.
- **NMC (National Motor Cycle Council):** Sets standards for electric vehicles.
- **National Electric Mobility Program:** Includes a "electric mobility program in the public sector."
- **Charging Infrastructure:** Promotes the installation of charging stations.

## Barriers to EV Deployment

Main barriers to EV deployment are 0.23x higher Capital Expenditure (CAPEX) and EVs resulting in higher capital expenditure compared with conventional vehicles. The high CAPEX is due to the need for additional infrastructure, which is financially unviable in developing countries with long payback times.

The incremental CAPEX of e-buses attracts financial institutions through a combination of concessional loans and grants, as well as blended finance instruments such as blended equity and debt financing. This reduces the financial costs and technical assistance which promote new business models with lower costs for both public transport operators and governments. The second barrier is the lack of experience in operating electric vehicles, which increases operational risks.

Other "second level" barriers are: (i) governance structures and business models not conducive to the adoption of e-mobility; (ii) limited charging infrastructure; (iii) high costs for maintenance and operations; (iv) targets have been established but concrete and tangible support policies which are credible and sustainable do not exist.

The Private sector tackles these barriers and offers a comprehensive e-mobility package including financial and technical assistance thereby improving the policy framework and the ecosystem and removing barriers which allows to kick-start the market. The private sector can also provide the necessary infrastructure, such as charging stations, which enables this daily electrification adoption in 16-19 months.

The barriers of lack of financial profitability and high risks are tackled by a combination of concessional loans and grants, blended finance instruments (e.g., equity and debt financing), and technical assistance. This allows for the development of business models that can be replicated across countries through a knowledge exchange platform and allows to learn from experiences of other cities in Latin America, and through the implementation of pilot projects. The private sector also provides guarantees and insurance schemes as well as a bus investments e.g., through establishing concession contract lengths in line with the longer (longer) tenure of a buses or tender documents which favour the adoption of e-buses based on their positive environmental and health impact.

### Business Models

The business models stand out that can improve in a decisive manner the attractiveness and commercial viability of commercial e-bus sub-projets: (i) bulk purchases - that will be tackled through the TA; and (ii) separation of asset ownership from operation. The private sector is more likely to invest in e-mobility projects if it has control over the bus as this allows to optimize the e-mobility system in terms of charging infrastructure and bus types allowing e.g. to use fast-charging stations for buses and slow-charging stations for other vehicles. In addition, a good asset management is critical due to high investment costs of buses which require strong financial players with access to credit facilities. The private sector can also provide guarantees and insurance schemes as well as the long-term clients of FTA. Business models with asset separation have been successfully implemented in Chile with e-buses provided by the private sector, where the public transport operator is responsible for maintenance and operation.

Business models also need the regulatory framework defining payment schemes, procedures in case of bankruptcies of private operators, and insurance schemes. It is also essential to work on standardized bidding documents to acquire EVs in order to strengthen the ability to replicate this public model in Latin American cities. One of the main challenges for the deployment of EVs in these countries is the lack of charging infrastructure, which limits delivery vehicles for widespread commercial replication of e-buses (through the TA component); eBuses are in two phases: a) pilot phase and b) full-scale deployment phase. ### Program Components

The Program and its Sub-Program I have a comprehensive approach to establish an e-mobility conducive ecosystem at national level, including activities such as targeted policy advice; business model development; stakeholder management; and capacity building (all of which gender mainstreamed) to achieve a favorable environment for the deployment of EVs. The Program will also support the transition from public transport that is reiteratively making future investments into e-mobility of vehicles. The Program can significantly reduce investment risks through its focus on policy development, the creation of business models and a policy framework conducive to EV deployment.

For each country a market assessment (Panes) for Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama has been performed which includes: i) an analysis of the current situation regarding public transport in terms of vehicles ownership; period, cash-flow and debt structure; ii) required financial instruments and support structures to overcome viability barriers; iii) identification of key stakeholders and their roles in the EV market; iv) identification of policy measures designed to accelerate market readiness and to foster widespread commercial deployment of EVs without further financial burden on public transport operators. The assessment has been used as a basis for identifying conditions that are favourable for EVs (in terms of policies, energy prices, interest of investors); ii) targeted financial and technical assistance to overcome viability barriers; iii) identification of market conditions which would allow the end of the Program and a sustained uptake of EVs based on commercial conditions.

The Sub-Program 2 of the E-Motion Program is implemented in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama and has the following components:

* **Component 1:** Establishment of an e-mobility conducive ecosystem by providing Technical Assistance to create a policy and business framework conducive for massive deployment of EVs on a local project design; business model development; stakeholder coordination; support of national enabling policies for EV adoption, advice on vehicle re-usage; recycling and end-of-life management (including the creation of an e-mobility advisory committee and regular program monitoring).
* **Component 2:** Deployment of large-scale e-bus fleets (major investment component) coupled with possible, with innovative business models for e-buses based on a separation of asset ownership and operations modalities. The Program will support the deployment of e-buses in public transport systems that are efficient and sustainable.
* **Component 3:** Deployment of electric light commercial vehicles (public & private).
* **Component 4:** Deployment of large-scale fast-charging and general awareness infrastructure for the countries.
* **Program Management:** Establishing a Program Management Link (PML) for project implementation and impact monitoring.

The Program is a comprehensive and ambitious Gender Action Plan (GAP) targeting to improve gender equality and reduce gender-based inequalities in public transport.

### Sub-Program 2 Outcomes

The Sub-Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems in the target countries Paraguay, Panama and Uruguay. The Sub-Program shall help overcome barriers identified to kickstart the mass deployment of commercial EVs. Long-term outcomes to be achieved are: i) GHG Emissions have significantly reduced through the deployment of electric buses; ii) CO2 emissions per passenger-km has been reduced by 70% (through private vehicles) to bus carbon transport modes (public transport); iii) improved air quality; iv) reduced fossil fuel import dependency; v) improved fuel economy. EVs are up to four times more energy efficient than fossil vehicles.

* **Component 3 is for both, public and private fleets in light commercial or light passenger vehicles. Just as to deal with public fleets (as described above), the same applies also to private ones, i.e., a operation of many public entities within the countries that also needs to reconfigure its mobility operations.*

The Sub-Programme figures are:

* Total investment USD 121 MM
    * GCF investment USD 76.6 MM (33%) of which USD 66.8 MM (29%) loans and USD 9.8 MM (4%) grants
    * Projected co-financing USD 45 MM
    * Co-finance ratio 67%
* Mitigation potential: lifetime of assets 3.3 MtCO₂e directly of investment sub-projects
* GCF cost of intervention of investment sub-projects 23 USD tCO₂e for direct emission reductions
* Energy savings: 5,100 GWh energy avoided annually

Program Pipeline:

The E-Motion Sub-Programme 2 aims to finance one project in each participating country. The initial project pipeline includes 6 projects (3 in Panama, 4 in Paraguay and 4 in Uruguay; see Annex 2). The Program will finance directly individual sub-projects and will work also through financial intermediaries which provide loans to public or private companies. These financial intermediaries can be either government agencies or commercial banks channelled through Special Purpose Vehicles (public, mixed or private led), or through financial intermediaries and other entities that are not government agencies but have a mandate to finance projects in the energy sector. Financial intermediaries will use to deploy the finance to public and private companies and disbursements process of resources, are described in the section 8.3 (Delivery channels and new business models) and 8.4 (Implementation Arrangements).

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

B.1 Climate Change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

Climate Change Problem and the Relevance of Electric Mobility

Latin America already accounts for 10% of global GHG emissions as of today. Overall, the transport sector in Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay is responsible for about one-third of the countries' total CO2 emissions. For comparison, globally transportation is responsible for 24% of direct CO2 emissions from fuel combustion in 2019 [1]. In 2035, the share of electric vehicles (EVs) on the road will be around 7%, and by 2050 it could reach about 20%. The climate impact of the transport sector in the project countries is expected to increase even further in combination with economic growth. According to a study from the International Energy Agency, the transport sector accounts for 14% of global energy-related GHG emissions in LAC. In Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay, emissions from transportation are even higher than this average: 20%, 35% and 38% respectively.

In addition, NDCs for Panama and Uruguay are explicit in EV adoption, where E-motion would contribute with 150 and 400 EVs. In Paraguay, the NDC is more general but still indicates that E-motion would contribute to 200 EVs. In Panama targets range to 700 EVs and E-motion would contribute with 400 EVs. Finally, in the case of eLCVs, NDC in Uruguay targets to 900 EVs and E-motion would contribute with 100 EVs.

Current transport demand per capita in developing and emerging economies are far lower than in Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD) countries. However, it is expected that this will increase over the next decades due to rising incomes and development of infrastructure. GHG emissions from transport are anticipated to double by 2050 compared with today's levels [2]. The Paris Agreement aims at limiting global warming to well below 2 degrees Celsius, and limiting the global temperature increase to below 1.5 degrees Celsius requires changing this transport emissions profile.

[1] Climate Watch, Historical Emission: https://www.climatewatch.world/historical-emissions

[2] Climate Watch, Historical Emission: https://www.climatewatch.world/uruguay-emission

[3] National Development Strategy (NDS) of Uruguay: https://www.ministerioeducacion.gub.uy/sites/default/files/NDSE%202045.pdf

[4] IEA, Tracking Transport 2020: https://www.iea.org/reports/tracking-transport-2020

## Avoid-Shift-Increase (ASI) has been a long-standing framework for classifying mitigation interventions in the transport sector.

A new dimension needs to be added to this framework: ASI: Electrify-Avoid-Shift-Increase. Electrification is a policy lever that can be used to achieve multiple climate and energy security goals, including reducing GHG emissions, increasing occupation rates etc., typical efficiency measures. Electrification is a game-changer and a must if carbon intensity reduction targets are to be met. Electrification coupled with renewables, they have immense GHG reduction benefits, especially plugged into grids which are clean or have low carbon footprints. Electrification coupled with fossil fuels can also reduce emissions in the transport sector. Electrification coupled with a fossil-free grid creates this opportunity. Not surprisingly do carbon neutral buses and trains exist.

### Source: AFDF, 2021

The Program implements the required interventions to kick-start mass deployment significantly earlier than with a BAU scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and by comprehensive technical assistance. The Program only works in countries where the following conditions are met:

- **Electricity supply**: There is sufficient electricity generation capacity to meet the demand for scale e-mobility.
- **Market readiness**: There is a market that is ready to adopt electric vehicles (EVs) at scale, and there is a willingness of consumers to purchase EVs.

The Program does not have the same sequencing and activities as well as support levels in all countries. The Program is the first step towards a more comprehensive approach to e-mobility deployment. It is important to note that the Program acknowledges that there would be "ready-to-implement" opportunities in more organized and sophisticated segments (i.e., public transport, fleet electrification, etc.). These opportunities will be addressed through the E-Motion TA.

The Program has a clear roadmap for scaling up e-mobility deployment. The roadmap is based on the results of interviews from market studies and interviews with interviewees to work this roadmap through E-Motion TA so that financial resources are fully aligned with the needs of the market. The following sections describe the key elements of the roadmap, which have already been established (Colón City and Heredia City) groups of ex-individual vehicle owners, thus preparing their families for the transition towards electric vehicles.

### Strengthening Public Transport (PT)

In many countries, PT operators in countries resulting is decreasing mode shares of public transport (PT). In many countries the PT sector is under severe financial pressure with limited willingness of financial institutions to provide loans and grants. The result has been a reduction in service levels and an increase in fares to maximize revenue.

The fastest urbanization and motorization rates worldwide, the vehicle and motorcycle fleet in Latin America may triple by 2050. This will have significant implications for air quality, traffic congestion, and public health. In many countries, PT operators are under severe financial pressure with limited willingness of financial institutions to provide loans and grants.

The result has been a reduction in service levels and an increase in fares to maximize revenue. The result has been a reduction in service levels and an increase in fares to maximize revenue. This has had a negative impact on the modal share of public transport, which is already low in many countries.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

Electric mobility offers a unique opportunity to re-structure the PT sector, introduces new PPP and business models (ind. green roads of BEET) based on separation of asset ownership and operations, when possible, favours the entry of private investors in the sector, which can be an important source of funding for the transition towards electric users of PT. This disruptive, and largely unpopular among existing transport operators, move is promoted through e-vehicles, which are more attractive to customers than conventional vehicles due to their lower operating costs, and interests of different users' segments and profiles, such as women, other caretakers, children, people with disabilities. It also offers a new business model for the PT sector, which can be an important source of funding for the transition towards electric vehicles.

The electrification of public transport is expected to have several benefits: it reduces GHG emissions; it improves air quality and indoor health problems associated with air pollution; it reduces dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks.

### Local Context

Latin America has strong enabling conditions in terms of clean transport and electricity. This region has one of the highest penetration levels of renewable energy sources (high hydroelectric potential, wind and growing deployment of wind and solar power plants). Therefore, the adoption of an e-mobility strategy would allow the sector to move towards a decarbonization pathway that is more aligned with the global trend in other regions such as Europe or North America. With these grid factors EVs can reduce GHG emissions on a well-to-wheel (cradle-to-grave) base by 80-100% (2030+), compared to fossil fuels.

The region is also well-known for its electric vehicle (EV) infrastructure and public transport systems and cities like Bogotá, Medellín, Santiago and Curitiba have become international benchmarks for innovation in this area. Finally, Latin America has the largest lithium reserves in the world, with Chile being the largest producer of lithium carbonate in the region (Argentina, Brazil and Mexico) and Argentina, Bolivia, and Chile are called the "Lithium Triangle" as the three countries hold more than half of the world's lithium reserves that are an essential material for building electric batteries.

The main measures to promote e-mobility in the countries included in the Program are:

- Reduce GHG emissions;
- Improve air quality and indoor health problems associated with air pollution;
- Reduce dependency on imported fossil fuels and exposure to external price shocks;

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 16

## Background

- **Air pollution is a large problem in most Latin American cities with levels affecting seriously human health.** According to the WHO, there are more than 250 million people living in areas where air quality guidelines are not met.
- **The poor are disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work closer to its sources of emission.** The poor also have less access to clean air due to their lower ability to afford or access cleaner fuels, which is a major source of air pollution. The poor are disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work closer to its sources of emission.
- **There is no information available that correlates specific transport-related air pollution with health outcomes in Latin America.** However, studies show that in general, women bear the brunt of the burden of air pollution. Studies also show that in pollution in general affects more women than men. The differential consequences are in general attributed to women's roles as well as a combination of women's higher rates of caretaking activities and their higher poverty rates.
- **The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for Latin American countries, from the health perspective but also from the economic one.** It is creating new opportunities for sustainable development in energy, transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic recovery. A recent report prepared by the ILO shows that the pandemic could lead to a 20% increase in global poverty levels. The pandemic has led to significant savings of consumers on fossil fuels and vehicle maintenance resulting in increased spending on goods with a high-polluting footprint.

From a socio-economic view, urban mobility in each of these three countries operates optimally; therefore, there is certainly no need to initiate adoption on a large scale (the technical gap has already been overcome). However, another point that is relevant for the Region as a whole and for the three countries individually, has also been integrated into this proposal: and that the Program seeks to solve through SA.

Finally, each country has its own challenges caused by each local context, indicated in Annexes A2/Pre-feasibility Study and that the Program seeks to solve through SA.


## Related projects/interventions

Multilateral Development Banks (MDBs) are promoting the promotion of e-mobility. CAF, AGROFUND, CGI, and KfW, all of which are included in the E-Motion Program, are important actors in e-mobility in various project countries. IDB also intends to present to the GCF a proposal for financing projects related to electric mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC). These MDBs will collectively have the potential to contribute to transformational change in the transportation system of the Region.

The Inter-American Development Bank (IDB) is also an active e-mobility player in various countries, e.g. financing a pilot project for the deployment of electric buses in Santiago de Chile; and supporting the development of a public transport network with electric buses for the Historic Center in Panama City (CA), which would be under the conventional scheme of owning and operating the vehicles. IDB is also working on a proposal to finance a program of investments focused on e-mobility for the Latin American region, which will have complementarities with the E-Motion Program. The two proposals are complementary because they both aim at promoting electric mobility in the transportation system of the Region. For instance, IDB will include different elements such as green hydrogen and maritime transport, urban mobility, and charging infrastructure. In addition to these projects, IDB has also been involved in providing technical assistance loans or sovereign-guaranteed loans (IDB) and its role to promote electrification mobility in sub-program 2 countries has been complemented by the provision of financial resources for the implementation of the program.

In accordance with the need of each country context, project activities through their respective technical groups (all parallel FA/and TA programs that may happen at the same time. We don't have further detail on IDBs activities for its respective projects).

As part of their energy and transport sector low-carbon recovery plans, countries are requesting more support from all multilateral agencies towards a paradigm shift to low emissions transport systems, with a boost to electrification and green hydrogen technologies. The GCF has been requested by several countries to provide support in promoting e-mobility in Latin America" in thirteen countries in the region. The Program focuses on enabling strategies and policies to promote electric mobility in these countries. Each country has been brought together so that a complementarity exists between their activities.

Baseline activities and project/programmes in the region and target countries implemented by leading implementing partners (IDB, CAF, KfW, CGI) and other actors are focused on the following areas:

* **GZ – Changing Transport™:** Supporting partners in defining low carbon transport strategies, implementing electric vehicles, reducing emissions, and harnessing sustainable urban mobility solutions.
* **E-Motion:** Implementing an Urban Electric Mobility System (UEMS) through the development of an E-Mobility Master Plan for Public Transport and Logistics and strengthening capacities for sustainable urban mobility planning and promoting electric urban mobility in Latin America and the Caribbean.
* **IDB – Accelerating HCC with Electric Vehicles:** Developing a Clean Bus Fleet Technology Consortium (CTC) to support the deployment of electric buses in LAC cities through Clean Bus Fleet Technology Consortia (CTCs).
* **UNEP – Global Programme to Support Countries with the Shift to Electric Mobility:** The aim of the Global Programme is to provide technical assistance and financial resources for countries in Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) to support low- and middle-income countries in overcoming these barriers through a combination of electric vehicle deployment, policy development, capacity building, and institutional strengthening. This will be done by raising campaigns, capacity building and the development of institutional structures to support the introduction of electric vehicles in the region. The Global Programme is based on scaling up for large-scale market introduction of electric mobility through 1) The development of policies, including fiscal, regulatory and fiscal measures; 2) The establishment of

adequate transport models and finance mechanisms; and 3) the development of plans and studies to ensure environmental sustainability. The investment component of the Country Child Projects will be used for electric mobility projects in the respective countries.

* **E-Mobility in Central America:** The UNDP Regional Office in Panama currently implements a GCF Readiness Programme on E-Mobility with the title "Advancing a regional approach to promote sustainable urban transport and energy efficiency through the deployment of electric vehicles (EVs) in Costa Rica, Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay. The project has a budget of USD 2,800,000 and aims at building capacity, developing enabling policies and creating an enabling environment for the deployment of EVs and supporting the transition to electric mobility in the region and will be implemented continuing a national with a regional approach, whereby close collaboration is established between the countries.

* **UNE-P "MOVE Electric Mobility in Latin America":** In 2016, UNDP and Mario Molina Centre Chile launched a project titled "UNE-P: MOVE Electric Mobility in Latin America" (UNE-P) which has three components:
    * (1) capacity building to public technical officers from almost 20 countries in the region through online webinars and face-to-face workshops.
    * (2) policy development, including the elaboration of National Electric Mobility Strategies in Chile, Argentina and Peru; and
    * (3) technical assistance, through the elaboration of National Electric Mobility Strategies in Colombia, Argentina and Peru.

The project will also facilitate the establishment of enabling conditions to transition to electric mobility – with a special focus on electric road mass transport. The E-Mobition Project will leverage on ongoing programs of GIZ and Euromobil specifically trough the following activities:

* **The NUMPI in Uruguay:** will set a long-term vision regarding sustainable mobility that will enable different sectors to engage. The NUMPI will generate capacity at the city level to develop mobility plans; those plans expect to be financed by the cities themselves, with the support of the national government and international partners. The project will also establish a working group that will set the agenda for fleet renewal as for energy production. Considering their almost 100% renewable electricity generation mix, Uruguay is well positioned to lead the region in terms of electric vehicle (EV) transition as to work on innovative multi actors networks to accelerate the transition and to outreach lessons learned.

* **The NUMPI in Paraguay:** will set a long-term vision regarding urban sustainable mobility by the development of the following components: (1) capacity building for public technical officers from almost 20 countries in the region through online webinars and face-to-face workshops; (2) policy development, including the elaboration of National Electric Mobility Strategies; (3) technical assistance, through the elaboration of National Electric Mobility Strategies; (4) generation ecosystem, the master plan and its MRV (monitoring, report and verification); capacity building a social approach to electric mobility; (5) urban sustainable mobility strategies; (6) feasibility projects; and (7) financial instruments that will be leveraged by E-mobility.

In addition, as GIZ is one of the G-mentor partners and therefore, the complementarity of these two programmes will be a key factor in the success of the project. The other two mentors are the World Bank (WB) and the case of the IDB, and another TA that can take place in the country. It is important to point out that E-mobility will make the connection between G-mentor and TA components.

The G-mentors are: GIZ, IDB, WB, MEF, STRI, Ministry of Energy, Ministry of Environment, Ministry of Transport (G-mentor), Ministry of Economy (G-mentor), Ministry of Education (G-mentor), Ministry of Health (G-mentor), and the Ministry of Tourism (G-mentor). The previous is because they are the ones who orchestrate the design of national strategies, policies and projects. They will be in charge of providing technical assistance to the project team with the technical teams mentioned, with which the TA proposal for the project was built.

The ongoing and existing programmes of GIZ and Euromobil reflect the current relevance of socially and economically sustainable transport to fossil fuel-based transport, as one of the key drivers associated for the on-going reliance on internal combustion engine vehicles. The two programmes are also in line with the fact that there is a wide variety of low-emission electric vehicles exist in many countries, particularly: (i) high upfront investments; (ii) lack of infrastructure to support EVs; and (iii) limited availability of charging infrastructure; (iv) limited offer of electric vehicles and electric vehicle supply equipment in low-middle income countries; (v) uncertainty regarding to end-of-life issues of used batteries.

For further information on the G-mentor, please visit: https://www.giz.de/en/programmes-and-initiatives/g-mentor.html. The investment partners will be key in the Program countries. Gap finance and financial incentives are still missing to a larger scale for electric vehicles and charging infrastructure. This is why the G-mentors will provide technical assistance, while providing business model support and complementary TA elements for commercial fleets and general enabling activities, which will help accelerating the transformation towards low-carbon mobility in the respective countries.

B.2 (4). Theory of change narrative and diagram (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagram)

The theory of change is described in the following; a detailed diagram for the E-Motion Sub-Program 2 is included in Annex B.

Climate Problem

The environmental problem of sharply increasing GHG transport emissions due to fossil-fuel-based vehicles (ICEs) is addressed by this Program through the promotion of low-emission transport modes and electrification. The current situation and business-as-usual projection will result in a non-alignment of climate targets. Fossil vehicles are in urban areas, where they cause significant health impacts on children, elderly people, women and men with disabilities, and women and vulnerable people. Electric mobility combined with low carbon electricity production can significantly de-escalate the problem.

Goal Statement

The Program aims to persuade people to use clean public transportation modes by enabling a large-scale region transformation in urban transport in Latin America, focusing on intense-use vehicles leading to reduced fossil fuel consumption, greenhouse gas emissions and air pollution. This will induce not only more demand into the existing public transport system but also a shift in motorization towards low-emissions options within the regular journey.

If countries succeed to kick-start the deployment of EVs and the shift to low-emission transportation modes, transport emissions can be achieved as overall emissions are reduced significantly. To achieve this transformation, transport ecosystem EV technology fueled by emission-free renewable energies is critical. Ambitious climate targets for 2030 require a significant reduction in transport emissions. This will only be possible if a large-scale deployment of low-emissions options within the regular journey.

The Program believes that to set the regulatory and political framework (including standards, regulations, subsidies and tax rules, and procurement guidelines) is enhanced for EV deployment, relevant public policies are needed. The Program will support countries in developing their national strategies, action plans prepared, financial and construction THEN the foundations for a mass deployment of e-mobility and ultra-low-emissions vehicles. This will be achieved by creating an enabling environment for the development of a sustainable, secure and affordable public transportation; hence, cities can influence users' mobility patterns and promote a mode shift towards low-emission transport modes. Because of the enabling and public transport ecosystems created, the mass deployment of e-buses and EVs is kick-started and innovative business models are promoted.

Key areas

The Program supports a paradigm change to low-emission transportation systems. The Program not only shall enhance the regulatory framework but also support countries in developing their national strategies on how cities can influence users' mobility patterns by setting the foundations of a low-emissions, reliable, efficient, secure and affordable e-transportation.

Accelerated EV investments take place due to an enabling conducive policy environment, innovative business models, and the availability of a sufficient number of charging points. For this purpose, the Program invests in the local banking sector to account for its risk and opportunity perception, and develop appropriate business models for financing EVs. The Program will also support countries in developing their national strategies on how cities can influence users' mobility patterns by setting the foundations of a low-emissions, reliable, efficient, secure and affordable e-transportation.

The country studies show that depending on respective scenarios the Sub-Program 2 will have significant spillover effects that lead to an uptake and acceleration of EVs in all major vehicle segments. The high-potential scenarios show that EVs will reach levels up to 100% of heavy-duty vehicles within cities by 2035.

# Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal V3.0 | Page 21

## Responsibility for responsibility is based on three pillars:

### 1. Market-based price decrease: EV prices are decreasing in all vehicle segments due to decreasing battery prices and increasing competition and supply of EVs. Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) parity with fossil vehicles is expected by 2035, which will make EVs more competitive.

### 2. Reduced risks and costs for 2nd- and 3rd-party buyers due to showcasing of successful implementation sub-
#### - Market-based price decrease: EV prices are decreasing in all vehicle segments due to decreasing battery prices and increasing competition and supply of EVs. Total Cost of Ownership (TCO) parity with fossil vehicles is expected by 2035, which will make EVs more competitive.
#### - Improved performance: Enhanced range, charging speed, and other performance capabilities.
#### - Reduced risk: Lower resale value risk due to improved performance and availability of spare parts in secondary spare parts markets. Reduced risk on the willingness of fleet operators to invest in EVs due to reduced costs and increased reliability.

### 3. Policies which are more conducive for e-mobility:
This allows EVs to be more competitive. Countries have different levels of readiness, but all can benefit from the Program by improving their conditions for the transport sector. The Program will improve the framework conditions for EVs through Technical Assistance (TA) and capacity building.

### Sustainability
A core element of the intervention strategy of the Program is the medium-term commercial viability of EVs. For each country, the market and commercial feasibility has been assessed based on expected future price developments. Only vehicle types and countries per area have been included where it is deemed as feasible that a significant number of vehicles will be sold in the medium term (5-10 years) at prices which match the EV evaluation.

## Country needs

Programmes at national regional and municipal levels of the sub-program 2 counties, the Technical Assistance at the national level will be differentiated according to the needs of the country. No program country, with the exception of Uruguay, has a specific TA programme for each county. The technical assistance (TA) programme is described in Annex 2 provides country reports including the specific scope of each country-specific TA component. Targeting the last mile and the first mile, the TA programme will support countries to implement their national strategies. Some wave-sub-projects might focus on secondary cities, according with the strategy of intervention of Uruguay, Paraguay, and Argentina. The TA programme is designed in a way that it can be easily adapted to other countries’ needs. The programme, e.g., regarding institutional capacities/capacity needs, technical know-how, business and operational know-how needed for the implementation of the wave-sub-programs.

The transport sector is transitioning to a sustainable low-emission system with a dominance of public transportation and non-motorized transportation (NMT). Multiple countries in the region are establishing Green Recovery Plans due to the COVID-19 pandemic. The transition towards sustainable low-emission systems will bring about significant economic, environmental and social benefits, short-term jobs and a long-term greening of the economy. Electrified and shared non-motorized transportation systems are thereby a core feature.

## Outcomes & Co-Benefits

### Long-term outcomes

- **GHG reduction (Long-term Outcome 1):** An increased share of low-emission transportation is reached by replacing fossil fuel-based vehicles. EVs have significantly lower lifecycle GHG emissions than fossil units in all countries, and the use of renewable energy sources for charging will further reduce these emissions.
- **Improved energy security (Long-term Outcome 2):** EVs use up to four times more efficient than fossil vehicles, which reduces the need for imported fuels. The transition towards sustainable low-emission systems will bring about significant economic benefits.
- **Improved energy diversity (Long-term Outcome 3):** EVs are up to four times more efficient than fossil vehicles, which reduces the need for imported fuels and increases energy security by diversifying energy sources.
- **Implementation of innovative business models facilitated (Long-term Outcome 4):** The Programme contributes to the implementation of innovative business models in public transportation systems, including among others, for paratransit. This also includes the support of public and private financing institutions including banks and financial institutions.

### Co-benefit 1: Improved air quality and health. EVs have no combustion process and therefore emit no PM2.5 and NOx emissions in cities. By replacing fossil fuel-powered conventional vehicles with EVs, the air quality is improved significantly, but improvements are also expected for public health and well-being due to the reduction of noise pollution.

### Co-benefit 2: Socio-economic benefits. Due to a higher energy efficiency and particularly due to the reduction of the lifecycle GHG emissions, the transition towards sustainable low-emission systems will contribute to economic growth. Moreover, the updated EV market and technology as a model for public transportation led to the creation of new jobs.

### Short-term outcomes

- **The co-benefits of this programme are elaborated in more detail in section D.3.**

## Program Activities / Inputs
The Sub-programme activities (see section B.2(b)) are aligned with financing needs targeting towards reversing the current trend of increasing transport emissions and towards low-emission electrified and sustainable transportation infrastructure development.

### Inputs 1: Loan de-risking of investments to enhance bankability and leverage resources in electric mobility and sustainable urban transport systems (under Component 3, 2.4) and concessional investment is a game-changer and also provides an opportunity for the private sector to invest in public transport. This includes electrification of urban public transport buses, public fleets and light commercial vehicles (LCVs) used in the urban context; Public fleet electrification can be achieved through a combination of incentives such as subsidies, grants, tax breaks, a modernisation and increased attractiveness of public transport. Public fleets and light commercial vehicles are better suited for electrification because they have lower operating costs than private vehicles, which makes them more attractive to investors. The electrification of public fleets also reduces the number of privately owned such vehicles and removes a core barrier to mass deployment of such vehicles.

### Inputs 2: Policy and regulatory framework development conducive environments on a local, national and regional level fostering innovative business models, policies which favour e-mobility, training and education programmes for drivers and passengers, as well as awareness campaigns. These are all aimed at increasing activities to increase awareness and information on e-mobility, and gender actions to reduce existing gender gaps with respect to access to public transport.

### Inputs 3: Grant-based technical assistance to source and structure investment sub-projects for the Program which will be used to finance the electrification of public fleets and light commercial vehicle deployment (Component 1.9)

## Barriers and Risks Addressed by the E-Mobility Program (Sub-Program 2)
### Financial barriers
A core barrier to EV deployment is the 2-3x higher CAPEX of EVs including installation costs, e.g., grid connections and bus depot upgrades. The higher CAPEX results in higher financing requirements and insurance costs and higher new exposure due to higher fleet levels. Higher CAPEX can be partially offset by lower maintenance and operational costs and a higher lifespan of EVs. However, the profitability of EVs is only in the long-term because initial investment costs are high compared with conventional vehicles. The payback period for an electric bus is longer than its diesel span, due also to having to invest in charging infrastructure. This makes it difficult for municipalities and other public transport operators to finance their own investments. Municipalities therefore prefer to invest in second-hand resources or a larger number of new fossil vehicles although electric units have potentially lower total costs of ownership (TCO).

|  | Electric bus | Diesel bus |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 2020 | €182,000 | €345,000 |
| 2020 | €67,000 | €90,000 |

This barrier can be overcome with alternative financial structuring and business models which allow financially stronger investors to invest in assets while transport companies (private or public) operate the system based on e.g. a leasing scheme.

## B

### Policy environment not conducive for mass EV deployment
The barrier is related to conductive policies. Initial steps have been taken by all countries and some have developed comprehensive EV targets and roadmaps. However, policy implementation has been slow in many cases due to lack of political will and resources. The current pace of policy development does not foster mass change. As example, targets on numbers of EVs on the road in 2030 are a visible signal. However, if there is no accompanying policy framework, it may be difficult for countries to achieve these targets.

# Lack of Infrastructure & Charging Amenities, incl. Battery Recycling & Disposal

The barrier is related to a lack of knowledge on how to optimize EV ecosystem design (identification of type of EVs to use, fast/levelopportunitiescharging etc.). Numerous studies have shown that the cost of ownership for EVs is lower than that of conventional vehicles in terms of fuel costs, maintenance and recycling and disposal. This barrier is overcome through regulatory support, capacity developing and training efforts as well as awareness material.

## Technical Barriers

Performance risks and related satisfaction of EVs: Refer primarily to higher than expected energy consumption (in range), high operating costs and repair costs. The higher risk of EVs compared to fossil vehicles as the technology performance level in the respective country is unknown, the long-term profitability of EVs depends on long-term cost reduction and increased efficiency. These are related to battery degradation, maintenance and operations. Energy costs in many countries are dependent on time of usage and are split in a consumption and demand charge. The higher energy consumption of EVs compared with fossil vehicles can be attributed to the lower range of EVs.

Performance risks are also related to vehicle power, usage pattern and battery life/health. This results in a (sometimes) shorter driving range than expected. These risks are not only related to the vehicle itself but may require experience of large-scale operations. The investment-subject projects will allow to gather real data on EV performance and charging infrastructure.

## Lack of Low-emission Infrastructure Investments for Enhancing Deployment of Mobility

One of the main barriers for EV deployment is a missing large charging infrastructure in the cities that generate traffic in these new technologies. This barrier can be overcome by providing incentives for private investors to invest in charging infrastructure, reducing prices (e.g., in Panama) from generally recognized brands, in that sense, promoting the dynamization of this sector. The government should also provide support to the companies in the countries with help with the shift to EVs, while the program finance large scale and well-organized fleets.

## Social Barriers

Displacement transportation uses often than those employment mobility. Travel patterns considered when designing buses, stops, charging facilities, routes, infrastructure for modal shifts, etc. respond to a male standard. Many studies have shown that women are more likely to use public transport as their main mode of travel and this is based: it mostly takes into account commuting purposes, and a stereotypical (male) worker as a model user. Affordability is also an issue in terms of the cost of charging infrastructure. Women face additional difficulties in their exclusion at a certain extent, e.g., a less efficient system. Women, who are responsible for household chores, have to balance between work and family life. This leads them to use public transport short walks with micro transit, to spend more time on the buses, at the stops, at the streets, to travel with children and other people. The lack of charging infrastructure is a barrier that affects women in particular. Women are based public forms of violence. If a paradigm shift ignores this information, it will not only be less efficient and transparent but also less inclusive.

These barriers can be overcome in the most efficient and effective manner by targeting high-usage vehicles and by focusing on cities as these allow for a high mitigation impact with a good value for money; is less complex due to low competition; and has a higher potential for social inclusion. The shift towards e-mobility and it is more equitable approach as the focus is on commercial, public and shared mobility (e.g., taxis, buses, etc.). This can be achieved by: (i) promoting the use of electric vehicles in the public and mobile package including taxicabs and buses; (ii) improving the policy framework for EVs; (iii) providing financial support for commercial EVs (Component 3); and charging infrastructure (Component 2). Financial support for commercial EVs (Component 3) and charging infrastructure (Component 2).

### Assumptions and Drivers

The main assumptions are that: (i) investments in EVs take place as planned, despite potential unpredictable impacts of the Covid-19 pandemic or any other drivers for economic crisis; (ii) the political framework in the respective country is supportive to the deployment of EVs; (iii) the public transport system in the respective programme implementation is as expected; (iv) fleet electrification and new operational models for public transport are implemented, including the introduction of charging infrastructure; (v) the government implements all necessary measures in place to enhance and make public transport more attractive, e.g. attract fares linked to new business models, such as pay-per-use or subscription-based services; (vi) the programme is able to provide a high level of enhanced service (incl. more lines, and interconnection etc.); beyond the programme scope, the interest of new investors is linked to electric mobility and new business models (hence the programme’s paradigm and potential use

unified: (v) EVs will facilitate learning leads to effective know-how transfer and spillovers, (vi) EVs have lower emissions than fossil fuels (i.e., electric energy systems can cope with growing EV charging demand with renewables), (vii) future electricity generation capacity will be sufficient to accommodate the growth in EV charging demand.

In addition, a key driver for the accelerated uptake of EV's will be spillover effects to other vehicle categories influenced by the project’s business models and financial schemes. This will be flanked by the strong political support and prioritization of electric mobility in the countries involved.

### Assumption VII is a fundamental assumption related to the mid- to long-term grid performance in the envisaged case of a large-scale roll-out of EV deployment and high penetration rates for buses, LCVs, and spillover effects to other vehicle categories. The study examines the potential impacts on the countries’ grid, charging infrastructure, and power generation capacity under high shares of e-mobility. Varying scenarios are considered in order to address the Program’s infrastructure interventions. However, in order to adapt to implications/results associated with deployment of EVs, it is necessary to consider the impact of other vehicle categories on the grid, charging infrastructure, and power generation capacity.

The studies aim at identifying required grid capacity in different parts of the countries, as well as analysing the impact of different scenarios on the grid.

# B.3. Project Programming & Intervention Strategy

The E-Motion Program and its Sub-Program in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama shall contribute to overcoming the barriers for EVs adoption by: (i) removing regulatory and policy barriers; (ii) removing technical and implementation-oriented activities such as targeted policy advice, business model development and capacity building to overcome market entry barriers; (iii) providing financial support to overcome liquidity barriers; (iv) removing feed-in tariffs. The establishment and operation of large-scale EV fleets works as proof-of-concept of the commercial viability of EVs in the respective countries. In addition, the E-Motion Program will contribute to overcoming the investment interventions combined with the implementation of business models appropriate for EVs (e.g., asset and charging infrastructure).

For each country, a market assessment (see for details section below) has been performed analyzing: (i) the current financial viability of commercial EVs in terms of total cost of ownership; profitability, payback period, cash-flow and debt service coverage ratio; (ii) the level of awareness among consumers and fleet operators about the technical feasibility barriers; (iii) market readiness of EVs in the specific country based on energy and vehicle prices and future expected EV technology development; (iv) the availability of public charging infrastructure and the willingness to install more public charging points. The E-Motion Program will provide financial support for the establishment and operation of fleets of EVs without further financial assistance. Therefore, interventions in each country are targeted to vehicle segments with a high potential for market penetration and/or where there is a need to overcome specific barriers such as electricity mix to ensure environmental integrity; (i) limited financial support can make a decision change and (ii) investments will be made in the medium term (by the end of the Program) for a sustained uptake of EVs based on commercial conditions.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 29

The feasibility study in Annex Z provides market assessments for buses, public fleets and LCVs in all Sub-Program 2 countries (Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama). The proposed intervention instruments are thus targeted to the specific needs of these markets. In addition, the analysis has identified that there is a need for additional technical assistance at the end of the Program. Also, the needs for technical assistance on a national and local level have been identified through the stakeholder consultations. This has resulted in interventions not being suggested for all countries in all vehicle segments.

The Program is for commercial EVs and charging infrastructure. Private passenger cars and motorcycles are not included as they are considered to be part of the broader transport sector, which is outside the scope of this Program. The potential for electric buses is high due to their lower operating costs compared with diesel buses, higher ride quality and have significantly lower GHG abatement costs than private vehicles. With a small number of vehicles and a limited charging infrastructure, the current market situation for EVs in Sub-Program 2 countries is not socially inequitable and could result in a mode shift from public to private transportation. Commercial EVs targeted are buses, urban vans and light commercial vehicles.

The potential segments for investment sub-programs in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama that have been identified (see also Annex Z) are:

- **e-buses**: Public/private bus operators with different legal regimes, which could operate 500 e-buses. In business model [1] (direct purchase), the operators would own/lease the fleet (i.e., leave from a special-purpose vehicle).

The Program only includes 100% BEVs, hybrid electric vehicles (HEV) and plug-in hybrid electric vehicles (PHEV) are not included. HEVs and PHEVs were an intermediate technology as long as BEVs were not yet technologically mature and cost-effective. The current market situation for EVs in Sub-Program 2 countries is not socially inequitable, but the environmental impact of around 25% emission reductions. A review of over 100,000 plug-in hybrid buses deployed in China shows that PHEVs are not commercially attractive due to their high cost and low range (see Annex Z). The costs for energy and GHG savings (AEB), 2018). Exceptional cases where such buses can make sense are in cities or cities with very high congestion. In these cases, the benefits of reduced emissions and noise pollution may outweigh the costs.

PHEVs are not a commercially attractive option. The battery size of PHEVs is small resulting in an electric driving range of only 30-50 km. This means that the vehicle will be driven as a conventional diesel bus for most of its life, using up their already small batteries. A recent study of ICCT revealed that commercial PHEVs only run 2% of real-world driving on electricity and the rest is driven with diesel or gasoline. The battery size of PHEVs is too small to replace combustion powertrain. The limited operational savings are insufficient to recover incremental investments of PHEVs compared to BEVs. In addition, the battery life of PHEVs is shorter than that of BEVs due to their higher energy intensity cycle base 3/4 more electricity than BEVs due to highly energy intensive hydrogen production. They therefore result in a lower total cost of ownership (TCO) for BEVs.

The current market situation for EVs in Sub-Program 2 countries is not socially inequitable, but the environmental impact of around 25% emission reductions. A review of over 100,000 plug-in hybrid buses deployed in China shows that PHEVs are not commercially attractive due to their high cost and low range (see Annex Z). The costs for energy and GHG savings (AEB), 2018).

Market Assessment

A market viability assessment has been made for each of the three vehicle segments (urban buses, public fleets and LCVs) based on local prices, costs and existing regulations (for all 60-Motion countries). This reflects the current market situation for EVs which is then expanded, based on projected future selling prices of EVs until 2030. The main assumptions used in this assessment are:

- **The primary target segment** is primarily TCOs, the TCR compared to the weighted average capital cost (WACC) of the non-electric fleet.
- **The secondary target segment** is primarily investment levels (see Annex Z). Whilst results differ between countries and vehicle segments core take-aways are:

[1] EV adoption does not promote earlier replacement or scrapping of vehicles. Scrapping programs in any case have limited effectiveness as they only apply to vehicles with low mileage, which are still used to replace their older rural or private transport buses with low mileage.

[2] There is no evidence that fuel-pumped buses and full electric buses were purchased for the BRT Proshwari.

[3] ICCT. 2020: Real-world Usage of Plug-in Hybrid Electric Vehicles.
