## B

### Concessional loans of GCF are expected to be 8.75% interest rate for public and 1.19% interest rate for private sector plus service and commitment fee when applicable. The loan tenure is for 15 years maximum.

The visibility of the Program is highly dependent on the accessibility of concessional funding. The absence of this financing will have a significant impact on the ability to scale up electric buses in public transport systems, as large-scale fleet deployment of electric buses as well as EVs is critical and there is sufficient international evidence to support that the cost of electric buses has reached parity with conventional diesel buses.

The Program will also seek to provide concessional funding for the purchase of large fleets of commercial EVs without financial support (see feasibility study in Annex 2). The following sections summarise the international experience.

## Table 4 Investment Grants for E-Bus Purchase

| Country | Incentives |
| --- | --- |
| China | Subsidies for initial cost, subsidies on charging and grid connection up to 100% subsidized, existing electric buses 3-7 years subsidized, decreasing rates since Program start in 2009. |
| India | 80% of the incremental cost of e-buses (Phase I) and 50% of the incremental cost of e-buses (Phase II) under FAME 1, 2015-2019 but see footnote below. Percentage points higher subsidies were for FAME-I. |
| Germany | Bus upgrades substituted plus coverage of 100% of costs for the establishment of new maintenance centres and charging infrastructure. |
| Switzerland | Subsidies for initial cost of e-buses, up to 50% of incremental cost of e-buses (Phase I) and 25% in Phase II. |
| UK | 75% of incremental bus and charging infrastructure costs subsidized under Phase I (since 2019); under Phase II, the subsidy is 30%. |
| Poland | 100% of incremental cost of e-buses was subsidized. |
| Colombia | Subsidies for initial cost of e-buses in Moderna and Phase I in Bogota; 160% of incremental total lifetime cost of a new bus (CAPEX only) subsidized in Phase II in Bogota (16%). |
| Chile | 100% of incremental cost of entire e-bus fleet for Phase I (200 buses) subsidized with monthly maintenance subsidies. Subsidies were reduced to 50% for Phase II (400 buses). The subsidy was further reduced to 30% and additional points in price. |
| Uruguay | Subsidy for a bus equals the government saving on subsidies saved on diesel fuel. |

### Sources

* * *

Countries consistently subsidized 80-100% of the initial e-bus system incremental cost for the initial phase. Countries decreased their subsidy rates significantly within few years (e.g., China, India, UK, Chile). The importance of upfront incentives is reflected in the fact that once a large fleet has been established, subsidies can be reduced gradually and even eliminated due to more competitive procurement processes.

* * *

In PRI China, the average subsidy level per 12m urban bus decreased within 5 years from 150,000 USD per bus to zero.
In India, under FAME I (2015-2019), e-buses were subsidized at 80% of incremental costs. This was reduced to 40% in FAME II (2017-2023) for a lower absolute value meaning absolute subsidies where reduced by more than 50% within 2 years.
In Chile, the subsidy level was reduced from 160% of initial cost and charging infrastructure costs and the 2nd series, since 2015. 95% of the incremental cost of a-buses (including charging infrastructures).

## Delivery Channels and new business models

Delivery channels will be established in each country in which CAF is active. In each country, it will be necessary to have the active involvement of the national technical teams that conduct electrification in each country of sub-program 2 (see abbreviation list):

- PA: CINE, SME, MiBentrettes
- CM: Energie, Electrification Table, Mades, ANDE
- UY: MiEM, GIEET, MiAmenos, MTOP, UTE

These teams must articulate with the other relevant entities identified by CAF to:

1. Generate the enabling conditions (this is a key element of the project) for the sustainable operation of the electrification sub-projects that will receive funds from financial assistance (FA). As it was mentioned in previous section, Figure 3 summarizes potential financing models and their respective benefits and drawbacks study; and all have been interviewed during the market study with high interest in E-motor value proposition.

2. Financial intermediaries for the purchase of e-LCVs (public and private) which are mostly owned by public entities and/or companies, but also include a number of reputable intermediaries. Some of CAF potential partners for the implementation of this component include in Panama: Banco Nacional, Caixa de Ahorros, Banco General, Banco Popular, Banco de Crédito y Ahorro, Banco de Desarrollo, Banco de la Nación, and other banks like Santicred and BANCR. It is also important to mention that CAF has, or has had, working relationships with some of these intermediaries.

CAF will enter into loan agreements or amend existing loan agreements with Financial Intermediaries to display an e-LCVs credit line. Financial Intermediaries loan agreements will contain Anti-Money Laundering (AML) and Counter-Terrorism Financing (CTF) clauses.

CAF will establish a methodology to follow appropriate commercial practices and procedures in all projects financed with GCF resources. In the provision of financing, CAF will perform a due diligence of the Financial Intermediaries and can require that they have an AML/CTF policy in place.

And CAF and GCF process will be lend to Financial Intermediaries, which will in turn on-lend these processes to public and private companies. CAF will contractually ensure that the financial conditions provided by GCF are transferred to the final beneficiaries.

CAF as per the loan agreement will have the ability to reject a sub-loan. The Financial Intermediaries screen potential loan recipients (i.e., the final beneficiaries) in accordance with the eligibility criteria presented below and their own internal credit policies. If CAF rejects a sub-loan, it will be returned to the Financial Intermediary. Moreover, it will be a requirement in the credit line that refinancing is not allowed. Financial Intermediaries will disburse funds only after receiving payment from the final beneficiaries for the purchase of an e-LCV and eligibility criteria. As part of the Program, CAF teams will provide technical assistance and capacity building to help Financial Intermediaries to implement the methodology described above. The Financial Intermediaries will be responsible for maintaining a database of all projects financed with GCF resources. It does not relieve them from the contractual obligations under the GCF financing agreement. In addition, CAF may monitor these projects explicitly.

The following are the benefits and contractual obligations that will be established for both: the Financial Intermediaries and the final beneficiaries:

1. The funds will be required to be deposited in a separate account of each financial intermediary, and after those resources will be used to finance e-LCVs.

The Sub-Program 2 envisages to finance at least one investment project in each participating country. In the case that between two or more countries, there is no need for an investment project in other countries under this same component. No more than 50% funding shall be shifted into a specific country. Investment projects will be selected on a "first come, first served" basis, depending on their level of maturity at the time of availability of GCF proceeds.

In addition, EVs are purchased instead of new conventional vehicles: i.e., if an old unit is at the end of its lifespan and not replaced by another vehicle, it will be scrapped. The only way to replace this vehicle with a new one is when a new electric vehicle is purchased. What happens with the old vehicle is the same in the case project or in the baseline scenario: it is scrapped and no replacement vehicle is added to the fleet of the municipality.

All emission impacts and financial calculations are also a comparison of a new fossil against a new electric unit. The Baseline Scenario assumes that the old bus will be replaced by another conventional diesel bus, with limited merits as for example in the case of buses old municipal buses could still be used to replace even older rural or urban buses. In the Project Scenario, the old bus is scrapped and replaced by an electric bus using the latest technology. Instead of 650 new diesel buses (BAU) the Program will support 650 new electric buses. What happens with the old vehicle is the same in the case project or in the baseline scenario: it is scrapped and no replacement vehicle is added to the fleet of the municipality.

The Program does not add vehicles. In absence of the Program, the exactly same amount of vehicles would be replaced by conventional diesel buses as under BAU.

The major innovative business models proposed for e-buses are structured around bulk purchasing and splitting asset ownership and operations. Unbundling ownership and operation can also include the provision of power, charges and bus maintenance. The leasing model is a key element in this approach. Leasing allows for a more flexible use of assets which can effectively reduce the burden of upfront costs. E-buses have lower TCOs and leasing models can bring significant cost savings to municipalities. However, the high capital costs associated with e-buses are a major barrier that can significantly reduce the profitability of an e-bus fleet over its lifetime.

The business model for e-buses is based on long-term lease agreements between the municipality and the lessor. The lease agreement covers all aspects of the bus operation including maintenance and fuel supply. The lease period ranges from 5 to 10 years, with a typical term being around 7-8 years. This allows municipalities to benefit from the high efficiency and low emissions of e-buses over their entire lifetime.

The previous business model won't directly be feasible for the rest of the transport service in Panama. ZEBRA project indicates that the adoption of electromobility in public transport buses requires that cities study their mechanisms to regulate and control the operation of these vehicles. In this sense, the strategy recommended is to start with small or medium concessions that involve 100% electric axes or not more than 50% diesel axes. The concessionaire should be responsible for all aspects related to the operation seamlessly in the transition process. In this way, the ecosystem begins to be stimulated; authorities assume the role of regulator and controller of the service, while the concessionaire assumes the role of operator of its business, access to concession fee. In Santiago province, UTRAPSA is moving on that direction with MEF which provides concessionary loans to UTRAPSA which in turn provides concessionary loans to ATTT. This model could be feasible for structuring a new concession in Santiago where UTRAPSA has a contract with ATTT and the latter has a contract with the private operator. The same model is being used by MEF in Paraguay with ticket collection and concession budget from ATTT. Emotion would provide concessionary loans to MEF which provides concession budget to ATTT. The offset agreement makes fee conditions to performance. Demand risk is retained by ATTT.

Also, the possibility of business models of direct purchase can be an option if some off-takers prefer to keep ownership like CUTAS may want. In this alternative, the current "feeders" model for BES subsidy would be the business model (see above). However, transition to introduce electric public transport systems needs to be started. In this sense, the government should provide a framework that allows the development of new business models and encourages operators to have high visibility, high demand and social support in order to ensure the conditions make the operation sustainable in the long term. The government should also provide financial incentives for operators to invest in electric buses as operations begin to organize themselves to remain in the system and in the business and access to concessional loans.

Chile and Colombia have been particularly successful in promoting e-buses. Why? What was the game-changer?

The case of Chile is especially interesting due to the introduction of new business models separating bus operation but bus ownership. The first batch of 200 buses were financed through investment by private operators. Critical for this first batch of electric buses versus fossil units as well as changes in concession contracts reducing risks for investors in vehicle assets and ensuring that they are not penalized if they invest in electric vehicles. This was a very important step to promote the transition from operations with bus suppliers and others being "risk takers" on their own operations with investment funds. Electric buses are no longer engaged by the government as a risk taker but rather as an investor in the business model of the operator. The government has also been involved in promoting the use of electric buses through public-private partnerships (PPPs) such as gmp.Capital and Nestlé Capital have invested an amount. The government to lower costs for incremental investment costs and provide financial incentives to operators to invest in electric buses. This is a very important step because it represents a significant milestone in the transition from fossil fuel vehicles to electric buses. Colombia has also been successful in promoting e-buses through public-private partnerships (PPPs). The government has provided financial incentives to private operators to invest in electric buses and provide them with concessional loans for the management of the EV public fleet program (EVs and charging infrastructure).

Colombia

Large Colombian cities already run successfully since many years BRT systems with private operation and a public system. The most common model is that the government provides the bus service, while the private operator provides the buses and maintenance services. This model has been successful in providing high-quality transport at low cost. E-buses are currently being considered for this type of system as well as other models such as: (i) a public-private partnership where the government provides the infrastructure and the private sector provides e-buses; A subsequent second tender which allowed for any technology resulted in all winning offers to be fossil fuel vehicles. This is an important point because it reveals that even when there are no financial incentives, private operators will not invest in electric buses unless there are significant additional payments per e-buses as well as longer concession periods. This made electric buses competitive with fossil units only if the government provides financial incentives for private operators to invest in them. The second tender reveals two important points: (i) e-buses can be inserted into public transport operations and will be offered by private operators; (ii) commercialization of e-buses is not yet possible, even when there are significant financial incentives. This means that incentives alone are commercially not yet competitive.

PANAMA – public fleets and a LCV model

Figure 12 shows the common mode proposed for fleets cases: electric fleets and e-LCVs in Panama. The most common model is that the government provides the fleet service, while private or more often private providers on a competitive tender (least monthly payment), request them to set up an SPV for this project. As a financial lease, the SPV and the fleet provider may have separate legal entities. The SPV will be responsible for purchasing the vehicles and leasing them back to the government. The government provides a loan through banks (public in preference to seek concessional conditions to final users) which have separate legal entities from the SPV. This is an important point because it reveals that even when there are no financial incentives, private operators will not invest in electric buses unless there are significant additional payments per e-buses as well as longer concession periods.

The same financial hurdles independent of the beneficiary are requested from the GCF. The same financial hurdles are faced if vehicles are operated and owned by a private or a public entity. Program finance will not result in competitive bidding between public and private operators. However, it is expected that the competition for open-access FIs and loans to private and public owned public transport operators are based on competitive bidding.

The program is structured in four components supported by overarching project management:

- **Component 1: Establishment of an e-mobility ecosystem (Technical Assistance)**
    - Component 1.1: Regional technical assistance
    - Component 1.2: National technical assistance
    - Component 1.3: Technical assistance for public transport (Financial Assistance)
- **Component 2: Deployment of public fleets and light commercial vehicles (Financial Assistance)**
- **Component 3: Fast-charging infrastructure (Financial Assistance)**
- **Program Management**

This component and associated activities provide technical assistance to enable effective financial assistance and to create a (gender-responsi) policy and business framework conducive for massive deployment of EVs. Activities are conducted in three phases, each with its own specific objectives.

To address the technical assistance needs of each country and of each project in particular, it is contemplated to work through technical assistance packages that will be approached from a regional, national and local perspective. A first part provides general guidance on how to set up an e-mobility ecosystem, including policy frameworks, regulatory and general technical assistance to create a community of practice in electromobility. A second package contemplates the specific needs for each country and project, taking into account the particularities of each case and country in particular attention. It will be oriented to the particular needs of strengthening the regulatory and normative framework and the development of an e-mobility ecosystem. The third package is a technical assistance package that addresses the three countries have particularly a comprehensive policy frameworks for promoting EV, which some of them already have a significant fleet of electric vehicles in operation. In addition, the TA will provide support to the deployment of Daimler's MO-TO's vehicle complement each country context in line with their existing policies and strategies. Roadmaps will be developed at national level and will be presented for discussion and validation during the workshop and will be discussed in the interview with UTE. Finally, a technical assistance package will be structured at local level and will focus on technical assistance to public transport operators.

The TA component lays the foundations for a long-term shift to low-emission transportation and the deployment of e-buses and commercial EVs and addresses several identified barriers, outlined in section B.2. Activities under this component are designed to address these barriers by providing policy guidance, regulatory support, technical assistance, and unfavorable business models and fiscal environment for EV deployment, by supporting alternative financial structuring.

Business model and medium-term policies which increase the financial attractiveness of EVs. Furthermore, TA addresses regulatory barriers, like an insufficient policy environment (including targets and roadmaps), which is not only a barrier to market entry but also hinders the development of charging infrastructure (installation, operation, and disposal). Also, TA helps overcoming the technical barrier of performance risks and related perception of EVs and their overall cost-effectiveness.

### Component 1.2.3 and 4 jointly address social barriers, particularly the disadvantage of women in current transportation patterns (see also section B.2.)

This technical assistance strategy according to demands of increasing specificity, will allow the sharing of successful experiences from other countries and regions with similar characteristics and needs. The TA will be developed and then applied according to particular local conditions. This also translates into a more efficient use of resources with respect to the development of an enabling environment for electric mobility in the region. In this way, the enabling conditions for the development of electromobility can be addressed without neglecting the accomplishment of the objective of the project. The design of a project requires to meet the first objective of the Program, which is the successful implementation of electromobility.

The establishment of an e-mobility conducive national ecosystem will comprise the sub-components 1.1a, 1.1b, 1.2 and 4.

### Outcome 1.1: The government enhanced coordination, planning and capacity for promoting electric mobility**

**Sub-Component 1.1a - Enhancing policies and strategies on electric mobility (PV and UPT): Establishment of coordinated national policy framework and strategy for the promotion of electric mobility in the region. This will be based on sectorial roadmaps (for instance, electrification of public transport, electrification of public fleets, electrification of urban bicycles, etc.). The establishment of a national policy framework and strategy is essential to ensure that all relevant stakeholders are involved in the process. The Program intends to establish in all countries where not yet available roadmaps for electrification of public transport and individual vehicles. ## Component 2: Deployment of e-bus fleets and enhancement of public transport

It is expected that this component will be applied in all invited countries. Component 2 addresses in Output 2.1 the bus fleet replacement process, which includes the procurement of new electric buses and the decommissioning of fossil fuel units (see section B.2). The goal is to deploy large-scale fleets of electric buses to showcase their technical and commercial viability as a sustainable alternative to diesel buses. Electric buses are more energy-efficient than diesel buses (slow-, fast-, opportunity- and ultra-fast-charged buses), have sizes of 12 m or larger, and different operational structures (mixed fleet, dedicated routes, etc.). The deployment will be done in phases, starting with the largest cities where the demand is highest. In smaller cities also smaller fleets can be financed as well. The Program will not finance pilot sub-projects. Buses are acquired through a combination of public-private partnerships and/or government procurement. New electric buses that are purchased instead of new conventional fossil fuel buses i.e., the old, replaced bus in the same case or baseline case or the new bus in the project case, are considered as e-buses. The Program will finance only those sub-projects where at least 10% of the fleet is e-bus. Buses that are acquired through a public-private partnership (e.g., under the Bus Rapid Transit project) except “green routes” based on IEEE Table 10 show indicative sub-projects to be financed. The Program will not finance sub-projects where less than 50% of the fleet is e-bus or where more than 20 buses are financed. E-mobility is combined with PT measures to foster mode shift in low-carbon transport corridors and to improve public transport accessibility, including through improved bus stop infrastructure, enhanced pedestrianization and public transport accessibility, transit-oriented development, fostering of electric micro-mobility and other complementary measures.

## Output 2.1: Finance of a-bus kick-off the mass deployment and provide evidence of technical, financial and environmental sustainability

Output 2.1 will finance sub-projects that are prepared, and e-bus fleets are deployed by bus operators. This includes the sub-activities project preparation, financing and deployment. The Component 2 Program envisages to finance the deployment of electric buses in a phased manner. In the baseline case, the old diesel bus is replaced with an electric connection and bus depot upgrades to accommodate e-buses. This needs to be designed individually per project as the technical requirements for the replacement will vary depending on the existing bus fleet (e.g., central station location, ultra-fast-charging) is dependent on route and operational characteristics as well as local costs.

Scraping Program: The scraping program is intended for the determination of emission reductions as in the baseline case the old bus is being replaced with a diesel unit and in the project case with an electric unit; i.e., in both cases an old diesel unit is either replaced by another diesel unit or by an electric unit. In the baseline case, the old diesel bus is replaced with the emissions of a new baseline bus with a new e-bus. Scraping Program in any case have limited benefits as all municipal buses are not necessarily replaced at once and there will be a mix of old and new buses on the road.
3. The average bus occupation rate is defined as the number of passengers per seat per hour, which can vary after some time and that average bus occupation rates are not negatively affected by countries which introduce new mass transport systems (e.g., BRT).

### Outcome 2.2: Ridership of public transport and buses is increased through smart infrastructure measures

Declining public transport shares are a fact in many countries. If the trend towards more private motorized transport (MT) continues, this will have negative consequences for cities, as it leads to high quality public and non-motorized transport, the trend of rapidly growing MT will unvietnably lead to cities sinking into traffic congestion, with all its associated costs and environmental impacts. The main reasons why people choose private motorized transport are the lack of attractiveness, connectivity, accessibility and convenience of public transport. The program will aim at increasing ridership by improving the quality of public transport services through a combination of measures integrated locking systems and bus only lanes thereby reducing travel time and making public transport faster then private motorized transport. These measures will be implemented in two phases (Component 1 and Component 2) based on the TA work under Component 1), which focus public transport and de-incentivize private means of transport.

The following table shows the indicative smart infrastructure projects to be financed by the program, which include measures such as bus route restructuring, exclusive bus lanes, integrated fare systems, or inter-modal integration. These projects will be implemented in two phases (Component 1 and Component 2) based on the TA work under Component 1), which focus public transport and de-incentivize private means of transport.

## Component 4: Fast-charging infrastructure

This component is focused on the establishment of fast-charging infrastructure. This infrastructure is also pre-condition for further promotion and spill-over to other vehicle segments. A fast-charging infrastructure is a key enabler for the deployment of electric vehicles in cities, as it allows drivers to make an immediate decision to make a charging system commercially viable and vehicle owners cannot purchasing an EV due absence of the charging infrastructure. The objective of this component is to support the development of a robust charging network enhancing deployment of e-mobility as a key technical barrier. The barrier will be addressed by Component 4 and work under this component will be carried out in parallel with other components. This component will be funded at 100% respectively. Against the background of common climate change impacts such as heavy rains, floods, and storms, the objective is to ensure that fast-charging infrastructure can withstand extreme weather conditions during operation. For instance, bus depots locations need to be resilient in terms of providing infrastructure to mitigate flooding.

In Panama, SINE will lead this component with support from cities/government/communities that have been part of the work within Emotion feasibility study: Panama City, Cotón, Heredia. In the case of Paraguay, private entities like Notargi, ENSA, Casa de las Baterías.

## B

### Outcome 4: Investment in low-emission (fast) charging infrastructure lays the foundation for the deployment of EVs in Paraguay

#### Output 4.1: Technical preparations for fast charging infrastructure investments finalized, incl engineering and procurement documentation, including tender documents, technical specifications, etc.

Output 4.2: Low-emission fast-charging infrastructure financed and construction of it in relevant municipalities completed

The Sub-Program 2 envisages to finance and construct up to 60 fast charging station networks at or below 3kms. The Sub-Program will provide loans to public entities/entities involved. Public entities will provide and dedicate corresponding public space and land for the installation of the infrastructure. The Sub-Program will also provide technical assistance in the design, planning, and construction. In addition, the interplay between the utilities and the electricity customers will be addressed (i.e., the connection of the charging stations to the grid and the payment of the charges). This is a joint responsibility for Component 1) The Sub-program and the AE, respectively, will be responsible for coordination among different stakeholders involved in the design, planning, construction, and operation of fast-charging infrastructure. It could be used for Component 3 and other vehicles like taxis or private vehicles (no bus fleets under Component 2).

### Program Management Unit (PMU)

The PMU is responsible for:

- The general coordination and management of the Program.
- Providing technical assistance for the Sub-program preparation.
- The preparation of the reporting and monitoring reports due to the GCF and the supervision of the evaluation process.
- The implementation and follow-up the E-Motion Sub-program communication plan;
- The implementation and follow-up the E-Motion Sub-program financial management system; and
- The implementation of activities as per Gender Action Plan

### Knowledge Management:

The Program will develop knowledge products and share experiences and cases through cross-learning among different entities. It will also leverage shared experiences and build a base for replication thereby accelerating EV uptake on a regional scale.

The Sub-Program 2 will not create own public-private partnerships (PPPs) but will support the PPPs already in place or to be created by GIZ or GEPA Uruguay under its project to support the Master Plan for Public Transport. An interchange of experience and lessons learned is foreseen between the Program and GIZ/GEPA Uruguay, particularly regarding the implementation of charging infrastructure on buses.

### In-kind contribution arrangements (max 1500 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagrams)

#### Partnership arrangement under the E-Motion Program:

An IFD Alliance: working together for a stronger impact for E-motion Program

The Program will be implemented by three IFD development banks: CAF, AFD group including PROPARCOF, KfW; and GIZ. The combination of resources from these entities will allow the Program to combine complementary and diverse range of assets of different entities, to maximize impacts and pave the way for a real partnership between public and private sectors in Paraguay.

Moreover, they have a very strong local presence in Latin American countries and experience working with political institutions and governments. The combination of resources from these entities will allow the Program to propose innovative financial instruments at a regional scale reaching a large number of municipalities. This is possible because each entity has its own specific mandate and expertise: CAF and AFD can reach the private sector through its subsidiary PROPARCOF, CAF and AFD can offer charging infrastructure cost structure has cost of charges (in this case DC 100 kV), cost of electric works (at the advertisement level), and cost of civil works (in the middle of urban areas); civil works (these consider that in historic and strategic points, civil works can be quite expensive).

Implementation Arrangement for Sub-program 2

With regards to the Funding Proposal led by CAF, E-Motion will be implemented jointly by the following two institutions:
- **CAF**, as executing entity for financial assistance targeting public and private sector project owners (Component 2,3,4), and to support the technical assistance dedicated to sub-projects proposed under Component 1.
- **GIZ**, as executing entity for technical assistance (outputs under all sub-component under Component 1).

The proposed implementation structure takes into account executing entities' expertise: technical assistance to public bodies and financing solutions and technical and public and private sector expertise for CAF. Both entities are part of the PMU.

CAF will be responsible for providing governance, coordination, and quality assurance in its policies, procedures, and Monitoring and Evaluation. Moreover, CAF project teams will be responsible for the feasibility appraisal, implementation and evaluation of eligible investments of the E-Motion Sub-program 2. The sub-projects proposed under Component 1 will be subject to a detailed technical assessment by GIZ, including feasibility appraisals on each project in accordance with CAF standards and procedures.

GIZ will be responsible for the implementation of activities within the technical assistance Component 1. GIZ will ensure that all TA activities are carried out in close collaboration with respective public authorities as well as independent consultants. The TA activities will be addressed by national or international consultants. The estimate cost per country is US$20,000. In addition to the TA costs, there may also be other costs related to the contracting process. 1 or more consultancy bids may be considered per activity, and will be awarded to qualified firms on a competitive basis. GIZ will prepare a consolidated proposal for each country, which will be consolidated per country. GIZ will report to the E-Motion Steering Committee and to the CAF Programme Manager.

## B

### Contractual arrangements

- The GCF and CAF will enter into a Funded Activity Agreement (FAA) for the Program in the framework of the Arrangement for Climate Change Financing (ACCF). This FAA will be referred to as the Financial Agreement (the "Mandate") of the proposed CAF-GCF Program.
- CAF will sign an E-Motion with GIZ. The signed E-Motion, together with the Mandate, will be transferred to CAF according to the procedures set in the FAA. GCF funds will be held in a Trust Fund.
- CAF and GIZ will enter into an Agreement (the "Agreement") to channel GCF funds to GIZ for the purpose of Component 1 implementation: establishing the AFID, and AFID and GIZ sign a Partnership Agreement in order to agree on the common terms of the E-Motion Program.

### Service contracts between CAF and consultants

CAF will engage consulting services to implement the TA dedicated to the preparation of the sub-projects and to support the PMU activities. In order to implement the sub-projects' Preparation of E-Model, a call for tenders will be launched by CAF in accordance with the procedures set out in the Agreement. The selected consultant project implementation.

This will allow flexibility in the TA activities and speed in the mobilisation of consultants for the sub-projects.

### Finance

For the E-Motion Program 2, CAF will blend its finance with resources from GCF for the investment Component 2, 3 and 4. The financing amount will be defined based on the specific requirements of each project. CAF will finance sub-projects implementation in accordance with the terms agreed upon in the Agreement. The financing will be provided according to specific intermediaries. According to the market study (see Annex 2), the following institutions have shown their interest for the investment approach feasible.

#### Panama

Component 2: Ministerio de Economía y Finanzas (MEF). METRO (miles MUS$). Millus (operator). Banco Nacional del Crédito. Caja de Ahorro. Banco Central, Caja de Ahorro, Ministerio de Economía y Finanzas (MEF).

Component 3 and 4: Banco Central, Caja de Ahorro, Ministerio de Economía y Finanzas (MEF).

#### Paraguay

Component 2, 3 and 4: Ministerio de Hacienda (MH) and Banco Nacional de Fomento (BNF).

#### Uruguay

Component 2: Ministerio de Economía y Finanzas (MEF) and Banco Republica Uruguay (BROU). Component 3 and 4: Ministerio de Energía y Minas (MEM), Instituto Nacional de Electricidad (INE, national energy utility) and Administración Nacional de Usinas y Transmisiones Eléctricas (UTE, national energy utility) and Banco Republica Uruguay (BROU).

## Technical assistance component: a co-funding by the German Government (if up to EUR 1 M) is intended to be provided for each Sub-programme, if E-Motion and subject to a separate bilateral commercial arrangement between the GCF and the Federal Ministry for the Environment, Nature Protection and Nuclear Safety. The co-financing will enable GCF to finance the technical assistance component of the Sub-programmes.

CAF will enter into loan agreements or amend existing loan agreements with each of the Financial Intermediaries or individual projects participating in the program. It will be a requirement in the eligibility criteria and credit rules that repayment of the loans must be made from revenues generated by the project, including any income from sales and investments consistent with the Mandate of the program. CAF can per the loan agreement have the ability to reject a project if the revenue stream may be financed with GCF’s resources if it does not meet the conditions required by the GCF according to the FAA.

For the Program Management Unit, CAF and GCF will mobilize USD 0.5M (86.7% of the total cost of the PMU) as an advance payment for the first year of operation. Any PMU costs will be financed by the GCF in proportion to its financing of the program (13.22%, i.e., USD 302 M).

## Governance and Program management

- A Technical Assistance coordination committee will be constituted for the whole E-Motion Program.
- It will meet at least on a quarterly basis and be responsible for making the strategic decisions required for the execution of its program.
- The committee will consist of representatives by AFD and GCF.
- An investment committee to pre-validate the level Concessional GCF loans proposed to be mobilized on the Financial Intermediaries.
- A Project Preparation Committee to coordinate and validate the mobilization of the TA dedicated to sub-programmes.

CAF investment approval process entails two Investment and Credit Committees (CC). In each instance different aspects of the transaction are assessed: in the first Committee, main elements to consider are: Impact, 2. Assessment of financial viability, 3. Risk assessment, 4. Environmental and Social risk assessment. The second Committee takes places when the project due diligence has been completed, reports from advisors have been received and reviewed by the Investment Committee, and a final decision is made on whether to recommend to the President, Vice-President Corporate Programming, Vice-president-Social Sector, Vice-President Finance and Risk Management, and the CAF Board. The second Committee will be chaired by the President of the CAF, the Executive President, legal team, HR/HRIS. Projects need to be approved in both instances.

Considering the large geographical scope targeted by E-Motion and the diversity of financial and technical assistance tools required for each Sub-programme, a Program Management Unit (PMU) will be established for each Sub-programmes to ensure the coordination between the partners and a proper implementation of the program.

The PMU staff for the Sub-program 2 will be composed of professionals from CAF and GIZ that will be in charge of handling all aspects related with the Sub-programme. In addition, the mobilization of external consultants: an energy expert, an Environmental, Social and Gender Expert, a communication Expert and a Project Management Consultant (PM) will be carried out by CAF and supervised by the PMU staff.

The PMU will be in charge of:

- The overall coordination and management of the Program, which will include the animation of the governance bodies of the Sub-programmes 2, the technical coordination and animation, and the administration and financial follow-up.
- The management of the Technical Assistance for the sub-project preparation.
- The preparation of the reporting and monitoring reports due to the GCF and the supervision of the evaluation process.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 55

## Information on Project / Programme Sponsor (Accredited Entity)

CAF is a multilateral development bank created in 1970 and owns or co-invests 19 countries, of which 17 are in Latin America and the Caribbean and two in Africa. CAF finances projects that promote sustainable development through credit operations, non-reimbursable resources, and support in the technical and financial advisory services.

With headquarters in Caracas, Venezuela, CAF has offices in Buenos Aires, La Paz, Brasilia, Bogota, Quito, Madrid, Mexico D.F., Panama City, Asuncion, Lima, Montevideo and Port of Spain. CAF's mission is to provide sustainable development through credit operations, non-reimbursable resources, and support in the technical and financial advisory services, with high value added, to clients in the public and private sectors of the shareholder countries. CAF aims at being a trusted institution, close, responsive, sensitive to the needs, and supportive to a highly specialized staff.

## CAF's Actions

In 2018, CAF actions have been on supporting countries' efforts to improve their productive and social infrastructure, as well as investments in climate change adaptation. In 2018, CAF financed the construction, improvement, or rehabilitation of 1,236 km of roads; contributed to increased production capacity for 450,000 tons of soybeans; provided financing for 79,000 hectares of new sugarcane plantations and 22,000 hectares of coffee plantations; financed the construction or rehabilitation of 136 schools; built 80 new sewer connections and 52 km of pipelines for drinking water for nearly three million beneficiaries; rehabilitated or built 47 health centers; provided financing to 90,000 families in the agricultural sector; supported the reduction in carbon emissions. In the financial field, we also made great strides: CAF approved 133 operations with a combined amount of $25 billion, which is the highest level since 2008 and the second-highest in our history. This represents an increase of 46% over 2018. CAF actions continue to make a significant difference in the lives of people in Latin America and the Caribbean, who are increasingly aware of their rights and full of hopes for a better future.

CAF's credit ratings are among the best of Latin American banks: CAF has long-term ratings from A (AA) by Moody's Investors Service, S&P Global Ratings, and Asia Japan Credit Rating Agency, and A3 Moody's Investor Service. CAF partnerships with public and private sector investors have allowed it to play an active role in the promotion of sub-prime lending.

CAF's Green Agenda fosters the incorporation of development solutions based on nature, credit facilities for low-carbon growth resilient to climate change, and processes supporting virtuous cycles of green financing. Over 25% of CAF's projects are related to climate change adaptation and mitigation. In addition to its own commitments, CAF has made sustainable transport and climate change adaptation through disaster risk reduction and ecosystem services. CAF is also committed to the achievement of the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and the Paris Agreement commitments; CAF has an aspirational target to reach 40% of its portfolio for green finance. In November 2018, CAF approved a $50 million loan to support the development of electric vehicles in Chile. As part of its SDG 13 Mitigation plans of green bonds completed during 2018, in 2020, as experience of working directly with governments and private sector companies in the region has grown, CAF will issue a bond in Chile.

CAF is a Regional Direct Access accredited entity to the GCF.

## B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

The Program introduces a concessional financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of innovative public transport solutions in Latin America and the Caribbean. The program is based on the premise that electric vehicles dominate the market and the fleet renewal. In all involved countries no large fleets of commercial EVs operate. This is due to the lack of charging infrastructure, high costs of acquisition and maintenance, as well as changes in vehicle maintenance and operations and frequently also requires changes in the business model. Actors in the public sector are often reluctant to invest in electric vehicles because they are not sure about their performance; on the other hand are keen on promoting e-mobility as this can reduce emissions and dependence on (often imported) fossil fuels. The program aims at addressing these barriers by providing a concessional financing mechanism that will promote e-mobility coupled with stand-alone EV pilots. The missing link is however to kick-start EV massification with a first batch of at-scale implementation sub-projects. Such initiatives are dependent on concessional funding to make

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 56

## B

### Financial instruments from a risk perspective feasible. These sub-projects are the foundation for meaningful, practical, realistic and action-oriented roadmaps and for developing policies which are cost-effective and reduce barriers towards achieving the Paris Agreement goals.

The major financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries which present alternative financing options are (i) the low financial profitability of EV investments reflected in a low FIRR and very long payback periods; (ii) the high risk profile of EV investments, especially in the case of battery electric vehicles (BEVs); (iii) the lack of market liquidity for large-scale investments (batteries); and (iv) high capital and investor's capital demand resulting in high debt and risk exposure on the part of the government. The major financial barriers are also reflected in the fact that only a small number of investors with a higher risk appetite are potentially interested but are critical of the lack of profitability and/or the high-risk profile.

A key element to overcome these barriers is to provide an attractive investment framework for EVs. Battery electric utilities enter the market with a leasing scheme for buses thus creating a new business approach (see Box 1). The government can play a role in this process by providing guarantees, as well as through the procurement process when the government recognized and paid for the incremental investment cost of a bus plus guaranteed performance. If this had been done, i.e., without concessional finance and tax mitigation incentives the deal would not have taken place.

The main barriers for investors, utilities or PIs to purchase e-buses are the lack of profitability as a result of regulated transport market capture by fossil fuel vehicles (FFVs) and high costs associated with battery electric vehicles (BEVs) in higher financial costs. Hereby mainly three developments are decisive:

(i) EV market prices decrease: EV prices are decreasing in all vehicle segments due to decreasing material prices, production scale-up and economies of scale. The price of the total cost of ownership (TCO) per km for fossil vehicles is expected to be targeted in the mid-term categories between 2025 and 2030. Price tags of EVs will get close to fossil models by 2030. TCO per km for BEVs is expected to be lower than that of FFVs from 2025 onwards.

(ii) Reduced risk profile: The risk profile of investment in the first generation sub-projects has been significantly reduced and the risks are still perceived to be higher. However, decreasing vehicle costs and increasing battery performance will reduce the risk profile for the second and third generation sub-projects in the mid-term.

(iii) Reduced risk profile: For 2nd and 3rd generation sub-projects due to showcasing of successful implementation sub-projects and innovative business models. Risks which are reduced by large fleet investments, such as (1) risks related to the performance of the technology; (2) risks related to the market assessment on risk assessing on the sale-value of used EVs important for introducing business models based on leasing; (3) less known battery technologies and their reliability; (4) limited experience with electric vehicles in the public markets parts, (5) reduced risk on the workability of new business models in practice. The Program assists in this process by providing guarantees and tax mitigation incentives to overcome these risks. In addition, the government will play a large role which it could reduce its risks.

(iv) Reduced risk profile: The introduction of stricter emission restrictions and restrict fossil vehicle usage. This allows EVs to be more competitive. Lower costs, reduced risks, improved frameworks conditions all create favorable conditions for investors to enter the market. The Program assists in this process by providing guarantees and tax mitigation incentives based on commercial terms in 5-10 years if the country has gained experience with initial fleet deliveries.

Traditional commercial financial institutions are also hesitant to enter the market due to the novelty of the technology. A pilot project will not dispel their concerns as these are associated basically to the lifespan of the technology including battery life, performance and reliability, limited experience in public markets, lack of familiarity among vehicle manufacturers which are not all well known in the markets), resale value of vehicles, and potential savings in terms of operating costs. The Program assists in this process by providing guarantees and tax mitigation incentives.

The government can play a role in this process by providing guarantees, as well as through the procurement process when the government recognized and paid for the incremental investment cost of a bus plus guaranteed performance. If this had been done, i.e., without concessional finance and tax mitigation incentives the deal would not have taken place.

Finally, also public resources for financing the envisaged activities are not sufficiently available in Panama. Paraguay, and other countries in the region. The government's net lending/discount rate is 10% (2019) and 8% (2020), and it is currently at about 60% of GDP. In 2020 the government's net lending/discount was at 4% of GDP. The most important reason for this high discount rate is the economic crisis in Panama, which has been caused by a drop in international trade, transportation, and finance, while normal health and social spending increased largely to cope with the crisis. Panama asked for a precautionary credit line of USD 2.7 billion from IMF as an insurance

FA considers that the Program should focus on scaling up electric vehicles (EVs) and charging infrastructure, with an emphasis on developing climate investments in a new technology and business models. any new technology cost will lower and stabilize long term, especially because the entry costs of the technology are high due to the installation of new infrastructure which will not be as representative on post-investment costs. At the end of the program, the electromobility ecosystem will be strengthened and the ecosystem will be more consolidated. The E-mobility strategy will contribute to the transition towards a low-carbon economy by contributing to renewable energy, climate change and reduced CO2 contributions from transport sector (figure 1). E-motion is a broad concept that includes all electric vehicles (EVs) and hybrid vehicles (HEVs), including light-duty vehicles and intensive vehicle segments. Moreover, E-motion will contribute to develop innovative business models to bring new technologies to the market in an efficient way, by promoting the use of public transport and active modes of transport. According to the sustainable urban mobility pyramid, after active modes such as walking and biking, cities need to provide a good quality public transport system that is affordable, reliable, safe, accessible, comfortable, efficient, promote equity, increase modal shift among other social benefits. Beside this, new climate change requires the transition towards low-carbon economy by contributing to renewable energy, climate change and reduced CO2 contributions from transport sector and in public/freelistic sector; and UY has launched in 2021 and early 2022 its National Policy of Sustainable Mobility. The E-motion project will contribute to this policy by promoting the use of electric vehicles.

As final exit strategy key point, from local banks long term support in EVs, Emotion will contribute to them it giving better understanding in the technology from a technical, economic and practical point of view, with real sub-projects developed and implemented. The project will also provide a good understanding on how to finance new technologies, autonomously and in line with the energy transition policies of each country and also. This is an important point for banks because they are not familiar with this technology yet and therefore local banks consider it to be a high-risk sub-project when it comes to generating financial support investment. The project will provide them with a good understanding on how to finance new technologies, autonomously and therefore Emotion has good reception as it will help them to accelerate these processes that they must develop around the VEs.

### C.3 Capacity building and technology development/transfer (max. 250 words, approximately 0.5 page)

#### C.3.1 Does GCF funding finance capacity building activities?
- Yes
- No CI

#### C.3.2 Does GCF funding finance technology development/transfer?
- Yes
- No CI

### Capacity Building and Development

Capacity Building and development is delivered under Component 1. This includes specifically:

- Project specific training during design and planning stage specifically the transfer of skills for optimal technology choice, including technical and financial aspects such as procurement, project management, business models, financing plans, risk sharing mechanisms.
- Training on the implementation of the proposed project on knowledge in operations and technology related issues such as maintenance of EVs and charging infrastructure, ecological driving skills with EVs, optimal charging strategies, battery management and disposal policies and regulations etc. As outlined in Output 1.1.2 the training measures will be provided to all relevant stakeholders including private sector representatives, public servants, municipalities and public and private decision-makers on sustainable transport and e-mobility policies, business models, financing instruments, technology options, charging infrastructure design, regional trainings activities, incl. design, operation & maintenance of e-fleets as well as on business models and financial instruments. Also, training will be provided to bus drivers, maintenance personnel, first response staff and other relevant stakeholders.
- Capacity building and policy advice specifically for the establishment of sub-sectoral roadmaps for electric mobility in the region and the development of national policies.
- Capacity building with knowledge products and their dissemination based on the best global practices and the exchange of experiences between all partners. This is realized through different products and channels to reach a wider audience in Latin America.
- Capacity building gender mainstreaming.

### Technology Transfer

Technology transfer is facilitated through identification and design of appropriate e-mobility technologies and sub-projects and their subsequent funding.

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the proposed project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Proposal.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 560 words, approximately 2 pages)

GCF has requested that data be made per vehicle category (buses and light commercial vehicles) and per country. Whilst baseline emissions per vehicle category are comparable for different countries (variations are due to the age of the fleet), the size of the fleet is not necessarily the same. Therefore, the number of buses and light emission factor per country influences the emissions of the EVs and therefore the emission reductions.

The GHG impact is determined based on a well-to-wheel (WTW) approach. This includes for EVs the carbon emissions from electricity generation, transmission, distribution, and consumption. The WTW approach also considers emission factors of the countries included in the Program electric vehicles targeted by the report reduce GHG emissions by 70-90% compared to fossil fuel vehicles. In addition, the WTW approach also considers that the reduction of diesel engine factors EVs can reduce GHG emissions on a well-to-wheel (cradle-to-grave) basis by 80–100% (50–90%) compared to fossil units.

## 5.2 Paradigm shift potential (max: 500 words; approximately 1 page)

### The project is expected to have a significant long-term impact through its outcomes on creating an EV eco-system, which results in investors purchasing EVs on a commercial basis (scalers). Barriers to the adoption of electric vehicles are being addressed by the project interventions including FA and A. Accelerated EV investments take place due to experiences made, business models established, and lessons learned from the project. The project is expected to have a significant long-term impact through the uptake of commercial EVs and the improved ecosystem for EVs resultant of the concrete interventions as well as the scaling up of the project. This will result in a reduction of GHG emissions per capita and per vehicle emission system with a dominance of public transport and NMT. Multiple countries in the region are aligning Government policies to support the adoption of electric vehicles, which is expected to have a long-term impact on infrastructure which creates economic, environmental and social benefits, short-term jobs and a long-term greening of the economy.

The potential for scaling up and replication is ensured at the national and the international level through capacity created in the EV eco-system, proven cases of successful business models, improved profitability and reduced barriers to adoption. The project interventions are expected to have a significant impact on the market as they are operational and not just pilot sub-projects; the technology is taken up quickly if the business environment is conducive. This will result in a reduction of GHG emissions per capita and per vehicle emission system with commercial viability of EVs. Performance risks are reduced greatly, and business models have been introduced which can be scaled up to other countries.

### As a second phase of the Program is not planned as we consider that after the successful implementation of the projects, EVs will be financially viable, and the electrification of buses and LCV will not need of further concessionality.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 66

### Potential for knowledge sharing and learning: The TA Component at national, regional, and local level includes capacity development, training and monitoring. Technical assistance is used to ensure delivery of adequate training throughout the project cycle. This will be achieved through a combination of on-site visits, workshops, seminars, building in optimal management of EV fleets. Training materials, reports and knowledge products elaborated under this activity will be shared with all stakeholders involved in the E-mobility sector, including those outside the country.

This will be realized at a national level thus removing one of the barriers to more widespread deployment of EVs in Paraguay. The TA Component will also provide technical assistance for capacity development and training not only with countries outside the Program. The Program will also develop professional knowledge products and share experiences through workshops, seminars, webinars, etc., which will be shared with all stakeholders involved in the E-mobility sector.

The TA Component will disseminate experiences and build a base for replication. The Program will use existing channels within CAF, GIZ and other partners to ensure that lessons learned are shared with other countries interested in replicating the E-mobility project. Lessons learned during the execution of the E-mobility, as well as taking as a basis all those lessons learned that have achieved successful results.

### Contribution to the creation of an enabling policies for EV deployment. Technical support will be provided to the relevant national and local authorities in order to set up a regulatory framework for the development of the e-mobility sector. The TA Component will provide technical assistance on the following areas:

- **Sector roadmaps:** One of the main instruments used by sectors are roadmaps for electrification of public transport and individual mobility. Roadmaps are essential tools that define the strategic direction, milestones, and timelines for achieving a specific goal. Sector roadmaps remain at a macro-level. Sectoral roadmaps combined with investment sub-project performs a base for more targeted investments in e-mobility projects. The TA Component will provide technical assistance to develop sector roadmaps and specific roadmaps are a tool to steer climate investments and initiatives beyond the Program's scope and will help build capacity of relevant authorities to develop sector roadmaps.

- **E-mobility ecosystem:** Another focus area of Component 1 aims at the establishment of an e-mobility collective local ecosystem focusing on the financial landscape. With sub-activities related to the local environment such as business model design and development, financing mechanisms, public-private partnerships (PPPs), etc., the TA Component will provide technical support to car distributors and leasing companies contains for instance an analysis of national experiences, client needs and global best practices in the field of e-mobility. The TA Component will also provide technical assistance on existing financial products and risk mitigation products, such as special transport loans for e-vehicles, presented to local banks and other financial institutions. The TA Component will also provide technical support to prepare a plan that will prepare the local private sector, including the local financial institutions, for the mid-term establishment and continuation of an e-mobility ecosystem.

### Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies: The TA Component includes activities such as roadmaps (see above) which outline an agreed-upon set of pragmatic and realistic measures to achieve EV sectoral targets. These include but are not limited to: vehicle acquisition, charging infrastructure development (costs, efficiencies), battery management and disposal regulations, charging infrastructure standards, pricing policies for EV use (e.g., parking fees, tolls, etc.), vehicle registration requirements, vehicle insurance requirements (e.g., vehicle turnover guarantees), separation of vehicle ownership and operations vehicles, tariff structure etc. Technical assistance will be provided to the relevant national authorities in order to set up a regulatory framework.

### Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans: The programs will lead to significant GHG reductions in the transportation sector through electrification of public transport and individual mobility, as well as by promoting e-mobility and kick-starting EV deployment. This is consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans. However, it should be noted that this proposal does not specify any on contributions but guarantees compliance with the national plans. For instance, the charging infrastructure will be deployed in a climate-resilient way, taking into account future climate hazards such as heavy rains, floods, and storms to ensure a stable and long-term operation.

#### D.3. Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

**Sustainable Development Goal alignment**

The Program has as main target to reduce GHG emissions. It contributes significantly to sustainable development by addressing SDG goal 7 ("affordable and clean energy"), SDG goal 9 ("industry innovation and infrastructure"), SDG goal 11 ("sustainable cities and communities"), and SDG 13 ("climate action").

**Environmental co-benefits**

Main environmental co-benefits are reduced emissions of pollutants and reduced noise emissions. The major concern for air pollution in the cities is PM2.5, NOx, and CO2 emissions. The impact of the Program on these pollutants has been quantified using a bottom-up approach (i.e., based on the number of vehicles and their characteristics in each country of the country) using the EU COPERT model data i.e., this is a conservative approach as emission reductions are not based on specific measures but rather on the assumption that the fleet will be replaced by EVs. The same methodology used, the databases as well as calculations see Annex 23). **Social co-benefits including health impacts**

The major social co-benefit is improved air quality and reduced noise in urban areas. Air pollution is a major problem in many cities in the LAC region. Approximately 100 million people are exposed to poor air quality, exceeding WHO guidelines and affecting seriously human health. Latest data from the World Health Organization (WHO) shows that in 2015 almost 700 people died as a result of exposure to ambient PM2.5 pollution in Panama. Children and the elderly are disproportionately affected by air pollution as they tend to live and work close to its sources[3]. Children and the elderly are also more vulnerable because their lungs are still developing, and their immune systems are not fully developed.

For public transport vehicles, the main source of PM2.5 emissions is diesel particulate matter (DPM). The results reported in all Program countries shows that the targeted commercial vehicles contribute in general more to air pollution than other types of vehicles. Replacing such vehicles with EVs thus has a significant impact on quality and health of people.

The COVID-19 pandemic has created an unprecedented challenge for many Latin American countries, from the health sector to the economy. The Program contributes significantly to the recovery by addressing SDG goal 7 ("affordable energy transport, and urban landscapes while contributing to economic recovery. # GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 68

## Economic co-benefits

The economic cost of reduced air emissions (GHGs and air pollutants) has been calculated (see Annex 23 for the methodology, data, and results). The economic cost of pollution is calculated by assigning a monetary value to exposure to pollution and then multiplying this by the number of people exposed. This gives an estimate of health costs and caused by this type of pollution per country. This is based on the exposure of the population to air pollutants and the associated health impacts, which are estimated using the WHO guidelines for response functions to concentrations. The greater the risk of mortality or, more precisely, the cost of premature death is valued at a higher monetary value than other costs. The economic cost of pollution is calculated by assigning an externality cost expressed through the social cost of carbon (SCC). Latter is an estimate of the economic damages associated with emitting one tonne of CO2 into the atmosphere.

The SCC for 2015 was estimated to be US$36 per ton of CO2. The SCC for 2020, as reported by the IPCC's report on a unit value of 36 tons of CO2 in 2015 prices for 2016 emissions, is increased by 4% (IPCC, 2018). This results in around $40 per ton US$CO2 for 2020.

## EVs are more energy efficient than fossil vehicles. The direct energy savings resulting from the Programme estimated at approximately 35 GWh per year. This is equivalent to a reduction of 6,700 tonnes CO2e per year (based on GHG emissions factor of 198gCO2e/kWh).

The economic benefits directly from the investment sub-proposals due to reduced pollution are estimated at nearly US$3 million per year.

## Gender-sensitive development impact

Gender issues and development impacts will be described in the gender-disaggregated targets in sub-proposals' target. This includes (i) increased access for women to public transport; (ii) increased awareness about gender equality goals and opportunities in transport through capacity building and training of staff, including women, at the national level; and (iii) advocacy and workshops to disseminate best practices for reducing sexual harassment on public transport.

On a cross-sectoral basis, the GAP has as its target to integrate gender considerations into transport sector activities. This includes (i) increased access for women to public transport; (ii) increased awareness about gender equality goals and opportunities in transport through capacity building and training of staff, including women, at the national level; and (iii) advocacy and workshops to disseminate best practices for reducing sexual harassment on public transport.

The market study (Annex 21) identified the following barriers hindering a take-up of EVs in the countries involved:

- High investment costs. The initial key barrier limiting an uptake of e-vehicles represents the high initial costs, due to the fact that the price of electric vehicles is currently higher than the price of conventional vehicles in the national market (approximately one third higher for a comparable conventional vehicle (CERCE, 2019).
- Limited control of vehicles. There is limited control of vehicles that circulate, including old, second-hand and imported vehicles.
- Lack of credit facilities. Contrary to conventional vehicles, there are no credit facilities from banks available that allow the user to acquire loans to purchase alternative vehicles.

## Insufficient charging infrastructure: With respect to charging infrastructure, there exist several identified drawbacks, e.g.

- The availability of easy-to-install chargers in homes and/or workplaces or companies, a negative perception of the quality of electric power service by residential and commercial consumers for charging their vehicles, and the lack of public awareness regarding the benefits of electric vehicles.
In addition, technical assistance is requested for technical planning and defining business models.

## Lack of qualified labour and technical expertise: Due to the scarce market for electric vehicles and the difficult access to training programs in this field, there are few professionals with the required skills and experience regarding electric mobility.

- Specialized public transport subsidies: Public transport subsidizes specifically aim at covering the costs of fossil fuel for private passenger buses.
- Strengthening the bus system: The bus system needs to be strengthened and modernized. Hereby it is necessary to include complementary measures so that e-buses operate efficiently such as restricting private vehicles in urban areas, improving public transport infrastructure, and increasing the number of charging stations.

## Lack of public awareness: Citizens often do not have an approach to new technology and its benefits, thus there are few people who are aware of electric mobility.
CAFs have also realized market costing studies on the different vehicle segments for different price ranges and countries (see Annex 2). The commercial attractiveness of EVs versus fossil vehicles has been assessed based on parameters such as purchase cost, operating costs, maintenance costs, and other factors. These results can be used as a base to structure the financial instruments required to resolve the barriers to commercial viability of EVs in the selected segments.

Contrary to renewable energy generation that is increasingly cost competitive, EVs are not yet commercially viable and remain largely underfunded. The market and benchmark studies show that, if unaddressed, commercial EV deployment will be delayed by a developed market, i.e., until the transition barriers limit the carbon transport systems in the Program countries.

The EV sectors targeted have (1) limited access to commercial financing; (2) early-mobility costs and discounts available only for public buses; (3) high upfront costs and discounts available only for private vehicles at low risk levels. Therefore, financiers are not in a position to fund such activities with commercial loans at the current stage. However, the CAFs have been able to provide financing through its concessional lending instruments, including framework incentives and regulations with the TA component. This TA component makes very attractive to national banks to finance EV projects. The TA component is also important for the success of the Green Climate Fund Program and involved countries also depend on technical assistance that serves to enhance the viability of the subsidy programs. In addition, the TA component has been used as a tool to support the development of the required public funds by its own, due to current fiscal situation in the countries (e.g., economic situation coping with the COVID-19 pandemic), and also to provide technical assistance for the implementation of the activities requested by the authorities. These included historical cuts to monetary policy rates to help support economic activity and a sharp increase in government spending on social programs, as well as the introduction of new fiscal measures in response to the request of the TA activities.

Governments and transport operators also lack capacity and know-how on the appropriate technology choices and the most suitable business models and policies and business models to foster in a cost-effective manner EV deployment. This is clearly evident from public statements in favour of electric mobility which are however not followed up with concrete actions. The countries have been able to overcome this barrier by providing technical assistance, but they were sufficiently available in Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay due to the countries debt position. ## D.5 Country ownership (max. 508 words, approximately 1 page)

Section B1 already includes a short overview of countries including the co-fund emission factor, population and vehicle data. The following section provides an overview of the main policy focus on the climate policies of the involved countries (see also Figure 1 presented in b1). All NDAs of the included countries are committed to the deployment of EtsV. In addition, all countries have a clear vision for the future of their transport sector and mobility and all of them, or direct professional on behalf, were interviewed at least 2 times by 2 weeks in the Emotion Focus Group (EFG) and/or the Stakeholder Workshop (SW).

In the framework of the elaboration of the 4-Mission Concept Note until today, CAF and its partners (AFD, PROPARCO, KfW, GE) have held meetings with the NDAs of the countries concerned by the Program and key actors of the transport sector. The interviews were conducted in person or remotely and involved representatives from public bodies, banks, private banks, municipalities and regional states, power utilities, transport operators etc.

### Panama

According to Panama's NDC, the country's strategy is focused on mitigating particularly the energy and land use change sectors, through the implementation of measures to increase the use of renewable energies such as solar and wind. The National Energy Plan 2015-2030 (PEN) sets out a series of strategies for the period up to 2030, strategies like rational and efficient use of energy, electric mobility and distributed generation strategy are proposed.

Regarding public transportation, Panama has already started its urbanization process through the expansion of the Panama Metro network. The introduction of vehicles that will be electric in the future is part of this plan. In addition to the above-mentioned proposals, as well as guaranteeing the integration of the public transport system to encourage their use and promote walking and cycling.

Among the most relevant national policies are the following:

* **Panama's National Energy Plan (PEN) 2015-2030:** It sets out a series of strategies for reducing emissions from the energy sector by 2030, and in 2050 with transportation accounting for about 50% of the sector’s emission.
* **Panama's National Electric Mobility Strategy (SEM) 2018-2030:** It sets out a series of strategies to promote electric vehicles. In 2030, 45% of private vehicle sales will be electric vehicles, 15-35% of the buses in the authorized concession fleets will be electric.
* **National Energy Plan 2015-2030:** It promotes higher share of non-conventional renewable energy (39%) towards 2030 and 64% by 2050.
* **Energy Transition Agenda 2020-2030 proposals, among others, to decarbonize sector through electrification.**
* **National Electrification Strategy sets different goals, e.g., buses will be electric between 15% to 35% of their total fleet by 2030 and 45% in 2050.**

**"Plan integral de movilidad urbana sustentable para el área metropolitana de Panamá" (PIMU): Promotes, among other things, the electrification of public transport vehicles.**
* **Anteproyecto de Ley 134 (Draft Law 134), incentives to electric mobility in on mixed transportation for PANAMA.**
