## Background

With support from CAF, Panama submitted a readiness proposal to GCF in 2017, including establishing its GCF core components and the National Designated Authority (NDA). The NDCs were developed under the Paris Agreement and adaptation (1). The country's National Designated Authority is the Ministry of Environment.

National Development Strategy (NDS) 2035: Panama has a clear vision for sustainable development. Some banks have been interviewed in Panama and they are very interested in participating, at least in the ebus component. Capa de Ahorro Panameño (CAPA) is also interested in developing special EB products with BES. Banco General also was interested in developing special EB financial products within E-movement framework.

## Paraguay

As mentioned in section B1: Paraguay & NDC, based on the National Development Plan 2030, targets to achieve GHG emissions reductions and aims at reducing fossil fuel consumption by 20% (2030). The NDC is currently being updated. According to the Ministry of Environment, Energy and Water Resources (MEEyR) and the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MAIDE), the mitigation goals would remain identical to the NDCs as defined above.

The legal framework in Paraguay is based on the National Climate Change Law, which establishes the National Council for Climate Change (CNCC) as the main body responsible for coordinating climate change policies. Also, Paraguay established a National Climate Change Commission (CNCC), the National Directorate of Climate Change (DNC), and the National Energy Policy (PEN). The most important documents related to the national strategy on climate change in Paraguay are the National Development Plan 2030 (updated in 2025), which encourages the use of electric vehicles, among other actions. The NDCs include a target for reducing fossil fuel consumption by 20% and includes encouraging the transformation of public transport powered by fossil fuel to an electrical system and/or biofuels.

Paraguay's NDC targets 10-20% emission reductions compared to the baseline scenario, including reduce 20% of fossil fuel consumption by 2030. NDCs were updated in March 2021, transport sector would increase electric mobility.

The National Energy Policy "Paraguay 2040", which includes, among other action plans, the Electric Mobility Action Plan (PME), was approved on June 28, 2019 by the Executive Power. The PME has great potential in public fleets as this country has set targets (National Energy Policy) to ANDE and to public fleets. Ande is a company that provides electric vehicles for public services. The target is to have at least 50% of renewal with EVs and ANDE 100%. ANDE owns 1300 vehicles.

The PME has been structured around five fundamental axes under a common vision of promoting the adoption of electric vehicles and 21 strategic lines that will allow the achievement of the objectives set out in the NDC. The Guide for the Standardization of Electric Mobility in Paraguay, published in 2020, is intended to provide the necessary technical support.

In the framework of "Aurora Green City of the Americas", Municipality of Asunción they want to implement measures that will allow them to reduce their carbon footprint and promote electric vehicles. The city operates with EVs its municipal fleet.

Law 1840/2019: Prohibits the importation of gasoline, diesel, and hybrid vehicles from the payment of import customs tax and VAT.
Decree for the creation of a Strategic Roadblock for Electric Mobility. Creation of a Strategic Roadblock for electric mobility in the city of Asunción. The roadblock will be located at the entrance to the city.

Grants Route 10 Ciudad del Este, construction and implementation of the green route to connect Asunción and Ciudad del Este.
Municipal Ordinance No. 2523: Refers to the creation of a roadblock for Electric Mobility. Regulate the change of license in public spaces in the city of Asunción.

ANDE will publish a list of incentives for electric cost to different electromobility applications and user (end of 2021).

## Uruguay

As expressed in B1 Uruguay’s NDC target certain goals towards 2025 for 9 motion categories, also, Uruguay aims at establishing a National Electric Vehicle Policy through the main axes across Uruguay, of which 52% have been completed in 2020 and a fast-charging network, which has not progressed yet. Most relevant policies for the country are:

- In its NDC, Uruguay’s specifically target to attain 110 units of Buses and 900 units E-LCVs, conditional to additional means of implementation.
- The National Electric Vehicle Policy promotes several initiatives to mitigate transport sector GHG emissions, among others, the increase share of electric vehicles, hybrids and other, in the different transport subsectors.
- The policy is aligned with the country’s NDC, as well as with the National Environmental Plan for Sustainable Development. The plan promises a transition towards transport technologies with better energy efficiency, lower emissions and lower environmental impact, including the promotion of public transportation systems, aimed to improve public fleets in their renewal process and the development of a scrapping program to remove older vehicles.
- The Energy Policy and the approved Energy Efficiency Law establishes the need to reduce fossil fuel consumption for transport.

Decree 2406/12 modifies the specific internal tax for hybrid and electric vehicles. Decree 2587/13 introduces the specific internal tax for electric vehicles. Decree 2190/13 also exempts lithium batteries for vehicle use and charging systems for electric vehicles.

Since 2014, all new passenger cars are required to be equipped with COMAP® technology. From 2018, electric passenger cars are included in COMAP benefits (before that, only LCV were included). The government has also introduced the Energy Efficiency Certificates (CEE), Monetary income to energy efficiency investments (in buses and the facilities for charging stations) and a tax credit for the purchase of electric vehicles. In addition, there is a specific law on waste management: Waste handling and final disposal. Regulation for batteries, extending responsibility not only to end users but also to manufacturers and importers (in progress as at 2021).

- **Complementary measures**: The government has launched several complementary measures in progress as at 2021.
- **Insurance conditions**: Reduction on insurance fees of Banco de Seguros del Estado (BSE). 15% discount on electric vehicles and 30% for hybrid vehicles.
- **Technical Guide for Urban Electric Mobility Planning**: A guide for local governments that are interested to develop a plan for urban electric mobility. The document includes the main principles, objectives, methodology, tools and recommendations for an adequate promotion and implementation at a city level (in execution, delivered by February 2019).
- **Specific tariff for public charging networks**: Time-based electricity rates for charging vehicles in the public domain: USD 0.50/ kWh (AWAH), USD 0.51/kWh, and to USD 0.62/kWh. Additional discounts: i) All EVs: 50% for charges made at Valle time.

### National and private banks see electrification adoption receive incentives.

As part of market study, banks have been interviewed in Uruguay and they are very interested in participating as a financial existing entity. Banco de la República Argentina (BRA) has already expressed interest to participate in the Sub-Program 2. Private banks like HSBC and Santander have participated in the ebus subsidy (first call) by giving more years for credit lines and lower interest rates.

### Regional Initiatives

As mentioned previously, GCF approved a readiness process first submission in May 2016 to Paraguay, Uruguay and Panama. The project is focused on supporting the government’s efforts of coordinating national e-mobility efforts through regional cooperation, including building capacities for technology assessment and deployment, as well as capacity-building for policy development. This objective will be ensured through close coordination with the existing programs and initiatives. The proposed Program is focused on strengthening the region’s market transformation while existing entities are more for creating readiness for electric mobility in general.

### Engagement with Sub-Program 2

In the case of the Sub-Program 2, CAP has performed country diagnostics and market studies support with multiple interviews and meetings realized with all major stakeholders including national and local governmental bodies, energy utilities, private sector representatives, NGOs, and civil society. These activities have also been carried out with stakeholders during the formulation of the Funding Proposal (see Annex 7 for details).

In the case of Panama, the community has been involved in carrying out the pilot tests of electric buses in Panama City. CAP has organized several workshops to inform the public about the benefits of electric mobility on these buses and give them to know the benefits of electric mobility in terms of environment and sustainability.

In the case of Paraguay, the community is general welcomes the different electrification initiatives, understanding that this technology can be a solution for the lack of public transport services. In addition, citizens support this technological innovation to an efficient and environmentally friendly transportation system.

### Relevant NGOs will be consulted during the implementation, such Mesa de Movilidad Eléctrica (like GIEET, but private members); AUDER (Asociación Uruguaya de Energías Renovables) that started with renewables; and now also involved in electric mobility; and CONSORCIO DE USUARIOS Y Transporte Colectivo (Coordinator of Public Transport Users).

Also, having the surprises of electricity generation, the support for electrification can generate an important field of opportunities.

### Public Transport System

In Panama, the Autoridad del Tranvía y Transporte Terrestre (ATT) is the one who has the power to administer and implement regulations regarding operating and enforcing regulations, in addition to assuring road safety competences. It is also in charge of assigning operating permits and public transport concessions. However, this entity is not a monopoly but it is a public service provider that operates 10 bus lines with 250 buses (private sector) and 340 buses with METRO, which has 1,400 buses (apprent). And is a public company. Payment method for Metro is by card and is integrated with the national system of electronic payment (Banco de la Nación). ATT also operates a bus line that uses a consolidated electric vehicle which already has pilot e-buses (2016). In 2017, it was introduced in order to understand the impact of E-vehicles on efficiency and environmental performance. The fleet is around 3,150 buses.

In the case of Paraguay, all operators in the transport sector are private. For the Asunción area, the Metropolitan Area Passenger Transport Enterprises Center was created (CETRAPAR) and also interviewed when they have decided

To have 100% electrification with electric buses by 2050 (about 200 buses). Transportation companies at the metropolitan level are private and their routes are regulated by the National Transportation System (SNT). The SNT also determines, through a public tender process, the number of permits for each route. At the national level, there is competition between municipal, inter-municipal, departmental, and national levels. PV has a pilot at the private level with the concessionaire company MAGNO. In 2019, the government decided to extend the concession period from 35 years to 40 years for the passenger transport system in Paraguay. MAGNO was interviewed and declared to be 100% electric by 2030, leading to its inclusion.

### Efficiency and effectiveness: (max. 500 words; approximately 1 page)

The Program introduces a concession financing mechanism that addresses barriers to the uptake of commercial EVs and supports a transformation shift to low carbon transportation. GCF concessional financing is critical – in absence of this, the project would not be viable. The program has been designed with a phased approach (Annex 22) clearly shows that it works for countries with large fleets of conventional EVs (N>100 units) used at least, initially focused on public transport and intercity buses. The program is designed to support the transition from commercially non-viable (with different degrees of non-viability). The market studies however also reveal that in the medium term, the commercial fleet will be more viable than the public transport fleet. Countries can take advantage of this circumstance from an early stage if they invest early on fleets of EVs to reduce production costs and increase their competitiveness.

The major financial barriers for the potential beneficiaries which prevent alternative financing options are (i) the low level profit margins of EV investments, (ii) high purchase price, (iii) long payback periods and very long pay-back times; (iv) the lack of availability of financing due to performance risks, asset risks (as a result of the lack of charging infrastructure), and operational risks. The program is designed with a phased approach (Annex 22) clearly shows that it works for countries with large fleets of conventional EVs (N>100 units) used at least, initially focused on public transport and intercity buses. The methodology used intends to capture two key variables: (i) the EIRR computes positive externalities from replacing high-emission vehicles with electric vehicles by considering reasonable costs of carbon and (ii) FIRR which intends to capture the additionality for using concessional funds.

No financial models and regulatory frameworks have been developed that demonstrate a significant EV uptake for any specific vehicle segments. Only Uganda started to push for E-buses on a small scale.

## Low

1. EV prices are decreasing in all vehicle segments due to decreasing battery prices, increasing competition and supply of EVs.
2. Risks associated with vehicle reliability and performance, information and re-charge capabilities, and the workability of new technologies are substantially reduced.
3. Policies are more conducive for e-buses and allow EVs to be more competitive.

The Program creates the required information for reducing risks and increasing adoption of EVs. Furthermore, The Program will improve the framework condition for EVs through the development of a regulatory package for an EV-conducive ecosystem, such as incentives for public and commercial buses, and eliminates or reduces barriers to mass EV deployment, such that the cost of EVs is comparable to fossil fuel models and financing structures can be developed. This will enable concessional support from GCF on a regional level the knowledge products and technical assistance provided by countries in implementing mobility sub-sector reforms, including the creation of a non-reversible dynamic towards electrification of the transport sector.

**Page 84**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.01 PAGE 78

## Sustainability

Initial steps have been taken by some countries and some have developed comprehensive EV targets and strategies. However, policies have not yet been rolled out to implement the level and intensity of change required for a higher level of commercialization of electric vehicles.

At the end of the Program (based on an implementation period of 8 years this would be 2030) there are operational charging points in many countries and in multiple countries also including urban fast charging infrastructure. This is due to dropping EV prices point-to-cost based incentives, and lower risk for investors for the involved vehicle segments and markets.

The market analysis performed per country shows that private investors would then be willing to invest in public charging points (public and private) at standard national financial conditions without subsidies.


## Medium

The magnitude and time of commercialization will depend on a decisive manner by the Program through rolling out incentives, policies through risk reduction measures making EVs more attractive for investors. Lower risk profiles of EV investments are critical for attracting private capital, for example due to better information of the market, improved maintenance and repair services, and the establishment of fast charging infrastructure which is key to its improved regulatory and enabling environment.

The Program will also work with the financial profile of EVs and due diligence on the business case being conducive a mobility surrounding. The Program will also aim at providing a long-term enabling policy environment for private sector investments in e-mobility.

### E.T. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The programme will apply the standard procedures established and agreed by CAF and GCF for monitoring, reporting and evaluation of operations in the Accreditation Manual for Programme Management Units (PMUs) as it is set up. The PMU will ensure the day-to-day monitoring by overseeing the implementation of the sub-projcts, meeting with the beneficiaries on a bi-annual basis, ensuring coordination with PMU, exchanging with local government, and reporting to CAF and GCF. The PMU will also monitor the project progress on an ongoing basis and report to the Project Owner on average once in a year. Following this project, a project implementation report will be shared with all the main stakeholders. Furthermore, PMU will ensure that the project is completed within the agreed timeframe and budget. The project implementation report will be submitted to CAF for review and will be assessed through an internal quality review process based on several indicators (technical implementation, disbursement, procurement outcomes, etc.). Red flag issues will be identified and addressed in a timely manner.

During the implementation reporting period (starting from the date of effectiveness of the FAAs and the Programme completion date), CAF will receive regular reports from the beneficiaries, in order to track progress and identify potential issues, as well as improvement opportunities. In this document, the beneficiary will report on activities carried out during the reporting period, including any changes made since the last report. The PMU will also submit a Project Implementation Report (PIR) at an annual basis for the period ending on 31 December within 60 days after the end of the relevant annual period. The first PIR shall be submitted by 31 March.

The mid-term and final independent evaluation of the Programme will be organized and managed by the PMU in the Program in accordance with the GCF’s evaluation criteria as per the GCF Evaluation Policy[16]. CAF will appoint a consultant firm to do so. In most cases, CAF would arrange for an external consultant firm to conduct the mid-term and final independent evaluations of the Programme. The consultant firm will be tasked to help develop local evaluation skills. The consultant firm will be tasked with evaluating the impact of the project (economic, environmental, etc.) and writing a report according to the GCF Evaluation Policy[16].

At the end of the implementation period, CAF will prepare project completion report summarizing the main events of the implementation phase, the results achieved, and the lessons learned. This report will also provide recommendations for future improvement.

Finally, during the implementation and repayment period, a biannual monitoring of the use of funds will be submitted to GCF.


[16] https://www.greenclimatefund.org/documents/evaluation-policy.pdf

# F. Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1. Risk assessment and mitigation measures (max. 3 pages)

### Selected Risk Factor: Probability of borrower to service debt

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Low | High |

**Description:** Inability of the borrower to service debt.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):

CAF's comprehensive due diligence and technical support are designed to assess the borrower’s ability to service the debt. It is envisaged that this program's interventions will not face demand risk, and public entities will be the main source of repayment.

### Selected Risk Factor: 2. Limited market demand for commercial EVs

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Credit | Medium | Medium |

**Description:** Limited demand for commercial EVs. No or limited commercial uptake of EVs after initial investment uptake resulting in limited indirect economic impact. The declining EV price is an external factor, while the magnitude and speed of EV price decline are uncertain.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):

Project preparation and sourcing as well as TA activities on enhancing the market demand will help to mitigate the risk. Successful EV investment sub-projects and TA activities will also contribute to reducing the investment risk level of EVs. Investment risk levels are together with the EV price, the 2 core factors which result in a commercial uptake of EVs. CCAF's comprehensive due diligence and structuring of sub-projects minimize this risk.

### Selected Risk Factor: 3. Technical underperformance

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | High |

**Description:** EVs do not perform as expected, and the operator cannot standard operations.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):

CAF's comprehensive technical design and structuring of sub-projects minimize this risk. All technologies have been pre-tested and proven to be reliable. The project management team will ensure that all procurement and acquisition warranties and contracts incorporate customary performance warranties and provisions for liquidated damages.

### Selected Risk Factor: 4. Failure to comply with national regulations

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

**Description:** Beneficiaries fail to comply with national regulations, including GCF environmental, social policy, gender requirements and compliance with standards, policies, and procedures.

#### Mitigation Measure(s):


---
**Page 99**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 93

Each project will be subject to CAF’s Legal, Environmental and Social Management System as well as gender assessment. Each Project will be appropriately appraised and structured to meet CAF’s GCF and national requirements. Environmental, Social and Gender Action Plans will be developed during project appraisal to achieve the objectives of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and to ensure that the project is in line with CAF’s policies.

Moreover, the program envisages the deployment of TA (component 1) to ensure the conditions both commercial and non-commercial are met for the successful implementation of the investment sub-program 2.


## Selected Risk Factor 5: Limited commercial uptake

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description

No or limited commercial uptake of EVs after initial investment update resulting in limited indirect Program impact. The declining EV price is an external factor. Whilst the magnitude and speed of EV price decline is disputed, the trend of reduced price differential between fossil and electric vehicles is expected to continue.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

Successful EVC investment sub-projects and well implemented TA are core to reduce the investment risk levels of EVs investments. The successful implementation of these measures will result in a commercial uptake of EVs. Good design and implementation of investment sub-projects and of TA can thus minimize this risk.


## Selected Risk Factor 6: Money laundering, terrorist financing and prohibited practices risks

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description

Risk of sub-projects being involved in illicit practices.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

CAF has developed an internal framework for the Prevention and Detection of Money Laundering and Terrorism Financing (PDLTAF). CAF’s risk matrix is implemented in all county offices and have the following elements:

* Compliance Officer appointed by the Executive President
* Internal policies and procedures
* Risk assessment
* ML/FT risk factors assessment
* Screening against sanctions lists such as OFAC, Consolidated United Nations Security Council Sanctions List, European Union Sanctioned Ltd. etc.
* Risk matrix
* AMU/CFT Framework
* AMLNFT Training for all staff

https://www.cafgroup.org/about-caf/what-we-do/access-to-information/art-money-laundering-and-countering-terrorism-financing


The contract (legal agreement) signed among CAF and the relevant parties, there are obligations to comply with AMU/CFT regulations to prevent prohibited practices. Additionally, the business manager performs due diligence through the AMU/CFT risk profile and KYC checklist. They also carry out the screening related parties against the international sanctions list. The risk matrix is updated according to the risk profile, once a year for low-risk clients and twice a year for medium risk.

| Other | Medium | High |

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 94

## Description

The COVID-19 impact potentially results in a higher risk adversity of investors and therefore lower investment levels. Lower demand for public transport services reduces even more the profitability of investing in EVs.

COVID-19 outbreak and related measures taken by the participant countries may impact programme performance. Confinement and social distance measures, as well as the economic crisis derived from the economic downturn can prevent beneficiaries from taking the risk of committing themselves to a credit, or may impede providers to comply with requests.

In response, CAF has approved a USD 50 MM Emergency Loan to each of its country-members directed towards the following sectors: USD 2.600 MM for the Energy Sector; USD 1.300 MM for the Countercyclical Regional Facility for Local Development Banks and a USD 1.200 MM Countercyclical Regional Facility for providing liquidity support to public sector entities in order to help them continue their operations and recover from the economic crisis.

Mitigation Measure(s)

Technical assistance will focus on supporting entities to identify, design and structure e-mobility sub-projects. Detailed legal and technical due diligence will be carried out by the CAF to develop robust sub-projects. Support will be provided under the Program to assist in developing favourable policies for deployment of e-mobilities.

G. OCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The E-Mobility Sub-Program aims to support a large-scale regional transition towards electric-mobility in Latin America focusing on intensive use vehicles leading to reduce fossil fuel consumption, greenhouse gas emissions and air pollution. The Program will be implemented through the Green Climate Fund (GCF) with funding from the GCF via AFD (Sub-Program 1) as an Accredited Entity to cover 8 countries, and one by CAB (Sub-Program 2) to cover 3 countries.

The E-Mobility Sub-Program 2 shall contribute to overcome the barriers to mass deployment of commercial EVs in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama. The potential segments for investment sub-projects in Uruguay, Paraguay and Panama are:

* **Segment of buses:** Private bus operators with different legal regimes which could operate 600+ buses.
* **Segment of LCVs:** A variety of LCV users, both public and private are interested in investing in this type of EV.

Overall, it is expected that the Program presents a low to moderate risk, financing only category B and C risk sub-projects; high risk category "A" sub-projects will be excluded.

The positive impact of EVs comprises reduced GHG emissions, reduced or avoided noise levels, reduced diesel consumption, and reduced air pollution. The Program expects direct emissions reductions of 820,000 tCO2e, over the lifetime of investments co-financed by the Program, as well as 16 million tCO2e of avoided emissions from the replacement of fossil fuel vehicles with EVs.

The Program contributes to sustainable development goals (SDG) 3 ("good health and well-being"), SDG goal 7 ("affordable and clean energy"), SDG goal 9 ("industry, innovation and infrastructure"), SDG goal 11 ("sustainable cities and communities"), and SDG 13 ("climate action").

The main adverse impact will only manifest at the end of the EV battery life when they no longer reach the high performance level required for their intended use. The batteries have an expected lifespan of approximately 8 to 10 years after which they must be replaced by new ones. The disposal of old batteries can generate environmental and health hazards, including toxic substances such as lead, cadmium, nickel, and lithium. These materials are hazardous waste that need to be managed accordingly. The two environmentally sustainable alternatives of recycling and second life of EVs are still in their infancy for many shared mobility companies, presenting technical, regulatory, and financial challenges.

Due to the interdependencies of activities that are part of the scope of this Program, other less significant but important impacts may also occur, which include: (i) increased traffic congestion; (ii) increased impacts during infrastructure maintenance (noise, dust, waste); (iii) soil and water contamination as a result of metal leaching and other sources from scrap EV batteries; (iv) potential increase in the number of accidents due to driver distraction; (v) possible increase in employment loss due to change in job dynamics (labor contraction); (vi) health and safety risks during charging and maintenance activities.

All Program interventions, at a local level, will be required to comply with CAB’s E&S Safeguards as well as GCF’s ESF and Performance Standards. In this regard, all sub-projects will be subject to an environmental and social due diligence following CAB’s E&S Safeguards in order to ensure that gaps of CAB's environmental and social due diligence are:

* **Avoid:** Environmental and social risks and impacts of each activity.
* **Propose:** Appropriate measures to avoid, minimize, and/or offset these risks and their impacts;
* **Monitor:** The implementation execution of these measures during the implementation phase for the operation;

**CAFs ES Safeguards Performance Standards (ESPS) are applied in the following areas:**

1. S01 Evaluation and management of environmental and social impacts
2. S02 Management of natural resources
3. S03 Conservation of biological diversity
4. S04 Pollution prevention and management

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 96

## GENDER ASSESSMENT AND ACTION PLAN (MAX. 500 WORDS; APPROXIMATELY 1 PAGE)

In Latin America, including Paraguay, and Uruguay, the gender equality agenda has gained strength from recent political changes in the region. These include the ratification of CEDAW in 2018, the adoption of non-discrimination, and the inclusion of CEDAW principles** in national legislation. However, despite new policy frameworks, women continue to face significant barriers on their progress towards gender equality, especially into the labour market and key decision-making positions.

In all 3 countries, the labour force participation of female as a share of the female population at working age is between 50% and 60%. This is lower than in other regions. Women are more likely to be employed in informal sectors across countries and women's concerns on low paid jobs and informality. Despite many laws, gender wage gaps persist in all three countries. In Paraguay, the proportion of women in managerial positions is above 50%, while in Uruguay it is below 20%. Political representation of women, in terms of seats as parliamentarians at the national and local level, shows even poorer figures.

Concerning violence against women, according to UN Women, in Uruguay, 2% of ever-partnered women and girls at the age between 15-49 have been subjected to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the last year. In Paraguay, 30% of women aged 18–64 reported that they had experienced physical violence in their lifetime; 27% of women aged 15–49 years reported that they had been subject to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner.

A report by CAF and the FAO Foundation shows that gender-based harassment and violence has many forms and takes place in public places, on streets, at the stations and stops used to access public transport, as well as on the vehicles themselves. The victim is often female, and the perpetrator is usually male. The harasser may be a stranger or someone known to the victim. The resulting fear impacts many women's ability to move freely and influences their mobility and daily decisions. In Paraguay, 20% of women aged between 15-49 years reported that they had experienced sexual violence in these countries and underreported; the COVID-19 pandemic is exacerbating the numbers. The ultimate goal should be to eliminate gender-based harassment and violence.


---
**Page 103**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 97

regular trips women do every day. Incidents will happen in the e-buses while waiting, walking to and from the stop or using charging stations, incidents will increase at night, in isolated vehicles and unplanned units, but in combined units as well. A highly sensitive issue considering that women are more likely to rely on public transportation than men and to be exposed to violence in this context. The gender gap is also a major concern for the sector, which is heavily affected by the construction and transport sector in the region. The pandemic has worsened this context, complicating the return to work and increasing the risk of exposure.

In terms of gender equality and social inclusion, there are two main goals the program should achieve:

1. Impact on the workforce: reduction of sex-segregation in employment while creating new jobs and expertise.
2. Impact on the transport sector: reduction of gender biases and exclusions from transportation while changing the system to a low-carbon one.

The project may reduce or broaden (whether gender issues are taken or not as consideration) the current disparities in terms of access and usage of transportation means by women, children, the elderly, people with disabilities and other socially constructed disparities.

To ensure that current public transport leadership levels are sustained or even increased they must tackle gender and other socially constructed disparities.

There are two main reasons behind the barriers and exclusions regarding women's mobility:
- First, the sector is dominated by men who have historically been in charge of decision-making and do not take into account trip-sharing and the different patterns and needs of women and other vulnerable groups.
- Second, harassment and other forms of gender-based violence in the vehicles, buses and, even, in general, the public space.

So, it is expected that for the Edition Program, the contribution to reducing the gender gap in e-mobility and low-carbon transport will be addressed through three-fold actions. These are grouped into four components of the project as follows:
- The first three-fold action: First, the transport sector is a catalyst to change social norms. For instance, by raising awareness on the importance of women's mobility and promoting gender equality in the transport sector, it can contribute to reducing the gender gap in roles within the EV (electric) vehicle markets; and by considering the mobility of care when designing transport operations, it can help reduce the gender gap in employment opportunities for women. Second, the project will support women entrepreneurs who are interested in starting a business or improving their existing businesses in traditionally sex-segregated occupations or improve access to investment loans for female entrepreneurs. Third, the capacity building, training, and sharing of know-how on women and men designing the transport sector can be improved by providing training opportunities for women in all levels of decision-making within the transport sector.
- The second three-fold action: This two-fold approach will be addressed as part of the implementation of the four Components of the project.

A gender assessment has been realized for all countries as well as a gender action plan. This serves as a guideline for each country to implement the gender action plan, which is an essential component in ensuring that gender equality and women's empowerment at the project level and support the use of gender-designated indicators. The implementation of this gender action plan will be supported by the following three-fold actions: (a) application of a gender lens when designing the roadmap for different EV segments and EV support policies; (b) increased awareness about gender equality gaps and how to address them; and (c) capacity building, training, and sharing of know-how on women and men designing the transport sector.

The Gender Action Plan guarantees that these general recommendations are transformed into specific activities at the project level. The implementation will be supported by three-fold actions: (a) application of a gender lens when designing the roadmap for different EV segments and EV support policies; (b) increased awareness about gender equality gaps and how to address them; and (c) capacity building, training, and sharing of know-how on women and men designing the transport sector.

## G.3 Financial management and procurement (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### Financial Management

As per the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), CAF, as the Accredited Entity, shall be responsible for the overall management of the financial resources allocated to the Program. This includes the development of policies, rules, procedures, including administering and managing the use of GCF Processes, as well as the monitoring, evaluation and reporting on the Program.

CAF will enter into a Funding Activity Agreement with the GCF, for the Loan and Grant, which will describe the specific requirements regarding the financial management of GCF’s resources for the program. In addition, CAF will provide the necessary information to ensure that the GCF can monitor the implementation of the project components (MR Components) implementation. GIZ will apply its own procurement guidelines.

For non-sovereign borrowers, a credit risk monitoring will be carried out by CAF through the assessment, among others, of the borrower’s financial statements and other relevant documents.

CAF will ensure absolute transparency in the use of GCF funds. For that purpose, CAF has internal processes and procedures that ensure a robust financial control, which were certified in the accreditation process before the GCF Accreditation Committee. The resources allocated to the Program are transferred from CAF’s central account to CAF’s portfolio management team. This team ensures that all conditions and covenants are met throughout the life of each transaction. In addition, CAF will ensure that all transactions are monitored on a semi-annually/biannually to CAF’s management the performance of those transactions undermanagement. Finally, as an additional measure, CAF has established a risk management committee which is responsible for monitoring and evaluating internal processes and procedures have been followed in accordance with existing guidelines. Portfolio functions are governed by the Manual for the Credit and Equity Investment Program (MN/VRP-058) and Manual for the Revolving Fund of Operations de Estructuración Financiera) and its Manual for the Revision and Risk Assessment of Operations Under Procurement (MN/VRP-064).

In addition, CAF will conduct external annual audits to assess the adequacy use of the GCF’s funds. These audits will be undertaken by international certified auditors.

### Disbursement

All GCF disbursements will be related to:

* Loan disbursements on sub-projects: Disbursement conditions will be defined on a project-by-project basis and will be included in the loan agreement signed between CAF and the borrower;
* Fund transfers to GCF whose disbursements will be reported to CAF;

Payment of disbursements will be subject to the following conditions:

* Loan resources will be administered as per CAF’s Manual for the Credit and Equity Investment Program (MN/VRP-058). The Manual guides the process for all the phases of the credit, including: (i) Origination; (ii) Evaluation; (iii) Approval; (iv) Disbursement; (v) Monitoring and Reporting. All disbursements will be made in accordance with CAF’s following its Manual for the Management of Technical Cooperation Operations (MN/VRP - 064).

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP195

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The programme focuses comprehensively on technical assistance and subproject origination, structuring and financing in each country. | Greenhouse gas reduction impacts and favorable financial returns are attributed to an indicative pipeline that is to be finalized. |
| Different financing modalities and range of local partners proposed, including local financial institutions. | Risks related to macroenvironment and impact on lending by participating local financial institutions. |

The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XIII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

a. The programme will implement interventions to kick-start mass deployment of electric vehicles (EVs) significantly earlier than a business-as-usual scenario by reducing the risk profile of investments and thus providing comprehensive technical assistance and subproject origination, structuring and financing in each country.

b. The programme also aims at a macroeconomic enabling a faster uptake of e-mobility, avoiding a lock-in of long-lived assets that rely on fossil fuel technology. It fills the gap between initial pilot schemes and large-scale market support, as well as providing technical assistance and subproject origination, structuring and financing in each country.

c. These interventions are made at a point in time where e-mobility is not yet commercially viable and thus requires significant public sector support, as it is expected to take several years before there is significant uptake of e-mobility.

d. Transport contributes almost a quarter of the current global energy-related greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions and more than any other energy end-use sector. Latin America already accounts for 10 per cent of global GHG emissions and transportation-related GHG emissions from many countries in Latin America represent even a higher proportion than the


---
**Page 114**
---

# Global warming

Limiting the average global temperature increase to below 2 degrees Celsius requires changing the trajectory of transport emissions, which involves the development of an integrated electromobility ecosystem encompassing various transport modes, coupled with the low-carbon electrification of power generation. (Source: Green Climate Fund, 2015; International Energy Agency and Climate Call to Action, 2015). Modelling published by the International Energy Agency suggests that EVs need to represent 35 per cent of global vehicle sales in 2030. Latin America is a key region for this transition.

The programme focuses on commercial EVs in intensive use categories (i.e. electric buses, light-duty and heavy-duty trucks). It will provide technical assistance to countries with their new charging infrastructure and grid upgrades. Investments are linked with new business models and service delivery structures that will enhance the attractiveness and sustainability of electromobility solutions. The programme is designed to make a significant contribution towards modal shift in the participating countries.

The total cost of the programme, including GCF investment, is USD 76.8 million of which USD 68.8 million is in loans and USD 9.8 million in grants for TA. Mitigation impact over the lifetime of the assets is estimated at 3.5 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) per year from avoided emissions by electric vehicles.

The programme envisages financing at least one project in each participating country.
## Component-by-component analysis

The programme will be implemented in Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay, and has the following components:

### Component 1: Establishment of an e-mobility conductive ecosystem (technical assistance) [Total cost: USD 20.8 million; GCF cost: USD 23.6 million]

This component will create a policy and business framework conducive to massive deployment of electric vehicles in public transport, including the development of relevant city EV policies, training, regional capacity-building, knowledge materials, outreach and dissemination, programme monitoring and national-level (sectoral electrification roadmaps, establishment of charging infrastructure, etc.), as well as technical assistance for countries on battery re-use, recycling and disposal.

This component addresses the issues of lack of policies conducive to EV deployment and lack of business models (barriers 1.1 and 1.2, respectively). The component and its associated activities provide TA to enable effective financial assistance and to create a 'gender-responsive' policy environment for the implementation of the programme. The technical assistance is coordinated and realized with the relevant national authorities to ensure complementarity of efforts.

### Component 2: Deployment of e-buses and enhancement of public transport (financial assistance) [Total cost: USD 178.1 million; GCF cost: USD 95.29 million]

Component 2 addresses the higher upfront costs associated with the deployment of e-buses, coupled with innovative business models based on a separation of asset ownership and operations, thus modernizing and increasing the attractiveness of the public transport sector while also making it financially sustainable.

### Component 3: Addressing the higher up-front procurement costs of e-buses as well as the need to deploy large-scale fleets of e-buses to showcase their technical and commercial viability to operators. Public urban transport buses are the target. This includes different e-bus technologies, including battery electric vehicles (BEVs), plug-in hybrid electric vehicles (PHEVs) and fuel cell electric vehicles (FCEVs). The programme will finance up to 12m and different operational structures (mixed traffic buses as well as units operating on bus-only

# 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Sustainable development potential

The programme supports a paradigm shift to low-emission transportation systems.

The programme aims to overcome the barriers identified to kick-start the main deployment of commercial EVs in developing countries, including lack of infrastructure, high upfront costs, and mobility patterns by setting the foundations of low-emission, reliable, efficient, secure and affordable e-transportation. The T4C supports the development of a conducive environment for the adoption of transport systems and penetration via enabling policies, infrastructure investment, technology and knowledge-sharing.

The programme has potential for scaling up and replication at the national and international level through capacity created in the mobility ecosystem, proven cases of successful deployment, and the creation of new business models, products, services, technologies, and knowledge products. It also has high potential for knowledge-sharing and learning through the T4C template and the development of a global network of experts and stakeholders, which contributes to the creation of an enabling framework. The T4C includes support for enabling policies for EV deployment, including support vital to set up, improve and maintain infrastructure, national public policies and legal, regulatory frameworks for a mobility shift.

The programme also aims to have an overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways in developing countries. Without the contribution of the target countries, the global transition towards significant GHG reductions in the transportation sector. Without this contribution the target countries’ efforts to achieve their NDCs and other national commitments on low-emission kick-starting EV deployment is thus consistent with relevant national climate-resilient development pathways.

The programme is well aligned with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and aims to reduce GHG emissions in the transport sector. The SDGs are: SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 9 (industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities) and SDG 13 (climate action).

The programme’s major environmental co-benefit is the reduction of emissions of pollutants and reduced noise emissions. A major component of this benefit will be the reduction in emissions of nitrogen oxides (NOx), which will be addressed via the shift from fossil-fuelled vehicles to EVs. The major social benefit is improved air quality and reduced noise in urban areas through the use of EVs, which will contribute to better health outcomes for residents living near transport infrastructure.

The economic cost of pollution has been calculated using a methodology based on the World Bank’s Global Burden of Disease (GBD) model, based on local levels of pollution at the ground level and the impact on health and costs caused by this type of pollution. The programme will contribute to the following areas: (1) improving urban transportation systems; (2) improving sex-disaggregated data collection on urban transport; (3) improving women’s access to jobs; (4) interventions including awareness-raising


---
**Page 117**
---

### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: High**

21. GCF concessionality is expected to address financial and technical capacity barriers, de-risk and mobilize private sector capital for the EV sector, support public finances, and incentivise the shift to EVs in an urban context at a time when the governments of the three beneficiary countries have severe fiscal constraints and macroeconomic uncertainties.

* The programme aims to provide concessional funding early-mover costs and disadvantages including high financial risks; and lack of sufficient commercial lending capacity. However, most private sector banks and other financial institutions are not currently able to fund such activities with commercial loans.
* The programme countries depend on TBA to enhance the viability of the subprojects and to achieve long-term transformational impacts in the EV sector. The beneficiary countries have limited access to concessional public funds on their own, due to the current fiscal situation in the countries. For example, the economic slowdown in China has led to a reduction in foreign direct investment inflows.
* High global inflation rate have required extensive policy support to be deployed by the authorities of the three beneficiary countries, including increased spending on energy subsidies, a sharp expansion of social support and health spending. Thus, the GCF grants are essential for successful implementation of the required EV activities.

### 3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

28. The programme supports the governments' policy objectives on low-emission transport, urban development, and climate change commitments, nationally determined contributions and sectoral plans. No objection letters have been received from all countries.
* The national designated authorities of the countries included in the programme have committed to the development of electric mobility. CAT and its partners (AEDF, PROPARCO, KIVI, GEZ) have held meetings with key stakeholders at the national level including development banks, private banks, municipalities and regional states, power utilities and transport operators to ensure that the programme responds to their needs.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

**Scale: Medium to high**

29. The total capital investment is USD 278.5 million with a requested GCF contribution of USD 74.1 million. This represents an internal rate of return (IRR) of 3.16% and the cost-effectiveness of the GCF investment in direct terms of USD 23 per EV. This value is lower than that calculated by applying the subsidy finance from other countries with significant EV numbers for buses, which are not included in this programme.
* The total capital investment includes associated costs such as infrastructure development and long-term benefits once a conducive ecosystem is in place and the market has been kick-started. The GCF contribution will significantly accelerate the transition to a fully electrified vehicle market, impacting not only the update of the fleet but also the overall EV adoption rate.
* According to the high potential scenario (not including decreasing emission grid factors), the programme can achieve a total CO2 reduction of 16 MTCO2eq. Results are based on the assumption that the GCF contribution is fully mobilized, and the programme mobilizes significant co-financing from both public and private sources.


---
**Page 118**
---

20. Concessional loans are expected to finance up to 90 per cent of electric buses, LCVs and public a/e; whereas equity from private sponsors will account for the remaining 10 per cent of financing. Concessional loans are expected to finance the total cost of urban fast-charging infrastructure projects. The GCF concessional loan is expected to be used for total financing of the three types of EVs related to finance investment activities for battery electric buses, e-buses and e-LCVs. Up to 32 per cent of the GCF concessional loans are expected to be used for total financing of the three types of EV.

The analysis shows that the projects would not be bankable financed under a structure that does not include the GCF concessional loan. The GCF concessional loan is needed to bring down the cost of capital and to influence the trajectory of the technology costs. It will need to decrease sufficiently in the future to bring the returns on investment within the range of 10 per cent or more.

25. In terms of economic returns, the GCF calculates a return per technology and per country. The average value is 15 per cent with all the interventions above 12 per cent. This economic return is above the expected social discount rate for the countries under consideration.


### IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

28. Programme Background: The programme is part of the regional efforts that aims to accelerate Electric Vehicle (EV) deployment and enable a large-scale transition towards electrification in urban areas. It will support the introduction of battery electric buses, e-buses and light electric commercial vehicles (e-LCVs) and their related infrastructure in France. The programme will also provide technical assistance for establishing a sustainable mobility ecosystem for electrification and shift to public transport. The programme will be implemented through direct funding of projects, 1,150 e-LCVs, and up-to-60 fast-charging infrastructure networks both directly and through intermediation. Among the key benefits of the programme includes the reduction of air pollution in urban areas, increased road safety for non-motorised transport and multi-modality options for urban dwellers with more resilient urban transport infrastructure.

29. Environmental and Social Risk Category: The ESMP defines the programme moderate environmental and social risk category as per the GCF’s environmental and social risk categorisation. This is rationalised by financing activities with potentially significant environmental or social impacts that are likely to be broadly site-specific, largely reversible, and readily addressed through mitigation measures. No activities in the programme are considered to have a high environmental or social risk; those that would fall under Category B will be supported under the programme. The Secretariat confirms the overall moderate environmental and social risk category and that this is within the GCF’s environmental and social risk accreditation level.

#### Safeguard Instruments

30. The ESMP provides an Environmental and Social Analysis and Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP). The ESMP is a key component of the programme for environmental and social risk assessment, an analysis on the potential environmental and social impacts associated with the project components, and mitigation measures to address such risks and impacts with specification of roles and responsibilities for appraisal and supervision. The ESMP establishes general


---
**Page 119**
---

guidelines including indicative outlines of subproject safeguard instruments to help implementers and partners develop subproject specific environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) and environmental and social management plan (ESMP) in accordance with AEF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards that are aligned with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards standards.

### 3. Compliance with GCF's Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards.
The paragraphs below describe the ESS components for each subproject, including ESIA and ESMP.

#### 3.1 Assessment and Management of Environmental and Social Risks and Impacts.
The ESMP provides an analysis of the potential environmental and social risks based on the characteristics of the project and its implementation. The ESMP will assess the severity of such risks and impacts identified, which focuses on EV deployment and the construction and operation of charging infrastructure. For each subproject, a detailed ESIA and ESMP will be prepared for each subproject. The ESIA and ESMP will be submitted to the AEF for due diligence evaluation.

#### 3.2 Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP).
The ESAP is an action plan that outlines how the project will address identified risks and impacts, as well as any gaps between the project’s environmental and social safeguards and those required by public entities. The ESIA and ESMP will be evaluated by the AEF to ensure compliance with applicable environmental and social safeguards of the AEF. For subprojects financed through international financial institutions (IFIs), the ESIA and ESMP must also comply with IFI environmental and social risk assessment systems (ESRAS) or the environmental and social risk management policies and procedures, as well as any other relevant requirements for environmental and social action plans (ESAPs) contractually required in the loan agreements. In both cases, gaps with AEF’s ESS standards will be identified and the AEF will depict additional measures to address these gaps.

#### 3.3 Environmental and Social Action Plan (ESAP).
The implementation of the ESAP is a key component for ensuring that the project meets compliance with AEF's applicable ESS standards. All subprojects under the programme are required to comply with the applicable national, local laws and regulations in the respective countries where they operate as well as GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards Standards.

#### 3.4 Labour and Working Conditions.
The programme identified medium adverse impacts on employment loss due to change in job dynamics and required skillset, and potential occupational health risks associated with EV deployment and charging infrastructure operations and of EVs and charging infrastructure. The programme will provide technical assistance to the project countries to develop a comprehensive training plan that requires the necessary new skills to transition to the new market demands, and an EV units’ providers/ sellers will be required to ensure that their employees are trained in EV-related safety issues. The programme will prioritize training of existing personnel. The ESMP triggers AEF’s ESS safeguard on working conditions and labour rights. As set out in the AEF exclusion list, the programme will not finance operations to be carried out by executing entities that carry out the production or activities involving harmful exploitation of child labour, forced labour, or other forms of human trafficking.

#### 3.5 Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention.
The programme’s ESMP is guided with the aim of avoiding and minimizing negative impacts on human health, biodiversity, and ecosystems associated with EV deployment and charging infrastructure operations that potentially generate wastewater and hazardous materials as regards the use, replacement, reuse and disposal of EVs and their components (e.g., batteries, chargers, and other electronic components). Additional impacts can be attributed to small-scale construction activities of grid-connection facilities, including the generation of noise, dust, air pollution, soil erosion, solid and liquid waste, and soil and water contamination from and scrapping of out-of-use vehicles, such as oil and lubricants, lead-acid batteries, tires and scrap metals. ### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESS 7: Indigenous Peoples
The three countries where the project is being implemented have indigenous peoples who may be affected by the project. The EAs will include a consultation process with indigenous peoples, including those with no prior experience of participation in such processes. The executive entities (EEs) will develop and conduct consultation and stakeholder engagement activities to ensure that indigenous peoples are adequately informed about the project where an environmental and social assessment identifies indigenous people as stakeholders. Participation and consent requirements will be implemented in accordance with the AIP, safeguarding policies, and other relevant international standards. The EAs should include a process which meets the requirements of the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, arrived at by context, depicting the necessary measures to ensure that the project is consistent with the rights of indigenous peoples for the negative impacts and to ensure that the benefits generated by the project are inclusive and culturally appropriate. The EAs will also incorporate an indigenous peoples’ participation approach.

### ESS 8: Cultural Heritage
The ESM establishes specific requirements for subprojects under ESMF, when warranted, must contain measures to ensure the protection of the cultural heritage from loss or damage and support its preservation. The ESM also supports the development of a project-specific cultural heritage management plan (CHMP). The CHMP application of field-based studies and documentation of findings as well Chance Findings Protocol (CFP) should be developed in accordance with the requirements set out in the CHMP Application, and preservation of cultural resource findings must be undertaken by or in coordination with the competent authorities. During the preparation of subprojects, All could require the implementation of measures to ensure that the project is consistent with the requirements set out in these requirements, and in the event of a lack of capacity of the partners, an external evaluation team should be established to provide guidance on the development of the CHMP and the design of specialized studies required. The AE is recommended to incorporate chance finds protocol (CFP) into the project-specific cultural heritage management plan.

### Sexual Exploitation, Sexual Abuse, and Sexual Harassment (SEAH) safeguarding
The ESM has identified medium SEAR risk related to the deployment of EVs in the public transport sector. The AE should consider the potential risks associated with the deployment of small-scale EV infrastructure works. Potential mitigation measures include training transport operators on gender-based violence (GBV), and supporting the establishment of an adequate regulatory framework for the deployment of EVs, including the development of a code of conduct preventing SEAR and GBV. The AE also incorporates in the gender action plan code of conduct establishing zero-tolerance for SEAR against community workers and project workers and requires that all subprojects include a gender action plan (GAP) with specific measures to promote gender-centred and gender-responsive grievance redress mechanisms (GRM) for SEAR-specific complaints. The AE should also ensure that the implementation of the GAP is monitored, and SEAH risks and GBV to the affected communities and subproject workers, and that GBV and SEAH risks must be mitigated in accordance with the ESMF. The AE should include a gender action plan and be disclosed. The AE provides several avenues for individual complaints and incidents related to SEAR and GBV, including through the project’s AE’s Integrity and Ethics Committee and the Prohibited Practices Prevention Committee, and through the subproject’s internal grievance redress mechanisms (GRM). The ESMF also requires that all subprojects must establish survivor-centred and gender-responsive GRMs incorporating specific procedures to receive, register and manage SEAH complaints and incidents.

## Implementation arrangements

The AE will monitor and supervise the environmental and social performance and compliance with contractual requirements of the specific subprojects financed by programme. All loan agreements will include monitoring and reporting requirements for environmental and social safeguards compliance. A Project Management Unit (PMU), composed of professionals from the AE and the Executing Entity and supported by an external E&S Gender expert, will be established to ensure that all project activities are in line with the objectives and principles of the programme. AE's specialists will carry out supervision missions, review level of environmental and social safeguards compliance, and provide technical assistance to the implementing partners to address non-compliances. The AE will ensure that adequate financial and technical resources are provided for the PMU to perform its tasks effectively.

## Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

The development of the programme is based on a multi-stakeholder approach involving key stakeholders including national government representatives such as from National Designated Authorities, national companies, civil society organizations (CSOs), international organizations, financial institutions, and main actors including those involved in capacity building and financing EV, charging infrastructure, and other mobility value chain. The programme will engage all types of stakeholders during the implementation of the specific sub-projects and capacity building activities through an ongoing process involving stakeholder analysis and planning, disclosure and communication with stakeholders on project progress, risks, benefits, costs, and environmental and social performance reporting to affected communities. The ESMP provides an indicative list of key stakeholders that will be engaged in the programme.

## Grievance redress mechanism

The programme provides access to grievance redress mechanisms at the AE, implementing partners and sub-projects level. As part of the AE's E&S safeguard policy, a centralized system for managing grievances is established with the magnitude and the nature of E&S risks identified. All implementing partners will be required to establish an internal mechanism for addressing environmental and social concerns and grievances of stakeholders that may arise in connection with a sub-project’s gender, social and environmental performance. The AE will provide guidance on how to implement such redress procedures or requirements. The programme expects subproject GMPs to be used to provide the necessary information to address environmental and social concerns and expressions. In addition, all subprojects are required to carry out an extensive information process to publicize the sub-project-level GMRs and ensure that affected people have access to it. These mechanisms will be reported in the ESMP and updated as needed. The AE is responsible for the development of a centralized CRM at the institutional level that can be used to address all possible complaints related to E&S performance, including those from third parties. The AE will keep all relevant stakeholders informed once its corporate CRM is established and keep the ESMP and guidance to subproject GMPs updated.

## Gender policy

The AE provided a gender assessment and GSP with the funding proposal and therefore completed the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

While the gender equality agenda has gained strength in Latin America (including Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay) regarding social and feminist movements, and the legislative framework for women's rights is more advanced than in other regions, there are still strong campaigns against Women (CEDAW) and others. The reality is in the actual implementation of the various commitments the countries have made, slowing progress in the realization of women’s labour


---
**Page 123**
---

market rights, meaningful participation and representation in decision-making processes, and overall different forms of gender-based violence, including realization of human/women’s rights in general. The gender assessment found that in all three countries the female labour force participation rate is below 50 per cent, with a range from 48 to 61 per cent in 2019, which is about 20 per cent lower than the participation rate of men. Gender wage gaps persist across sectors, and gender roles due to home-based care work (Lemma et al., 2017). In all three countries, women are more likely to be employed in low-skilled occupations; occupational segregation persists across countries and women’s careers are concentrated around the age of 35 years. The assessment also found that women aged between 18 and 29 per cent of women and girls between the ages of 15 and 49 have experienced some form of sexual violence in their lifetime, with a higher prevalence among young women (data leading to data for the 12 months from the time the gender assessment was undertaken).

### Specific actions that need to be undertaken to ensure safety when using public transport

There are specific actions that need to be undertaken to ensure safety when using public transport. The most frequent victims of gender-based violence when using transport, and the Covid-19 pandemic has exacerbated this issue. Gender-sensitive measures such as lighting, signage, security cameras and sexual abuse, and safety measures are required when designing and implementing transport systems. In addition, it is recommended that women be included in public sector jobs in the region, and this e-mobility programme presents an opportunity for women to access the green jobs created by the project. The design of new mobility services (e.g., public sector projects) should help women into professional driving licenses and in countries where they are able to opt for the direct jobs that do the -e-buses, e-taxis and e-vans will create). The gender assessment found that women are underrepresented in the transport sector. It is recommended the provision of scholarships for women to receive training on professional driving.

### Gender-responsive GAP

The programmes’ GAP aims to ensure that the various recommendations presented in this report are implemented, with a focus on ensuring the implementation of gender-responsiveness of the e-mobility programme. The AE will appoint a team of four specialists who will lead the implementation of the GAP and provide technical support for the project. It is recommended to ensure that gender specialists with experience in the targeted countries are recruited as part of the implementation of the GAP. The GAPP has identified two main areas where gender equality can be addressed (from an economic, environmental, and social inclusion perspective), as follows: 1) regarding the workforce: reduction of sex segregation in the transport sector; reduction of gender-based violence at work; reduction of ridership; reduction of gender biases and exclusions from transportation while changing to a low-emission system. The GAPP therefore outlines three main gender-specific outputs, each with its own set of indicators and targets. These are: output 1: implementation of the gender equality gender assessments undertaken as part of the development of low-carbon EV road maps and policy documents; output 2: integration of gender equality considerations in transport-related aspects in transportation (regional level); and output 3: gender components integrated in the subjective indicators for the GAPP.

### Key activities that will be implemented under output 1 are:

(1) adoption of a transformative gender perspective, with a focus on mobility, safety and jobs, in the design and review of low-carbon EV road maps and policy documents; (2) development of strategies and implementation plans: design an effective advocacy strategy and action plan for mainstreaming gender equality considerations into the GAPP; (3) capacity building: training of key stakeholders involved in the implementation of the E-Motion subprogramme 2 (Component 1); (4) develop a country-specific gender assessment, including the identification of gender-sensitive measures and the support given to design/review low-carbon EV national road maps and policies; to be rendered by the consultation/participatory processes that will be undertaken to inform the project. The GAPP will engage with key gender/social actors will also be included to strengthen the diagnosis in each country.

## Risks

### 4.3 Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

#### 4.3.1 GCF is requested to provide a joint of USD6 million and a grant of USD7.2 million for the deployment of electric vehicles (EVs) in Peru, Paraguay and Uruguay. GAF will co-finance a senior loan of USD 12 million and raise equity of USD8 million. GAF and GIZ are providing kind contribution amount to USD36 million.

#### 4.3.2 Within the public sector, the programme allows for the provision of financing for both public and private sector entities. The investment may be directly made by GAF or through a financial intermediary (e.g., a development bank) under the Private Sector Component. The level of support and an individual sub-project per country and per vehicle segment will be determined during the implementation period. Different business models (e.g., separate project, co-financing with other donors, public-private partnership etc.) are envisaged depending on the needs of the sub-project in each country.

### 4.3.2. Executing entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (risk)

52. CAF is the AE and EE for the programme. CAF has a strong record in the transport sector by financing over US$120billion in the past 10 years in Latin America. It also has an experienced implementation of EV projects. CAF benefits from preferred credit status and is rated as A3 by Moody’s.

54. GIZ is another EE for the programme which is responsible for executing technical assistance, including the preparation and development of large TA programs and working with different financiers in the M Mobility sector.

### 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

55. Credit risk: GCF will assume the credit risk of sub-borrowers for direct financing in Component B & 4, and LFs for intermediated financing in Component 3. The credit risk of sub-borrower recipients is considered low as the majority of the project costs to be financed will be blended and rank paid loans.

56. Co-financing risk: The equity structure considers 90% of the sub-projects funded with loans and 10% with equity. A minimum of 10% equity co-financing is included in the eligibility criteria. The co-financing risk can be further mitigated by the fact that the project will be executed through a public-private partnership.

57. Technology and market absorption risk: FP states that all technologies have been proven at least in pilot cases in Latin America with similar circumstances. Technological advancement and market acceptance are expected to be high, which is an enabling environment for mass deployment of the EV. On the other hand, this will also lead to the replacement of older vehicles by new ones. This will increase the risk of low residual value needs to be absorbed by the market. Currently, due to the nascent market in EV sector, there are no secondary markets available and hence the risk of low residual value assets due to the technology obsolescence. This will affect the subsequent EV projects as it will reduce the willingness of investors to invest in the EV sector.

58. Project viability and Concessionality: The project viability will depend on the political willingness and support from the government. The target impact of the programme is assumed that the number of electric vehicles will increase by 10% per year, which was not affected by pandemic. Stable frameworks and favourable regulatory environment will help to attract new investors in the EV sector and scale up the EV sector.

59. GCF concessional financing will reduce investment risks through the investment combined with private co-financing. The interest rates for public and private borrowers are differentiated. The interest rates for private and public sector borrowers will be differentiated. For the private sector interest rate, it is determined on project-by-project basis. The AE is contractually required to pass down this concessionality to the end beneficiaries.

### 4.3.4. Compliance risk (high risk)

60. The beneficiary countries – Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay - are not subject to United Nations Security Council (UNSC) restrictive measures. The AE along with GIZ will act as the Executive Agent for the programme. The AE has a strong record in implementing projects and has not raised any material issues when it comes to its ability to implement the planned activities, from a compliance perspective. However, there is still some risk of non-compliance with international practices and sanctions related risks to be of low probability and medium impact. Throughout the programme, the AE intends to implement its standard set of controls, inclusive of providing
