## 4.4 Fiduciary

### Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF)

The CAF will perform the dual role of AE and EE for the programme.

As an AE, CAF will be responsible for the overall management, implementation oversight and supervision of the programme in line with its own internal rules, policies and procedures, including the use of GCF resources. This includes the use of GCF proceeds, as well as monitoring, evaluation and reporting responsibilities.

On the other hand, as an EE, CAF will be responsible for providing governance, oversight and quality assurance to the project activities in accordance with the terms of reference, activity agreement and the accreditation master agreement (AMA). Project teams will be responsible for implementing the project activities in line with the approved work plan and financial investments of the programme. The subprojects' appraisal process will follow CAF's procedures.

Business plans will be developed by the project team in close collaboration with the TA in accordance with CAF standards and procedures. CAF will provide financial assistance targeting public and private sector project owners (Components 2, 3 and 4), and support the technical assistance for the implementation of the programme.

GIZ will be responsible for implementing activities within the TA component in close collaboration with the project team. This will involve a combination of its own technical staff based in partner countries, as well as independent consultants. # Results monitoring and reporting

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

71. As a mitigation programme, CAF E-Motion is expected to result in overall GHG emission reductions amounting to 3.3 MCO2eq over a 25-year lifespan, of which 0.79 MCO2eq is due to the EV investment. The total cost of this investment is €14 million, as reflected in the cost-benefit calculations available in annex 23 of the funding proposal, and as reflected in the metrics of the integrated results management framework.

72. The programme expects to improve air quality by avoiding emissions of 80 tonnes of PM2.5 and 6,000 tonnes of NOx at the end of the implementation period, as well as saving costs related to health care and traffic accidents. These benefits are estimated to be worth €14 million, as per the environmental and socioeconomic co-benefit indicators provided in section E.5 of the funding proposal.

### Legal assessment

The AMA was signed with the AE on 15 November 2018, and it became effective on 19 March 2018.

The AE has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme. It is recommended that, prior to submission of the funding proposal to the Board (a) the AE has obtained all necessary internal approvals and (b) the AE has provided a legal opinion from the AI in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF confirming that all final internal approvals by the AI have been obtained and that it has the authority and capacity to implement the programme.

The proposed programme will be implemented in three countries: Paraguay, Panama, and Uruguay.

The GCF has signed a bilateral agreement on privileges and immunities with Uruguay, which was signed on 14 November 2017 and became effective on 7 October 2021. The GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities in the two other countries. This means that the GCF may be subject to legal challenges or litigation in those countries, which risks need to be further assessed.

With respect to Panama, the GCF has provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Republic of Panama on 23 April 2018, followed by an updated draft agreement on 17 February 2019. The agreement is under review by the Government.

With respect to Paraguay, the GCF provided a draft agreement on privileges and immunities and a background note to the Government of the Republic of Paraguay on 4 December 2015, followed by an updated draft agreement and note on 3 August 2016. The agreement is under review by the Government.

The Ministry of Foreign Affairs of Paraguay and the Independent Legislature Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, any claims for compensation against the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expatriation in the countries, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP195

## Proposal name: E-Motion: E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation

### Accredited entity:
Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF)

### Country(s):
Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay

Medium

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential: Scale: Medium to high

- A bit of background information will be necessary for this funding proposal (FP). For the part overviews preceding the submission of this request for funding, please refer to the Agence Française de Développement (AFD), Kreditanstalt für Wiederaufbau (KfW), Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), and CDAC conducted independent studies that evaluated the potential impact of the E-Motion concept in the three countries.
- These studies, which were independently funded by each of these institutions, clearly drew out the high potential for the E-Motion concept to contribute significantly to the energy transition opportunity in that region, as shown by the results of the independent evaluation. Existing opportunities exist in the form of a large-scale renewable energy supply and demand mix including large hydropower plants in the region.
- The improvements obtained from the independent studies of the institutions mentioned in the last paragraph led to a decision to develop a regional E-Motion concept note that was not included as part of the original E-Motion concept note. In order to support the development of this E-Motion's concept note, no regional pre-programme was conducted. The E-Motion concept was introduced at the 10th meeting of the GCF Board in June 2023 and has been supported by efforts between all the institutions (AFD, KfW, GIZ and CAF) to avoid duplication of actions.
- It is expected that the E-Motion concept note will foster a more integrated approach within the 11 Latin American countries covered under this central note. At the same time, the original GCF E-Motion programme was broken into two subprogrammes:
    - A regional E-Motion concept note was developed during a question-and-answer interaction between the AE and independent TAP; the advice from the GCF Secretariat to break these submissions into two subprogrammes was based on the countries where the two AEs (CAF and AF) can operate. The GCF Secretariat has confirmed that the two subprogrammes will not operate in the whole region, AFD cannot operate in Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay.
    - Given these differences between the two central notes, the two subprogrammes 1 and 2 will have the following AEs and cover the following AEs:


---
**Page 131**
---

(a) Subprogramme 1 will have AFD as the AE and cover the following eight countries: Argentina, Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Dominican Republic, Ecuador, Mexico, and Peru; and

(b) Subprogramme 2 will have CAP as the AE and cover the following three countries: Paraguay, and Uruguay.

4. Subprogramme 2, with CAP as the AE, is the focus of this independent TAP assessment.

The climate context of this programme can be summarised as follows:

(a) Global emissions are currently on a trajectory to exceed 50 GtCO₂eq by 2030 and global emissions. It is the fastest growing sector of greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions globally and is growing faster than any other energy sector in most markets.

(b) Although there has been significant progress, it will be difficult to limit warming to below 1.5 per cent of GHG emissions, decarbonising the transport sector of Latin American countries, if proper conservation measures are not taken. This is because the transport sector is a major source of GHG emissions and also sound benefits in the countries in terms of delivering modern and clean public transport rolling stock, and so on.

(c) The technologies that will be covered by the proposed programme already have very green power infrastructure, with the following country grid emission factors: Panama (0.213 tCO₂eq/kWh), Colombia (0.458 tCO₂eq/kWh), Brazil (0.679 tCO₂eq/kWh) and Uruguay (0.213 tCO₂eq/kWh). This existing low-carbon production of electricity in the project countries of this FP is a sound requirement for a successful e-mobility system.

(d) The public-private partnership (PPP) and government programmes have been introduced in some countries in Latin America before these current proposed interventions. Countries such as Brazil, Colombia, Costa Rica, Ecuador, Mexico, Panama, Peru, and Uruguay. These e-mobility activities started as pilots that evaluated what kinds of framework and business models must be in place to allow unencumbered introduction of e-mobility in each country. The results from these pilots were then used by the respective government to decide how each country's federal government and the conclusions of these government-led activities can be summarised.

(e) Separation of ownership and operations can be an effective instrument to promote e-buses and can bring in a new financially strong players into the industry.

(f) Increasing the number of charging stations is also one way to reduce the need for the use of diesel fuel, which is the most common bus fuel in the baseline situation.

(g) In order not to thwart the realistic long-term success of the e-bus programme, these subsidies must be reduced significantly after an initial fleet of 10 buses and after having established a charging infrastructure.

(h) Without financial incentives in the early introduction, the e-bus programme will not be commercially competitive. However, some of these pilots have shown that some large cities can achieve a significant reduction in costs due to a boost improving air quality and reducing GHG emissions; and

(i) Uruguay, one of the three countries that will be covered by this FP, showed through its pilot project that there are no barriers that would hinder that.

6. Noting the claim that this government-sponsored pilot in Uruguay was fundamentally successful, the independent TAP questions why it could not be expanded to a commercial scale. From an economic perspective, what are some of the reasons why the success story above may be derived from the explanations below:


---
**Page 132**
---

### (a)
Between November and December 2013, different tests were carried out on a 100 per cent electric 12-meter bus, model K9 of the company BYD. The performance of the tests in Montevideo City, Uruguay was monitored by passenger load simulation and by replacing the diesel engine with an electric motor.

### (b)
Later, in May 2016, the same bus was used for a longer test but in real life conditions. It was used in different lines and transported passengers over several months. The results of the tests were very positive and confirmed that the bus could be put into service.

### (c)
The subsidy is expected to cover the price gap between a conventional bus and an electric bus as well as infrastructure requirements and charging management. As the Government of Uruguay has decided not to subsidize the purchase cost of the bus, rather than the conventional bus, the Electric Bus Subsidy was financed, in this first call, from the future revenues generated by the project.

### (d)
After successful operational results, the Electric Bus Subsidy was approved in 2018 by Law 19.763. This pilot in Uruguay led to the following lessons that are essential for the replication of the model elsewhere:

- The move from battery electric bus (HEB) pilot to a HEB commercial stage will require a subsidy from the start of the commercial stage to overcome the higher upfront costs and lower revenues.
- Since subsidies are easier to introduce and difficult to remove, the Government of Uruguay will prefer an implementation scenario that is not based on subsidy but will be based on a public-private partnership (PPP) with a performance-based baseline and the project;
- Without financial incentives, financing the 2018 Electric Bus Subsidy will have to be continued as it will be key to providing rights and duties for operators, therefore ensuring the HEB operational scheme will continue as planned to generate impact on the environment and public transport.

### (v)
It is therefore clear that without financial incentives (such as those that will be delivered by the Electric Bus Subsidy) it would not be possible to agree to a PPP even though its performance was remarkable at the pilot stage as operators will not assume the financial risk alone.

#### 2.
The main lessons for electric vehicle (EV) in the three countries covered by the FP can be summarised as follows:

- EVs require high capital investment (CAPEX) compared to the fossil fuel alternatives and this is reflected in the higher cost of ownership. The cost of an EV is two to three times higher than the baseline alternatives. This will result in higher capital requirements, higher finance and insurance costs, and higher risk exposure due to higher debt levels.
- The cost of capital available in these countries, like most developing countries, is usually too high for EV deployment to be financially viable. This is the situation in many developing countries with long payback times.
- Another barrier to successful introduction of EV programmes in these countries arises from the lack of a supportive policy framework and business models that are conducive to EV deployment, especially for the public transport sector.
- In the three countries covered by this FP, there is limited knowledge on how optimal EV deployment can be achieved.

### (h)
Lastly, when EV targets are established, there are usually no concrete and tangible support policies conducive to EV uptake in place in the countries.


---
**Page 133**
---

## Implementation methodology built into the design of subprogramme 2 (of this IFI to deliver more than a Sustainable E-mobility programme, which is expected to reduce or completely eliminate the barriers presented in paragraph 7 above). The general characteristics of the business model and the proposed project intervention in Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay can be summarized as follows:

(a) **The two key components of the business model intended to enable the attractiveness and sustainability of e-mobility programmes are:**

(i) **Bulk purchase**, which will be tackled through technical assistance (TA); and
(ii) **Separation of asset ownership and operations/management**;

(b) **Bulk purchase**: Bulk purchase is important not only due to significant price discounts offered by bulk purchasing, but also because it enables the creation of a mobility system in terms of charging infrastructure and bus types allowing, for example, the use of common charging points (e.g. 10 hours of charge).

(c) **Separation of asset ownership and operations/management**: Separation of asset ownership and management is critical due to high investment costs of e-buses, which require a significant amount of capital to be invested in each bus. This can only be done by high net worth private sector investors who are able to access both international and locally sourced financing (e.g., through the use of green bonds, loans with extended debt maturity, etc.) will purchase and own the assets (i.e. buses, charging stations, etc.) and make these available to operators and management of the e-buses lines along designated routes. This separation of asset ownership and operations/management can remove or drastically reduce the impact of higher CAPEX of e-mobility programmes, which has inhibited their deployment in many countries.

(d) **Subprogramme 2 implements interventions to kick-start E-mobility development significantly earlier than under a business as usual scenario by reducing the risk profile of investment and enabling the creation of an enabling environment for e-mobility. The outcome is that it functions as market accelerator, enabling a far faster uptake of e-mobility than under a business as usual scenario (e.g., 2015-2030 instead of 2040-2060). Subprogramme 2 fills the gap between existing initial pilots in some countries in the region (including Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay) and the full-scale deployment of e-mobility. E-mobility is not yet commercially viable and thus requires initial investment support, which is the cost of the subprogramme. The objective of Subprogramme 2 is to enable the development of electric commercial vehicles, namely buses, taxis, and urban freight vehicles together with the required charging infrastructure and grid upgrades. All private use vehicles will be financed.

(e) **Specifically, the proposed project intervention will be implemented in Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay and has the following components:**

(i) **Component 1**: Establishment of an e-mobility centre of expertise by Green Climate Fund TA to create a sustainable enabling environment for the deployment of e-buses in local (project design, business model design and development, city E-PV systems, training, etc.). This will be achieved through the creation of a knowledge hub with a set of national enabling policies for EV deployment, advice on battery reuse, recycling and disposal, as well as access to a range of tools (e.g. building, knowledge materials, outreach and dissemination, programme monitoring).

(ii) **Component 2**: Deployment of large-scale e-bus fleets (major investment component) coupled with the development of an enabling environment for separation of asset ownership and operations, modernizing and increasing the attractiveness of the public transport sector while also making it financially more efficient and sustainable;


---
**Page 134**

### Component 3: Deployment of electric light commercial vehicles (LCV) [public and private]

13 Sustainable development potential

The programme is well-aligned with Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) and aims to reduce GHG emissions. It will contribute significantly to SDG 3 (good health and well-being), SDG 7 (affordable and clean energy), SDG 8 (industry, innovation and infrastructure), SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities), and SDG 13 (climate action).

Major environmental co-benefits from this programme include reduced noise emissions and reduced air pollution. The major concern for air pollution in the country covered by the programme facilities is particulate matter 2.5 (PM2.5) and NOx emissions. Using a conservative approach (the EU GWP of PM2.5 = 100), the reduction in PM2.5 will be 3,800 tCO2e/yr. The reduction in NOx is 460 tCO2e/yr.

The programme also contributes to SDG 11 (sustainable cities and communities) by reducing air pollution from road transport. This is particularly important as the elderly are more vulnerable to the effects of poor air quality than men. Therefore, replacing diesel-fueled buses with buses with a higher fuel efficiency will deliver significant impact on air quality and health of people.

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

29 The need for an EV dominated transport sector is recognized by all three countries involved in the programme For example, Paraguay has commenced work on defining the potential of a pilot EV programme in the country even before subprogramme 2 was launched and currently has a national strategy for electric vehicles that exists prior to the successful launch of the E-motion project in the country One such example is the need to financially support the incremental cost difference between the cost of the diesel bus and the EV bus

30 A market study (annex 2), which was a part of the front-end work carried out for the organization, has identified several barriers that are hindering and will hinder future EV uptake in the three beneficiary countries (Panama Paraguay and Uruguay):

- High investment costs The key limiting barrier to upscaling EVs is the higher initial capital cost of EVs
- Limited supply of EVs Besides high initial costs for the purchase of EVs limited supply in the national market is another significant barrier
- Lack of effective incentive mechanisms Existing incentives are limited to the stipulation that the government will provide a 50% discount on the electricity used by the buyer hence, the incentive is blurred
- Limited charging infrastructure There is limited pollution control of vehicles in circulation including old secondhand combustion engine operated ones
- Lack of credit facilities Contrary to conventional vehicles there are no available credit facilities for EVs and this has been a major barrier to EV adoption among consumers
- Insufficient charging infrastructure With respect to charging infrastructure, there exist several identified drawbacks such as the lack of easy-to-install chargers at home and in workplace charging centres as well as the lack of public part investment in chargers among others;
- Lack of qualified labour and technical expertise Due to the scarce market for EVs and the insufficient charging infrastructure installed at the national level there is a lack of qualified labour and technical expertise in e-mobility


---
**Page 138**
---

**(b)** Inappropriate public transport subsidies: Public transport subsidies specifically aim at compensating costs of fossil fuels for public passenger buses and inappropriate bus system design: The existing bus system needs to be strengthened and modernized. The modernization should deliver clean and comfortable carbon-free public transport services, with a focus on electric buses, hybrid vehicles, and motorcycles in specific areas and routes. This will catalyse modal shifts.

The design of subprogramme 2 component models developed to eliminate these barriers is not appropriate for the three countries involved in this programme to meet their needs for the transportation system desired.

### Verification of financial sustainability

Verification of financial sustainability was conducted with respect to the three countries in this programme to meet their needs for the transportation system desired. A viable commercial market must have investors that are able to raise the CAPEX for this new e-mobility system (rolling stock and, where necessary, charging station costs). CAP carried out a feasibility study on the financial sustainability of electric vehicles in three ranges and countries (see annex 2 of the FP submitted to the GCF Secretariat). The GCF study also evaluated the commercial attractiveness of EVs versus fossil fuelled vehicles. Two of the results of the study showed that:

(a) The big incremental gap between the cost of the fossil fuel options and the EV option will require a financier who is able to raise this fund from the money market at very good fund rates; and

(b) A viable commercial market can be created if the ownership of the assets is separated from the operator.

The structure of the business model that is built into the operationalization of subprogramme 2, which requires that the CAPEX for the new e-mobility system when introduced, will succeed. The operators of the bus/ public bus system do not have the financial capabilities to fund the CAPEX of the EV alternative; nor do they have the wherewithal to obtain financing at very good rates from the money market. This is a major barrier enough to ensure that the three countries are able to have the kind of infrastructure that will be delivered under this programme.

The intervention will deliver a product that meets the need of the three nations covered.

34. The programme as conceptualized and presented in the FP is seeking seed trust and grant funding for the CAPEX of the new e-mobility system, which has an interest rate of 0.75 per cent annum. This will have a positive impact on the CAPEX of the programme and ensure that the CAPEX of the new e-mobility system is not being replaced by the old fossil fuelled system.

35. The independent TAP concluded that if subprogramme 2 is not implemented as submitted in the FP, many, if not all, of the barriers to the implementation of a successful and robust EV programme will remain in place and will be very difficult to overcome. The independent TAP therefore concluded that this programme will highly meet the needs of the recipients in the three countries targeted.

# Key actors in the e-mobility sector to ensure that the FP responds to the countries' needs (see annex 7). The interaction was at two levels: At the first level, an E-motion presentation was made to technical entities within public entities directly related to e-mobility in each country, typically national transport authorities, national energy companies, and national electric vehicle companies in charge of electric energy, and municipalities of the main cities. During this first interaction, discussion was centered on identifying synergies with current policies of each country and how these could be leveraged by the programme to help E-motion to adapt E-motion to the reality of each country to achieve the greatest impact.

The second level of interaction was at the entity selection stage of the subprogramme. This first round of interaction with public entities associated with county-level decisions on the transport sector was followed by regular interactions with these public sector stakeholders to update them about progress in the programme and to seek their feedback. During this phase, interviews were conducted as part of the market study framework. Thereafter, stakeholder mapping was used to identify key decision-makers at each level who could influence or enhance the success of the programme. The following entity classification was arrived at after this mapping:

(a) NDA: obtaining the letter of no objection from the NDA of the country;
(b) E-motion entity segment: national entities that are connected to or identify with one or more vehicle segments (e-bus, e-taxi, e-LCV, electric public fleet);
(c) Charging infrastructure: actors linked to all charging infrastructures that are part of the value chain or require support for the implementation of their e-mobility project;
(d) Financial role: associated with actors who already provide financing for e-mobility or the establishment of charging infrastructure;
(e) Batteries: actors linked to the battery value chain; and
(f) Capacity-building: actors linked to creating capacities in e-mobility subjects.

The six attributes listed above were further broken into the following nine attributes that was used as a decision-making tool for selecting entities. These nine attributes resulted in identifying critical issues that must be built into the operational model of subprogramme 2. The nine decision attributes include:

- NDA;
- e-bus;
- e-taxi;
- e-LCV;
- Public Fleet;
- Charging infrastructure;
- Financial role;
- Battery management; and
- EV capacity-building.

It is important to note at this point that none of the nine attributes above are focused on how the transport sector policies and regulations will evolve over the years and how it will impact current entities. At a high level, these nine attributes were used as decision criteria for two levels: the nine attributes were used in selecting entities that were considered in the market study and for the future implementation stage of the programme (useful information for CAI). The classification above was utilized to map important national entities from each of the three

#### Operations have been adopting EV mainly because the government has made the effort to implement gas subsidies. However, without incentives no EV investment would be made by operators and there are still the main barrier to e-mobility adoption:

* Common issues that were quite expressed as being the main barriers to e-mobility adoption:
    * EV CAPEX is too high;
    * EV supply is restricted, and more models/range (km) are required;
    * Electricity is perceived as expensive and requires incentives as well;
    * There is a lack of public charging infrastructure;
    * There is a need for transport authorities to be more involved and take the lead in the alignment with the national strategy towards EV goals; and
    * There is a need for incentives, and regulation is key for banks to work with several transportation companies.

# Efficiency and effectiveness

## Financial instruments

The private sector debt and grant financing from GCF that will be complemented by co-financing from CAF (debt), equity financing from other private and public sources, and in-kind contributions from CAF and GIZ. The funds will be used to implement e-box infrastructure for electric vehicles, including charging stations, as well as the introduction of dual-fuelled buses in its baseline public transport system. The investment programme will consist of:

- Electric commercial vehicles with highly intensive use, namely buses, light commercial vehicle fleets, public fleets and urban freight vehicles;
- Investments in the e-infrastructure to support the routes of these public vehicles;
- Upgrades of existing electricity grids that will be necessary to the charging infrastructure.
- No private passenger vehicles will be funded in the programme.

Investments in the E-motion programmes in the three countries will be via a new business model and service delivery structures, which have been designed to enable the introduction of electric buses. This is expected to reduce or even eliminate existing constraints in their workforce and ridership through the implementation of new business models, such as ownership splitting, that may transform the transport sector.

#### (a) Over 29.2 per cent of the total funding for the project is requested from GCF as a senior loan ($68.8 million) while about 3.3 per cent of the total ($7.5 million) is requested as grant funding. The senior loan will be provided on terms of a 15-year tenor, 0.25 per annual interest rate and 0.75 per cent annual interest rate. The requested grant funding ($7.5 million), about 3.3 per cent of the total, is requested to cover the cost of implementation of component 1, namely TA to create the policy and business framework for the deployment of EVs in the three countries covered by this project. A portion of the grant funding has been earmarked to mitigate many of the earlier identified barriers to the penetration of EVs in the three countries covered.

#### (b) Financial metrics

**(a)** The effectiveness of the concessional loan from GCF can be measured by the fact that every USD 1 from GCF will crowd-in about USD 1.8 of co-financing. The co-financing that will be leveraged through this project is estimated to amount to USD 22 million, which has a 15-year tenor, 3-year grace period and part-pass interest.

**(b)** Although not used to calculate the efficiency metrics of this programme, it is important to note that the total co-financing for this project will also come from public and private sources and from CAF/GIZ sources.

#### (c) Financial viability and best practices:

**(a)** The financial model for financing beyond GCF support has been designed around the proper implementation of the outputs/activities for a building a parallel initiative to deliver an e-mobility programme in Panama, Paraguay and Uruguay – the effectiveness and efficiency of this proposed project will score very well. However, there are two major risks that need to be mitigated: the financial viability and the effectiveness and efficiency.

**(b)** Until identified weaknesses are mitigated and a better project architecture is put in place, the independent TAP will score the effectiveness and efficiency metrics as medium.

#### (d) The risks of the programme can be summarized as follows:

**(a)** The first risk project that it the eventual GHG ER delivered by the project would not be so high as to justify the cost of implementation. This is based on the fact that the FP is based on the pipeline of projects that may not be implemented. Actual TAP will depend on which projects are implemented and how they are implemented. The second risk is that the mitigation impact will be implemented is not certain, the independent TAP rated the mitigation impact of this programme as medium. This is because the project is designed to deliver an e-mobility programme and the actual GHG ER mitigation carried out actually in each country to yield real measurable ER. If this is not done or if programme implementation, the mitigation impact metrics can be scored as high.

**(b)** The country ownership metrics were also scored as medium to high because there was no evidence that relevant government agencies were engaged beyond interviewing

# Summary

In Latin America, including Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay, the gender equality agenda has gained strength from converging national agendas at every scale, particularly with regard to women's movements and the inclusion of gender-discriminatory laws in inclusive CEDAW principles in national legislation. The reality, however, is that despite new policy frameworks, the implementation and real impact on women’s integration, especially into the labour market and key decision-making positions, is weak.

In all 3 countries, the female labour force participation as a share of the total female population at working age is between 53-60% in 2019, which, respectively, is about 20% lower compared to men. Also, occupational segregation remains high with women concentrated in low-skilled jobs and in less paid jobs and informality. Despite many laws, gender wage gaps persist across sectors and gender ratio inequalities are still present. Women also bear the brunt of unpaid care work, often having more responsibilities within households such as childcare while in Paraguay the proportion of women in leadership positions is very low with a representation of 15% compared to men holding equal gender representation. Both countries reach a share of around 35%. Regarding the representation of women in political seats at the national and local level, all 3 countries reach poor results. Among the three countries, Paraguay holds the lowest level with only 20% of seats held by women in parliament. Among the 3 countries, Paraguay reaches the lowest level with 15% of elected seats held by women in deliberative bodies of local government. In Panama, women hold 46% of seats and in Uruguay this figure is very low from 10% in Panama to 26% in Uruguay of elected seats held by women in deliberative bodies of local government.

Concerning violence against women, 2.8% of ever-partnered women and men aged between the age of 15-49 have been subjected to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months in Uruguay. This number is comparable yearly, as in Paraguay 8% and in Panama it is 3%. In all three countries, women are more likely than men to be subjected to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months.

The outlook to both keeping the progress and continuing reducing the gaps in gender equality is challenging after the COVID-19 pandemic. UN Women and other international organizations fear that recent gains made on women’s rights before the COVID-19 pandemic has worsened women's situation and has aggravated differences amongst groups.

Specific actions to guarantee that women feel safe when using public EV transportation, as workers and as users, are being implemented in Paraguay. These measures are frequent and include gender-based violence in the transport sector, and the COVID-19 pandemic is increasing the number of incidents. The urban transit system represents an environment where harassment and sexual abuses occur frequently. Women face a high risk of harassment when using public EV transportation such as on the taxis, while waiting, walking to and from the stops or using charging stations. Incidents increase at night in the region. In order to address this issue, the Ministry of Transport has implemented several measures not only benefit women but at the users. The recent study “Ella se mueve segura” (She moves safe) represents a timely and adequate guide from the region that will be used by the program in Paraguay. This study is based on the experience of other countries, such as Chile where initiatives such as "Bajito así que" in Quito, will be shared within the training. This initiative stands out for being an example of how to address gender-based violence and sexual harassment faced by the poor. It has been successful in reducing harassment in the buses in Quito, which in the end, can change attitudes towards women’s safety on public transport. The program also includes communication campaigns to raise awareness and prevent sexual harassment in the public transport sector.


2


---
**Page 150**
---

Women are not represented in the jobs generated by the transport sector in the region. The pandemic complicates the return to work for those women who lost their jobs and those whose care responsibilities have increased. The E-Motion Programme can be an opportunity for women in the programme to obtain professional driving licences, which will enable them to access employment loans. For instance, the public sector projects will help women obtain professional driving licenses to be able to opt for the direct jobs that the e-buses, e-taxis and e-vans will create. This can be done via scholarships or women-specific programmes on professional driving.

The Gender Action Plan translates the recommendations presented in this report are gender responsive and transformative, and translating into specific actions included in the E-Motion Programme, national strategies, and other relevant policies. The plan outlines a strategy for measuring, monitoring, and adjusting activities when needed. Finally, this report outlines that capacity building is crucial to learn, share, and improve transport systems and recommends including an activity that supports the collection of sex-disaggregated data for operating e-buses and e-taxis.

1. Introduction

This Gender Assessment Report refers to sub-programme 3 of II Modular: E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transport (hereinafter referred to as "the Programme"), which includes the countries Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay under the accredited entity Corporación Andina de Fomento (CAF) – Development Bank of Latin America). The prevailing report was created in cooperation with AFD and grúter consulting.

This document is well as the Gender Action Plan for the Program forms part of GEF's requirements for all Funding Proposals.

As expressed by the United Nations Sustainable Development Goal 5, achieving gender equality and the empowerment of women is an important goal in itself and also cuts across the rest of the SDGs. However, progress towards these goals has been slow or even reversed in many countries, often falling off track by the socioeconomic fallout of the pandemic. Furthermore, the Green Climate Fund (GCF) acknowledges that climate change exacerbates existing gender inequalities and can further widen inequalities. CAF as well on the other hand promotes sustainable development and regional integration and in order to contribute to a more inclusive development, through the gender approach. The Programme is designed to address these challenges by promoting the empowerment of all people, especially women from their diversity, and those who face obstacles to benefit from the fruits of development with equal opportunities.

Due to an interplay between factors such as lower incomes, and lesser livelihood opportunities, less access and control over land and other productive assets, fewer legal rights, lesser mobility and lesser political and professional representation, women inherit a greater vulnerability to climate change. Women are also more likely than men to be the primary caregivers of children and part of the solution to climate change, thus need to be equally included in decisions and discussions.

Regarding daily mobile patterns, women are more likely to rely on public transportation than men but they often face challenges such as overcrowding, lack of safety, and limited access. Women are also faced with an only commuter university – male model and do not take into account trip-chaining and the different patterns and needs of women and other vulnerable groups. (b) Sexual harassment and other forms of violence against women in public transport is a serious problem that must be tackled.

In addition, women are not represented in the jobs generated by the construction and transport sector in the region. A fact that could be considered central as the pandemic complicates the return to work for those women who lost their jobs and those whose care responsibilities have increased.

To ensure that current public transport ridership levels are sustained or even increased as part of the transition towards a more sustainable model, as a paradigm change, the gender and other socially constructed disparities must be tackled.

In general, for the Latin American and the Caribbean region, Gender Based Violence (GBV) is endemic and underreported, and before 2006 no country in LAC established femicide as a crime, while by 2015 16 countries had done so. The most recent data shows that Brazil has taken action against GBV and Argentina, Chile, Mexico, Peru, and Uruguay have backed up the motto "No una ni menos" ("Not one less").


---
**Page 152**
---

country, and careers in STEM (Science, Technology, Engineering and Maths) remain predominantly masculine. Occupational segregation persists across countries and women’s career concentrates in low paid jobs and informality, which moreover, have little and low benefits, resulting in lower earnings than men on average. Despite many laws, gender wage gaps persist across sectors and gender roles impede women's access to decent economic opportunities. Women's unpaid care responsibilities are mostly in their hands.

Education in girls is affected by adolescent marriage, which is still high and despite the downward regional trend in adolescent fertility rates, it is only exceeded by Sub-Saharan Africa. Marriage and sex are illegal for minors in all these countries; yet teenage pregnancies and minors living in union occur. Plans, strategies and policies (Ministries or agencies) have been given more power or have been recently created. These plans are not funded sufficiently to carry out reforms, and follow up with all the gender equality agenda.

Governments in the region of Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) have made significant progress on the legal framework for women's rights over the last two decades bringing significant advances in Latin American countries. All of the countries in this region ratified the Inter-American Convention on the Prevention, Punishment, and Eradication of Violence against Women (Convention of Belém do Pará). In 1995, they also signed the Convention on the Elimination of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Convention to Combat Discrimination Against Women in all its Forms (Convention on Women (CM)) of the Organization of American States, whose main achievement was to incorporate gender equality into their constitutions. Since then, and especially after their ratification in 1994, countries have been advancing to prevent, sanction, and eradicate violence against women. The Inter-American Commission for the Prevention of Violence Against Women (CICAVELAC) was established to ensure the State parties’ compliance with the Convention.

According to the Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean, guaranteeing sexual and reproductive rights and promoting sexual and reproductive health have been integral components in the region’s development agenda. The ECLAC has been working closely with Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC), 2012). Specifically, since 2003, this has been reflected in the agreements made by the countries present at each of the meetings of the Regional Conference on Women in Latin America and the Caribbean.

For the E Motion Program, the contribution to reducing the gender gap in e-mobility and low-carbon transportation and to preventing and minimizing the harm of lacking gender equality and women’s rights in transport is twofold. First, it will contribute to the reduction of the gender gap by raising awareness on sexual harassment and violence against women and girls, by providing visibility to women in decision-making positions, and by promoting the inclusion of women in the “gender mobility of care” when designing transport operations, a societal impact can be achieved. Second, this new e-transport paravolter will lower women’s access to green jobs in traditionally sex-segregated sectors such as public transportation. The E Motion Program will provide training on the capacity building, training, and sharing of know-how for women and men designing the investment plans in their projects with a focus on gender equality and women empowerment.

This gender assessment report provides information about gender equality and women empowerment in Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay. It aims at presenting the key gender gaps and opportunities in the e-mobility sector in the 3 targeted countries as well as providing recommendations for the implementation of the Gender Action Plan (GAP). The Gender Assessment Action Plan (GAAP). The report is organized into three relevant sections. The following section covers the most relevant data and analysis found in all four country studies on gender or data sources from

1) The mobility of care refers to travel implications of daily tasks performed by individuals with care responsibilities, which are mostly performed by women. For more information see (de MeloMangia, 2021)

### Gender equality by country

#### Latin America region

Countries in the region of Latin America and the Caribbean (LAC) show large discrepancies regarding political will and policy enforcement concerning gender equality. The region has progressively improved its performance on violence against women, including domestic violence, harassment, rape and honor crimes. Like all countries of the region, Panama, Paraguay, and Uruguay have ratified the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action (BPfA) in 1995, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW), and the Inter-American Convention to Prevent and Punish Violence against Women. In addition, 35 countries in the region achieved very low to low levels of gender-based discrimination in social institutions and practices. The LAC region has a high score of 28 on the Gender Inequality Index (GII). Panama accomplished a low SIG score of 22.2—the SIG level ranges from 0 to 100, with 0 indicating no discriminatory social institution or practice and 100 indicating complete discrimination. The medium score of 32.8, while for Panama no score could be indicated as the country lacks sufficient data.

Despite increasing political commitments, the implementation of ambiguous laws and regulations remains uneven. Weak enforcement by governments, discriminatory social institutions, formal and informal barriers to women's education and training, lack of access to credit, and gender empowerment and restrict their access to opportunities and rights in the region. Opinions and behaviors that influence discrimination against women are deeply rooted in society and lead to a division and discrimination in the household, leading for example to a traditional distribution of household chores between women and men. The lockdown and mobility restrictions, as responses to the prevalent COVID-19 pandemic, have exacerbated existing gender inequalities. In particular, women and girls across LAC countries experience some form of discrimination every day, including violence. The LAC region continues to have the highest female rates in the world. Various types of discriminatory practices women's involvement in decision-making in the private and public spheres.

#### Panama

Panama has been one of the fastest-growing economies in the world, with an average annual growth rate of 4.4%. Panamanian growth in 2020 has suffered a contraction, due to COVID-19’s impact, which has affected all sectors including health, education, and transportation. A recovery is expected during 2021 by trade improvement, reopening of main

[1] (OECD, 2020)


---
**Page 154**
---

services such as transport, logistics, as well as construction and mining sectors. Social inequities remain high, especially when compared to countries of similar income. Progress in poverty reduction has been significant in recent years, however, important gaps remain.1

According to the Global Gender Gap Index, Panama scored 0.737, this means that women are 26.7% less likely than men to have equal opportunities. In 2021, Panama ranked 44th out of 153 surveyed countries.

According to UN Women, in Panama 39% of legal frameworks that promote, enforce, and monitor gender equality, with a focus on violence against women, are in place. Frameworks that focus on employment and economic inclusion are only achieved by 63.3% and 27.7% of frameworks that focus on marriage and family.

As of February 2019, there are only 18.3% of parliamentary seats held by women in the national parliament, while only 10% of seats in local governments are held by women. In Panama, 44.8% of indicators show a gender gap in access to education and 57.2% in access to health services in rural areas. The maternal mortality ratio is 35 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2017, according to the UN Maternal Mortality Estimation Inter-Agency Group (MMEIG).2 The ratio is relatively low compared to LAC countries with a similar level of development. However, the data are not harmonized and suggests that indigenous women and women in rural areas face higher rates of maternal mortality.3 In 2019, Panama had one of the highest adolescent birth rates in Latin America, with an adolescent birth rate was 78.6 per 1000 women aged 15-19 as of 2016, coming down from 84.1 per 1000 women aged 15–19 in 2013.4 In Panama, 35% of women aged 15 to 49 years old have experienced physical or sexual violence by an intimate partner at some point during their lives.5 According to the most recent data from UN ECLAC, 20 women died of gender violence, of which 6 were killed by their intimate partner or former partner in 2018 (3 per 100,000 women).6 14.1% of women were victims of sexual violence during pregnancy and childbirth.7 Women with lower levels of secondary education. However, it should be mentioned that data are scarce, data collection presents challenges, and the quality of data is poor.8 The lack of harmonization in the collection and processing of data, the protocols differ, variables are not harmonized, and victims lack confidence and economic autonomy, have mistrust in authorities, or are unfamiliar with the processes. In Panama, only 50% of women who experienced violence sought help from a health professional, police, or other public institution, most turned to family or friends.9 The COVID-19 pandemic has significantly worsened the situation of violence against women and girls.10 According to UN Women, in Latin America and the Caribbean, cases against women helplines were much higher than in 2019 with an instant increase after the start of the lockdown.

Data indicate that school attendance of girls is significantly influenced by water and sanitation services, as girls have higher needs for sanitation because of menstruation. In schools with inadequate sanitation, girls in sixth grade were 6 to 10 percentage points more likely to have missed at least one day of school than boys. Girls are also less likely to be enrolled in secondary education if they are in third grade. However, in general, most indicators suggest that girls and women in Central America do not face disproportionate barriers in accessing education. According to a report by the World Bank Group (2018), 95% of girls aged between 6 and 17 years old were enrolled in primary school; 43% in secondary (72% vs 67%) and tertiary education (58% vs 37%), or much higher for females than for males. Women are also more likely to be employed in low productivity sectors, such as agriculture, compared with men in the technology society and are significantly less numerous in scientific areas, linked to technology, computing, and engineering. They are also on positions of decision making or high hierarchy and recognition in science technology and innovation.

Access to basic services is not universal and remains linked to factors such as geographic location, education levels, ethnicity and income levels of households. In Panama, 57% of women participate in the labor force compared with 64% for men. The gender gap in employment is highest among young adults being close to parity (15-24), the rate drops to 34% for female and 58% for male data refer to 2017). In the job market in Panama, and LAC in general, is characterised by structural unemployment, high structural underemployment, and informal employment among women households. There is a distinction between employment in high and low productivity sectors. High productivity sectors are associated with higher levels of education, better working conditions, and higher wages. Low productivity sectors are linked to workers with lower incomes, less level of education, instability of work, and poor working conditions. Women are more likely than men to be employed in the workforce work in the low productivity sector, including agriculture, commerce, and services, compared to 54% of all men in the labour force that work in this sector (27). Nevertheless, in very high segments of the economy, women have a higher participation rate. Women are more likely than men to be in the labour force, however, participation rates have increased since the early 2000s. When women join the workforce, they tend to enter low productivity sectors and remain there. Furthermore, particularly young women have weak job prospects. Furthermore, more than one in four employed women work part-time (19% of all female workers). In comparison with other Latin American countries, the gender income gap in Panama was the lowest in Latin America in 2010 at 58%.

According to a World Bank report, the public sector is an important source for jobs for women, for example, public sector employment accounts for more wage employment among women than men. Also, in terms of education and training, the public sector is the most important provider of this indicator that also the M Programme can enhance employment opportunities for Women in Panama.

According to the Global Gender Gap Index, Paraguay scored 0.302, this means that women are 28.8% less likely than men to have equal opportunities. In 2021, Paraguay ranked 68th out of 153 surveyed countries.[1]

According to UN Women,[2] in Paraguay 91.7% of legal frameworks that promote, enforce, and monitor gender equality, with a focus on violence against women, are in place. Frameworks that focus on employment opportunities are achieved by 100%, and 83.8% of frameworks that focus on marriage and family are in place.[2]

As of February 2019, there are only 15% of parliament seats held by women in the national parliament (Chamber of Deputies). Although the National Congress has a quota for women and has collaborated through participation quotas, these do not guarantee equal representation. Nevertheless, women's participation has been increasing. Female politicians and indigenous women established the Women's Caucus in 2018 to promote gender equality.[3] According to UN Women,[4] in Paraguay, 42.8% of indicators needed to monitor the SDGs from a gender perspective are available, with gaps in key areas such as violence against women.

In Paraguay, the maternal mortality ratio[5] is 129 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2017, according to the UN Maternal Mortality Estimation Inter-agency Group (MMEIG). The ratio is high compared to LAC countries but below the world average of 211 deaths per 100,000 live births.[6] 23.6% of women aged 20–49 in Paraguay were married before age 18 and 57.5% had their first child by age 20. The figure for women aged 15-19 as of 2016, coming from 61.8 per 1000 women aged 15–19 in 2008.[7] This figure is higher than the global average and the regional average (43 deaths per 100,000 live births). Moreover, Abortion is the third most frequent cause of maternal deaths and maternal and infant mortality rates in Paraguay are among the highest in Latin America.

According to the most recent data from UN ECLAC,[8] 37 women died of all gender violence, of which 34 were killed. Of these victims, 20 (54%) were between 15–19 years old and 16 (41.0 per 100,000 women). 8% of women aged 15-49 years reported that they had been subject to physical and/or sexual violence by a current or former partner in the previous year.[9] The data are from 2017. Despite the fact that data are scarce, data collection presents challenges and numbers are often underestimated. Despite having various institutions collecting data, the protocols differ, variables are not harmonized, and victims lack access to services and information on their rights. In addition, there is a lack of coordination between the processes. According to an IDB report,[10] there is generally limited access to services and a decent quality of life for women in Paraguay.[11]

Regarding literacy, most indicators suggest that girls and women do not face disproportionate barriers in accessing education. According to the World Economic Forum, girls seem to outperform boys in many indicators. For instance, in 2020 the enrolment rate in primary and secondary education for girls was higher than for boys in all regions except Sub-Saharan Africa. In rural areas, the proportion between girls and boys enrolled in basic and secondary education is 89 girls per 100 boys. Moreover, the enrolment rate in tertiary education for women is much higher than the rate for men in most countries. However, the quality of education received by girls remains low. Nevertheless, it is estimated that 40% of investments in female education are not capitalized upon by labour force participation. In Paraguay, for example, only 35.2% of women aged 15-64 years old work. Meanwhile, 40% of the indigenous population is illiterate, in comparison to 5.4% of the non-indigenous population.

In Paraguay, 85.5% of women participate in the labour force, compared to 97.1% of men, i.e., the ratio of women to men is 0.89 and far from being close to parity. For younger adults (15-24), the rate drops to 43% for female and 69% for male (data refer to 2017). Although labour participation of women in Paraguay has increased over time, it is still below that of men. This does not represent entry but could even promote gender equality but also result in large economic results. Estimates suggest that a higher labour force participation of those women who completed some level of education would have a positive impact on the economy and social development. The main reason for the sexual division of labour to support their economic activity. According to UN Womenwatch, the majority of women are engaged in informal activities such as small-scale agriculture, domestic service and unpaid family work, which translates into vulnerable and precarious employment, no access to social protection or social security systems. In 2016, 45% of women were economically active but not employed. Of these, 38.7% reported that lack of education was the main reason for their economic inactivity. Meanwhile, 39.5% of women reported housekeeping as their main reason for being outside the labour force, while men spend most of their weekly hours at their main occupation and only 16.3 % of their time on household work. The same year, 40.7% of women aged 15-24 were economically inactive or in labour inactivity. Also, women and girls aged 15+ spend 14.5% of their time on unpaid care and domestic work compared to men who spend only 3%. Figure 1 displays the reasons for labour inactivity for women and men in Paraguay for the year 2016.

Among those participating in the labour force, women face only marginally higher unemployment and informality rates. However, women face significant wage discrimination, as reflected in the large male-to-female earnings gap. In Paraguay, for example, the gender pay gap is 43%. When restricting the sample to full-time workers the gap goes down to 27%, still a high gender earnings gap relative to payments. In comparison to other LAC countries, the gender income gap in Paraguay is slightly below average. Women are concentrated in low-productivity sectors of the labour market, after retail, many entered government jobs (relatively high productivity) followed by financial services and construction. The largest share of women’s employment is in agriculture. For the labour market, nearly a fifth of new jobs for women were in other services, the least productive sector. According to the World Bank, women are more likely than men to be employed in informal sectors; for example, public sector wages and working conditions are usually better for women. This can be an indicator that also the Misiones programmes can enhance employment opportunities for women in Paraguay.

In the UNDP’s Human Development Gender Inequality Index, Paraguay ranks 4th out of 22 countries in Latin America and the Caribbean. Its performance is related to high maternal mortality rates and a low share of women in parliament.

### Uruguay

Uruguay is positioned among the first places in the region in relation to various well-being indexes. Institutional stability and low levels of corruption are reflected in the high level of public trust in government, according to the Human Opportunity Index. Uruguay has managed to attain a high level of equal opportunities in terms of access to basic services such as education, water flow, electricity and sanitation.

Gender inequality is still an issue and seems to be a difficult problem to solve, even though the country has made some improvements over the last five years. The country has a value of a 0.782 in the Global Gender Gap Index, ranking 85<sup>th</sup> place out of 153 surveyed countries.<sup>46</sup>

In Uruguay, the maternal mortality ratio<sup>47</sup> is 17 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2012, according to the UN Maternal Mortality Estimation Inter-agency Group (MMIG).<sup>48</sup> The ratio is very low compared to Latin America and the Caribbean region where it ranges from 53.7 deaths per 100,000 live births in 2016.<sup>49</sup>

- 24.8% of women aged 20–24 years old were married or in union before age 18.
- The additional birth rate was 35.7 per 1,000 women aged 15 to 19, down from 116 for every 1,000 women aged 15–19 in 2017.<sup>47</sup>

According to the most recent data from UN ECOSOC:

- 25 women died of gender violence, of which 22 were killed by their intimate partner or former partner in 2019 (3.2 per 100,000 women).<sup>50</sup>
- Also, in 2019, 74% of women reported having experienced sexual violence by a current or former intimate partner in the previous 12 months.<sup>51</sup>

Recent publication data reveal that the prevalence of lifetime experience with physical and/or sexual violence in all areas is as high as 77%. More specifically, nearly 3 in 2 women have experienced some form of violence from their partner or ex-partner throughout their lives. The most common forms of violence are verbal abuse (95%), threats to life (84%), and physical violence (61%).<sup>52</sup>

- 54.6% of women reporting having experienced some episode of violence in public places at some point during their lifetime, with the highest prevalence being in Montevideo (70%). The prevalence has also increased, from 34.2% in 2013 to 37.3% in 2019.<sup>53</sup>

It should be mentioned that data are scarce, difficult to access and not comparable across countries because various institutions collecting the data, the protocols differ, variables are not harmonized, and victims lack confidence and economic autonomy.

Regarding literacy, most indicators suggest that girls and women do not face disproportionate barriers in accessing education. According to the World Economic Forum, girls seem to outperform boys in reading and math at all levels of schooling. Girls also have a higher secondary school enrollment rate than boys for both public and private schools (95% vs 85%). Lastly, the enrolment rate in tertiary education is slightly higher among women compared with men (37% vs 26%), although this difference is more pronounced when comparing to male students who are pursuing studies in science or technology. Women also have a lower likelihood of pursuing careers in fields such as industry, construction, engineering, ICT, natural sciences, mathematics, or statistics was 12%.<sup>54</sup>

In Uruguay, 48% of women participate in the labour force compared to 53% of men, i.e., the ratio of women to men is 0.87. For younger adults (15–24), the rate drops to 37% for female and 58% for male data (refer to Table 1). Also, the proportion of 15- to 29-year-olds in the lowest income quintile who were employed was 60%, compared with only 30% among those aged 30 or older. This is true in the case of men and 43% in the case of women in 2014. This gender disparity is a result of the high proportion of young women who work doing household chores. In Uruguay, 36.7% of indicators needed to monitor the SDGs from a gender perspective are available, with gaps in key areas like “the quality and quantity of data on women’s access to education,” “gender equality frameworks.” Also, many areas such as gender and poverty, women’s access to assets including land, physical infrastructure, and financial services remain underdeveloped. There is also a need for reliable methodologies for comprehensive and periodic monitoring. In addition, the gender equality index prepared by the World Economic Forum (WEF) ranks Uruguay at 83rd place among 142 countries, one of the worst rankings in Latin America. ### Assessment of gender equality and women’s empowerment in urban transportation

According to the UN, two-thirds of the population will be living in cities by 2050, exacerbating overcrowding and increasing inequality. Urban areas together with pollution, overcrowding, lack or modality infrastructure are some of the problems that the public transport sector is examining.

While legislative structures in the Program countries are moving forward to address gender issues, other sources report lower levels, around 35%, e.g. (World Bank Group, 2019)

considering freedom of movements strongly differ between men and women when considering their agency. Agency in mobility can be defined as the ability to make and choices related to one's mobility and make full use of public transport systems. Using the agency concept under a gender lens to analyze or design urban transport systems is important because urban transport systems provide basic urban transport infrastructure and services vary between men and women. The lack of certain safety and security features in the design of the transport system can constrain a woman’s decision to choose a mode of transport, especially when she has to travel alone at night or during early morning hours, dark bus stops or overcrowded buses. When it comes to the design of the transport infrastructures, the realities of women's mobility are often overlooked and their specific needs for safety and security skills need and be taken into account when designing a transport operation. Assuming that when they serve women, they are adequate for the overall population and enhances the quality of the system.

As a consequence, lack of access to transportation and safety and security issues limits women participation in the labor market. Women's share of employment is 38% globally compared with 52.6% globally. Women turn away working opportunities depending on the traveling conditions to workstations. The gender gap in education and training is also a major factor that constrains access to education, health and jobs, the sector generates jobs itself but traditionally most opportunities are likely to remain in men's hands.

There is a myriad of links between gender and urban transportation, and these links have been deeply discussed for many years. There are gender differences in the purpose time, distance, mode of transportation, or perceptions on safety and security that affect women’s mobility agency and access to economic opportunities. The Program has conducted a gender assessment in the three countries in the region, even in the three countries included in the Program. For the purpose of organizing the information for this gender assessment, three interrelated dimensions can be distinguished: women's transport ridership, women's safety and security on public transport. Additionally, this report covers the impact of the pandemic with a gender lens in a fourth section.

Mobility in urban transport

One has to be aware that an electrification project generally impacts everyone accessing the transportation services, however impacts are often stronger on women as well as on poorer social classes. A gender analysis is therefore important when considering the impacts in terms of access of transportation means by women, children, the elderly, people with disabilities and other vulnerable collective groups. The Program has conducted a gender assessment in the three countries to assess women's ridership, hence also to increase public transport ridership levels; it is imperative to tackle gender and other socially constructed disparities.

A World Bank report ("Why Does the Metro?") reveals that in Latin American cities female urban transport users are more likely than men to travel alone from that of men. Women rely more on public transportation than men, women choose different destinations in a single trip (e.g. drop kids at school, buy food and go shopping), they often use multiple modes of transport for the same journey. They are often accompanied by others (e.g. children or elderly family members) and carry bags, strollers or purchases. Women also have to balance their time between work and home responsibilities; they combine multiple wage jobs and care responsibilities (family errands, care for the elderly, the kids, and their community) in one trip. Sometimes, the nature of the wage work (as part-time workers), combined with the need to travel long distances to reach different destinations or multiple destinations, and when a multimodal fare is not in place, they have to buy a new ticket every time or just walk more. Nevertheless, routes are designed with an only commuter university – male worker

10 World Bank Group, 2020
11 (Ibid., 2017)


---
**Page 162**
---

model, which does not take into account the different patterns and needs of women. If there is no public transportation available, they just walk or decide not to travel. Women in these countries use occasionally official taxis when travelling with heavy bags or children, but most times they use informal transport such as mototaxis. These are small motorized vehicles that are popular for women: they fill route gaps, their price is shared by passengers, they provide safety, security and comfort, and reduce stress from the trip. Some taxi drivers discriminate against women with bags, and some do not allow them to sit in the front seat (especially in countries where there is a high level of violence towards women or elderly). In Lima, women who live in the high hills feel discriminated against by moto-taxi drivers when taking them up the hill.

Figure 2 below shows the differences in the percentage of women and men in their mobility patterns (motorized trips, public transportation, and walking trips) in seven LAC countries compared to the EU27. Common mobility patterns are found in the region and they are similar to developed countries in the EU27. Women use more public transport than men for all types of trips except for walking and they do fewer motorized trips compare to men. However, when women are in higher-income groups, their share of motorized trips is higher compared with lower-income groups. This can be seen in Figure 2. Women are more susceptible to a modal change to individual modes of transportation if perceived safer or they can choose not to travel if perceived unsafe. Additionally, women can transfer their fears and concerns about safety to their children.

The growth of motorization rates in the LAC region and its negative consequences on public transportation have a direct impact on women's mobility. Women use significantly more public transportation than men for all types of trips except walking. This is due to the fact that women are more likely to be employed outside the home, which means they need reliable transport to get to work. However, there are also cultural and social factors at play here as well.

The gender gap in driving licenses by country is significant: in Costa Rica, female drivers' licenses represent only 30% of the total licenses issued for all types of vehicles. In Mexico, women hold just over half of the driving licenses (51%) compared to men who hold almost two-thirds of them (64%). The gender gap has been closing gradually since the introduction of new laws that allow women to obtain a driver's license in 2007 and have closed or have closed the gender gap among drivers[3]. In Costa Rica, driving licenses for men double those of women, but when analyzing the type of license the gender gap is even bigger for productive activities. Women hold only 15% of the total number of commercial driver's licenses in Costa Rica.

In Mexico, women own more than half (60%) of all public transportation vehicles and 82% of airport and regular taxis are owned by women [It might not reflect women taxi drivers, only ownership]. Data on freight and women have not been found.

The mobility of care is unnoticed by data collection methods. To design and calculate ridership, data collection methods focus traditionally on one-trip pattern (e.g., travels to and from work or school). The concept of "mobility of care" is not considered because it is fractured in a multitude of purposes and fall into different categories (e.g., shopping or escorting).

## The mobility of care is unnoticed by data collection methods.


---
**Page 164**

To give visibility to mobility patterns, the IDB has a Clarity Map Methodology™ which breaks them into 12 segments of a trip’s experience in Bogotá, Santiago and Medellín. Then different profiles of riders (women, elderly, people with disabilities) are assessed and the results help to design better urban transport.

Fare schemes can foster women's use of public transportation. Women have more price sensitivity than men because of their unique mobility pattern [short trips, with family members, off-peak hours] and the poor suffer the most with unregulated tariffs[1]. Urban transportation must promote a fare structure that is fair for all users. The IDB’s Clarity Map Methodology™ shows that women are more divided from men as they plan their trips to accommodate a busy agenda of care activities and are very concerned about safety and security when using public transport. Fare schemes, frequency and fare are key to their decisions. Further, because most women's trips are not related to income-generating activities, women might give up convenient modes of transportation and walk to save money[2]. In 2018, the city of Buenos Aires decided to increase transport tariff in 2018 by 35% for all users, but with a special discount for women. This measure was proposed from vulnerable riders, of which 90% are women with small kids, and specifically domestic workers.

Ride-hailing has become an alternative for women to increase their urban mobility in safer conditions than other modes. The main characteristics of ride-hailing, many times match women's unique needs. To date, there is a significant gender gap in the use of ride-hailing services, with women having lower gaps in public transportation given that public transportation mostly covers masculine trips (from home to work) and women’s trips are more related to care activities[3]. Women also have higher safety and women's perception of security segments when ride-hailing at night, especially when the supplier of the service has an app with safety and security features to monitor and share the ride and to know about the driver.

Gender-segregated ride-hailing benefits female passengers and drivers. Safety and security are the most limiting or facilitating factor for female’s agency in mobility and access to jobs and other opportunities[4]. The IDB’s Clarity Map Methodology™ shows that 20% of women riders reported that the lack of women drivers reduces the trip they would make, whereas 44% said that they would be more likely to use a ride-hailing service if they could select a woman as driver. Women are also more willing to pay for safety and security features in public transport system since women’s trips are chained because it has become a safer mode of transportation[5]. The IDB’s Clarity Map Methodology™ shows that 40% of women riders would use ride-hailing services when gender segregated transport was offered, which manifests how women can get jobs as drivers in cases when they perceive a safe environment at work.

Regarding the situation in the three target countries, a briefly daily mobility context is presented below:
