and reached 23.1%, including the reason for grocery shopping and errands that showed the highest participation with 9%. While regarding the modes of transport, a high percentage of the trips are made by public transport (50.3%) and walking (19.3%). However, it was not even possible to find public transportation characterise the gender mobility patterns in the metropolitan area of Panama. This fact highlights the importance of gathering information on transport disaggregated by gender.

For the case of Uruguay, data obtained through the Mobility Survey of the Montevideo Metropolitan Area developed in 2016 is presented. According to this survey, the main trip purpose that follows a similar pattern as in Paraguay and Panama is going to work. Considering the three trip purposes that follow, the most common are: going to work (45%), shopping and errands (38%) and going to the substance of the household; a total of four purposes constitutes the main generators of mobility in the city. Analysing the data according to gender, it is observed that there is a slight difference between men and women regarding the trip purpose for going to work: 47% of trips are made by men and 39.5% among women and 27.6% among women. There is also a gender difference in the case of mobility for shopping and errands, where 40.8% of trips are made by men and 51.5% by women; including trips for grocery shopping and errands and accompanying kids. While among women accompanying kids trips represent a 23.6% of the trips, among men this trip purpose is chose, reaching a 47%. In the case of mobility to the substance of the household, it is observed that more trips are made by women (representing a 2.29% of their total trips), among this mode purposes reach a 31% of the total of trips and 50% of these trips are related with the performance of tasks within the household between women and men than observed in other surveys in the region.

Regarding modes of transport, the survey shows a modal share of slightly less than two thirds of motorized trips (car, motorcycle, bus, etc.) and one third of active mode (walking and riding a bicycle). In this case, there is also a gender difference regarding the proportion of trips by bus; in terms of gender, women have a higher proportion of trips by bus (28%) and walking (17.6%), while among men private motorized transport is more used than public transport. This is consistent with the observed gender differences when the proportions of private motorized trips as drivers are analyzed: 30.7% among men and just 13.1% among women.

Paraguay has not published yet its first National Household Mobility Survey. In fact, during 2021 the first survey of this type was carried out for the Metropolitan Area of Asunción within the framework of a project supported by the United Nations (UN). However, in 2016, the IDB published a study which aims to characterize the mobility patterns and gender differences in the Metropolitan Area of Asunción. It included a quantitative survey that allowed characterizing the mobility of women and men in terms of trip purposes, modes of transport used and frequency of trips made daily or less than daily trips. On the other hand, 17% of women walk, 23% use a car, 4% use motorbikes, and 2% use taxis. And as for the reasons, they use public transport mainly on trips for shopping, work or health reasons.

Safety and security in urban transport

Six out of ten women reported having been harassed in public transportation in Latin American countries. In 2014, a survey in 15 out of the 20 largest cities in the world, asked 6500 women and experts in urban planning, about their perceptions when travelling in public transportation. Women's responses were compared with those of men. The results show that women traveling alone at night, the risk of being verbally or physically harassed, the likelihood that other passengers


---

would come to their assistance, the trust that authorities would investigate reports of harassment or violence, and the availability of safe public transport. Figure 5 below reveals the ranking of the most dangerous transport systems for women found by this study.

Regatta, the world's 16th largest capital, was ranked as the safest city for women to travel alone at night. More than six out of ten women in Regatta City, 46% polled online by YouTube said they had been groped or experienced physical harassment on public transport. Lima ranked 3rd highest (1% perceived sexual violence). In Rio de Janeiro, 25% of women reported being afraid of sexual assault and 80% of women report being victims of sexual harassment in Lima and Bogota, respectively. Women are more likely to be harassed by strangers than by acquaintances or friends, and many times happens traveling from and to the transport systems. In Costa Rica, 80% of women report being victims of the violent situation in public space.

Perceptions of transportation safety and security shape women's agency in mobility in such a way that they avoid paying higher fares for safer modes than face exposure to risks. In Rio de Janeiro, minivans are more expensive, their trips are more delayed, and queues are longer than buses. Women prefer to travel by bus because they feel safer on the bus than walking or riding with friends, and many times happens traveling from and to the transport systems. In Costa Rica, 80% of women report being victims of the violent situation in public space.

Adequate urban transport infrastructure can boost women's participation in the sector, both as passengers and providers. Good lighting and vigilance cameras can transform women's perception of safety and security and discourage perpetrators from committing crimes. Both simple solutions present cost-effective ways to improve women's mobility and reduce their exposure to violence inside and main stations, and recently they are installed in buses to protect both passengers and drivers. Installations, operation, and maintenance are costly and don't control harassments, slurs, aggressions. Combining security lighting and cameras can be a more effective solution.* In 2018, Buenos Aires

installed cameras and GPS in public transportation units to monitor traffic registered inside and outside the bus. These kinds of tools can potentially be used for safety and security as well. Particularly at dawn and dusk, isolated bus stops, empty or too crowded buses, poorly lighted park-and-ride stations, and other places where women feel unsafe are common. The surroundings of the transport infrastructure play a role in creating travelling stress for women. Further, measures easy to implement, such as allowing women to get off the bus between bus stops at night, can help reduce the feeling of insecurity. This is especially true when traveling alone, which can radically change their perception of safety and security at night. In Montreal and Toronto (Canada), this has been implemented.

Women adopt specific strategies, which can work or not, but make women feel safer in the public transport system. Sexual harassment in public transportation includes leering, winking, offensive gestures, unwanted touching, groping, pressing against the body, indecent exposure, and sexual assault. Women can avoid these situations by traveling with a group of friends, keeping their belongings close to them or stay near the walls, and even carry non-lethal weapons. They feel safer when traveling in a group, especially at night. Women also adopt strategies that help them avoid unwanted sexual advances, such as建立friendly relationships with drivers. In Manizales (Colombia), a medium-sized city where trips usually need only one mode of transportation, 73% of women have suffered at least one incident of harassment, sexual violence or assault in the past year. The city has implemented several measures to reduce this: women choose longer and safer routes; changed transport modes (to taxi or motorcycle) and changed the way they dress when out with others; or select a determined route with other women.

Campaigns to raise awareness about sexual harassment together with safety and security protocols to protect abuses can protect women and reduce incidents. In Quito, the 2017 campaign Bajo el Acoso (Stop Harassment) is active in the public transportation system. Videos and information campaigns in the bus stations are part of this initiative. The bus driver sends a text message to the woman when she sends a free SMS with the word "Acoso" ("Harassment") together with the bus number; then, a printed card is sent by post. This initiative has been supported by the city council and by local legal, psychological and social professionals start working on the case. The bus speakers alert the passengers about the campaign and provide them with information to report incidents of sexual harassment. Information and chooses whether to report in situ or later. Complaints and lawsuits have increased with this initiative. In February 2017, more than 2300 reports on sexual violence were received; 71 went to court, and 21 received a sentence of 5 and 8 years jail." Two years later, reporting fell 34.9%.

Sex segregation in massive urban transport is a temporary measure that increases mobility and projects the image of a city as safe for women. This has been implemented in Mexico City for decades in many cities worldwide (Brazil, Mexico, Japan, India, Indonesia, Nepal, United Arab Emirates, Thailand) and have received praises and critics. Women feel safer when using exclusive buses, but this measure is not always accepted by the public. In fact, women who use mixed- or sex-segregated modes are perceived as open to sexual advances, even though the initiative reduces incidents of harassment. The city of São Paulo (Brazil) followed Vignettes' "Segregated Women's Only" non-segregating units in its transport system and was implemented in other estates later, but the


---
**Page 168**
---

program did more than that, it located booths in transport stations to file complaints, transportation staff received training on violence against women and girls, vehicles and stations displayed prevention campaigns, and bus systems and trains had women-only cars. In 2015, Mexico DF responded by launching the “No Más” initiative as part of a comprehensive program which included emergency buttons in 90% of the Atenea buses driven to make a silent alarm call to an operator and 271 buses had cameras installed.

Mobile applications facilitate victim reporting and contribute to building trust with victims, which is informed by the quality of transport infrastructure and noise exposure on board. Digital technology helps women track and share their location with family and friends. Ride-hailing companies such as Uber and Careem have introduced features that allow users to monitor a trip and identify both the driver and the car’s plate. Passengers can leave comments after their journey and quantify a driver's professionalism, cleanliness of the vehicle, and quality of their trip. In Brazil, “Chat de Segurança” (Safety Chat) is an app that allows women to report harassment in real time and file reports of harassment. The video documented the woman’s regular interaction when walking and using public transport in Bogotá, Colombia. The city launched a study on safety and security in 2018. Bogota under the “Me mueve Seguridad” study, adapted the Indian application Safetipin to measure the index of safety and security perception at night of different routes and places by women.

Security is also related to the physical environment of transport infrastructure, such as Lighting (illuminated), Openness (sightlines), Visibility (overhanging trees, high walls etc.). Level of crowding or emptiness. Security is also related to the physical environment of transport infrastructure, such as Lighting (illuminated), Openness (sightlines), Visibility (overhanging trees, high walls etc.). Level of crowding or emptiness.

For example, in Mexico DF, women are more likely to feel unsafe when walking at night. Female participants were below 35 years old and 78 % work or study, and 50 % finished tertiary education. Age, sex, education, and poverty are variables to consider when implementing technology to protect women in the transport sector (e.g., mobile applications, cameras, lighting, signage, or internet) and user knowledge can be barriers for many women, especially the poorest.”

The transport sector can serve as a catalyst to the promotion of gender equality and inclusion. This has also been recognized by the Supreme Court of Colombia as one of the impacts of the Maria da Penha law on gender-based violence. Electronic locks, “tobemis” were installed in the Superior Rio’s suburban rail lines, which serve around 700,000 daily users. Anonymously, women sent data to a hotline that allowed them to report harassment and other forms of gender-based violence.

Sensitization and prevention campaigns on Gender-Based Violence (GBV), including Sexual Exploitation, Abuse and Sexual Harassment (SEAH), can equip public transport workers with tools to understand GBV and its impact. In Mexico DF, the “No Más” initiative included a gender-based violence campaign delivered workshops to drivers’ trade unions working in buses and “motociclistas” (drivers of private cars that operate as public transport). The workshops included topics such as gender equality, domestic-based violence in the domestic spaces and with passengers in the transport environment.

Feedback and grievance mechanisms located in the transport systems help women and girls to inform about their experiences. In Mexico DF, the “No Más” initiative included emergency buttons installed booths in some of Quito’s more crowded transport stations. Women could report acts of violence and file complaints. Staff was trained on how to attend to victims, even drivers were trained

To inform women on how and where to report incidents[1]. Capacity building and coordination among different bodies (police, judiciary system, staff) is essential to provide useful and appropriate assistance to victims. Lack of trust in authorities is common to prevent women from reporting abuses. Overall in all the targeted countries, there is a lack of local data regarding safety and security in the urban transport sector in Panama. However, as part of the contextual situation, in 2013 a national law project that originally sought to prevent, prohibit and punish street harassment, sexual harassment and other forms of violence against women in public spaces was approved by parliament[3]. That constituted an important opportunity to address the perception of insecurity in transport because it included the issue of street harassment as one of the types of violence experienced by women in public spaces, whether in parks, streets, buses stop, or on the subway.

But when the law was approved in 2018, the particular reference to the street harassment was not included arguing that street harassment is considered “a cultural and popular issue that cannot be avoided or regulated”.

Currently, the government’s and civil society organizations are working to develop campaigns to raise awareness about street harassment, showing that it is a recurring social phenomenon that implies a violation of women's human rights by restricting their freedom of mobility.

Although there is a scarce data available that allows to do a specific diagnostic of the nexus between street harassment and public transport in Panama, the official Plan developed by the Montevideo Government[4] called “Montevideo Libre de Acoso Sexual en los Espacios Publicos 2018-2023” includes a section on the issue of street harassment as an example of women’s public space experience. Using in-depth interviews, the Plan argues that street harassment also occurs in public transport and mentions it as an example that women tend to fear taxi drivers, mostly men, of experiencing sexual harassment.

Regarding the mobility of younger women, who are the ones who usually go out at night, the Plan mentions that they perceive mobility as a problem: “walking is not a possible option, buses do not respect the schedule and are very expensive, the bicycle presents risks”. For these reasons some young women avoid going out alone. Given that it is understood that mobility at night becomes a restriction for all women, with greater intensity according to intersections of gender, class, disability. These experiences reveal the daily inequalities in access to public transport services and spaces. In this sense, as part of prevention and self-care strategies, for example, plan to be accompanied at the bus stop or while walking, to use the mobile phone during the trip to pretend to be accompanied. Changes in women’s behavior that have a relevant impact on their routines to move around cities freely and autonomously.

The GB study (2018) already mentioned shows that the perception of insecurity in public transport is relatively high in Auckland Metropolitan Area: on average 6 out of 10 women feel public transport is unsafe or very unsafe, and this perception is higher among non-users of this service.

Moreover, the survey reveals that 24% of women who regularly use public transport were victims or witnesses of a crime while using transport or waiting for it at the bus station in the last 12 months. These results suggest that insecurity can further decrease the effective utilization.

Likewise, the perception of insecurity has led 35% of women to make some modification to their travel routine by changing the means of transport used.

It is also observed that around 80% of women have little or no confidence that the police will respond to the report that a woman was sexually assaulted on public transport.


## Jobs and urban transport

Around 30% of the female workforce in Latin America and the Caribbean is occupied in the care sector (a sector traditionally dominated by women), compared to only 6% of the male workforce, see Figure 5. Women are more likely than men to be employed in jobs that pay less than $12 per day, have similar educational level and demographic characteristics.

| Figure 5: Occupational Segregation in Latin America: women care, men build |
| --- |
| ![Figure 5](https://example.com/image.png) |
| Source: (Bustelo, Suarez & Volisz, 2019) |


Generally, the transport sector is a major and dominant access to education, health and economic opportunities. Investments in infrastructure are common to boost the economy and, if done right in the aftermath of the covid-19 crisis, this is a chance to enhance labour market opportunities for women and girls. The majority of jobs will be generated during the design and at the construction of the transport infrastructure, and during its operations and maintenance. In this program, most jobs will be generated during the operation and maintenance of the transport infrastructure.


---
**Page 171**
---

Following recent legislation to grant and enforce equal opportunities, most countries in the region have carried out initiatives to extend women’s employment opportunities in the transport sector. However, the current gender gap in jobs in the transport sector of the program countries is enormous. Women are underrepresented at all levels of management or administration, while men work as drivers, mechanics, or supervisors. Thus, both the number of women hired or trained and the quality of their contracts are low. Stereotypes, lack of family support, and a lack of role models also contribute to this situation. Women are often employed in sex-segregated jobs, sometimes perceived as unskilled. Reshaping women’s tasks when pregnant for their protection is not always possible due to the nature of transport work and the limited number of women in the sector. The power of labour unions can both ease or impede women's access to jobs in the sector.

Specific efforts need to be made to reach greater diversity in the sector. To bridge the job gender gap, it is important to advertise that the vacancies are open for women. Job description and terms of references will be gender sensitive; job postings will encourage women for applications and will be posted on women’s networks. The transport sector can also attract women by providing care needs at home, respect national quota regulations, building dressing rooms and provide childcare, offer scholarships to train women as incentives for the sector to attract them. Working with trade unions is essential in order to ensure that women are not discriminated against when they apply for jobs; one the train labour union La Fraternidad do not allow women to be drivers.[260]. Moreover, the Gender Equality Action Plan mentions: specific for women in order to enhance their skill set in accordance with newly created jobs.

In Chile, women drivers said that the visibility of women carrying a diversity of tasks and positions incentives other women to push into higher labour market positions. From 2012 on, in Santiago, new regulations were introduced which allowed women to obtain professional driving licenses. Women participation in the courses were supported by scholarships from the Ministry of Labour and Social Development. The courses are free for women to obtain professional driving licenses. Women participation in the courses were supported by scholarships from the Ministry of Labour and Social Development. The courses are free for women to obtain professional driving licenses. The price of professional licences is a barrier as well for women willing to act as drivers. Moreover, women drivers said that they have more time to leave their jobs or can’t leave their family responsibilities. Women drivers in Santiago were perceived as more compromised with the job, more responsible, have less sinisterness than men, or are more careful and attentive when driving. They also said that they are more kind, empathic, calm; they treat the passengers better and resolved conflict situations without escalating problems. When women started working, many colleagues changed their behaviours and became more respectful.

A growing body of evidence shows how companies can benefit from investing in women as employees, entrepreneurs, customers, and community partners. Such investments benefit companies by increasing innovation, improving customer service, and providing an opportunity to transform local and global markets.[261] Even though the data is scarce,[262], women drivers tend to be more attentive than men when driving and this can lead to a reduction in traffic accidents that can benefit transport operators when hiring women drivers.

For example, in Mexico DF, in 2008, the program Atenea was created to reduce route traffic to and from schools by hiring women bus drivers, who would offer more confidence to parents in the school routes. In 2017 there were 156 buses for the program, and 75 drivers were women. In the state of [263] (2019) [264] (2019) [265] (DEC, 2017) [266] World Health Organization Factsheet, 2002. **Women, Jobs and Covid-19**

*Women’s labour force participation[23] has been steadily rising in the last decade for each of the countries until the pandemic. The devastating impact of COVID-19 has affected disproportionately women's labour force participation. According to the UN Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC), 50% of women lost their jobs during the first months of the pandemic, many of them have been pushed out of the market, but that figure rises to 22.6% if assuming the same level of employment as in February 2020. In addition, it is estimated that women have been pushed out on the economically active population, being forced to fully commit to caring activities.*

[23] López Marmolejo, Ruiz-Arroyo (2021)

[24] Cepeda, A., Hargreaves, S., Rubenstein da Silva, E. (2018)

[25] Cabral, M. (2020)

*The labour force participation rate is a measure of the proportion of a country’s working-age population that reports having worked in the previous week or being available for work. It provides an indication of the size of the supply of labour available to engage in the production of goods and services, relative to the population aged 15–64 years old.*

*The data presented here refers only to those women who were employed during a specified brief period, either one week or one day, were in the following categories: paid employment or self-employment.*

In the second quarter of 2020, there have been significant drops in employment rates that have affected both women and men, and the pandemic’s devastation will worsen the figures. ILO says that women's employment dropped by 18.1% compared to men's, which dropped by 15.1%. Women's workforce is more extensive in the third sector, which is most affected by the pandemic. The number of employed women in this sector has fallen from 30 million to 24 million. In contrast, the number of employed women in the world (estimated at 510 million women) work in hard-hit sectors, compared to 36.6% of men's employment. Women are more likely than men to be employed in low-paid jobs and in occupations where they earn less than their male counterparts.

This pandemic affects women most acutely, and immigrants the most for a variety of reasons (for instance, they mostly work in the informal market, no new positions being created for young people or lack of legal papers to work). The global pandemic impacted employment rates in many ways; many companies have had to close down their operations due to the pandemic. Absenteeism has been a consequence of the pandemic since workers have not been able to attend their jobs' locations. Countries and countries have been designing ways to adapt to the temporary situation through fixing unemployment (e.g., income support).

The open unemployment rate is rising in women because the household care responsibilities have increased with the pandemic. Table 2 below clearly shows tendencies. According to ILO, Dominican Republic, Ecuador, and Colombia are the countries where the number of unemployed women has risen by 6.4 in the same period of 2019. Both people who were unemployed before the pandemic and those who lost their jobs because of COVID-19 are not looking for jobs. Figures are similar for both sexes in the Dominican Republic, Ecuador, and Colombia. Women have been more affected than men by unemployment less among women (by 7%) than among men (29.4%). The country is expected in an economic crisis. Fewer women are seeking employment because they are not only losing their jobs but also to the increase in care work, resulting in women not looking for paid work. While for men, who are still considered the breadwinners of the family, looking for jobs is easier after they lost their job due to the pandemic.

The fact that open unemployment has risen more for men (10%) than for women (6%), and more for men than for women (24%). It means that further research on open employment is required.

# 4. GESI approach in the E-Motion Program

Based on the findings of this assessment, there are two main goals that sub-programme 2 of "E- Motion: E-Mobility should achieve in terms of gender equality and social inclusion:

1. **Impact on the workforce:** reduction of sex segregation in employment while creating new jobs and expertise.
2. **Impact on the ridership:** reduction of gender biases and exclusions from transportation while changing the system to a low-emission one.

So, it is expected that for the E-Motion Program, the contribution to reducing the gender gap in e-mobility will be three-fold: promotion and to preventing and minimizing the harm of lacking gender equality and women's rights is three-folded. First, the transport sector is a catalyst to change social norms. For example, by providing visibility to women working in non-traditional roles in the EV (electric vehicle) market, it can help to reduce gender biases and exclusions from transportation while changing the system to a low-emission one.

This new e-transport paradigm will lever women's access to green jobs in traditionally sex-segregated occupations or improve access to investment loans for female entrepreneurs. Second, the E-Motion Program will promote gender equality at project level by means of designing the investment plans and the sub-projects as part of the program that will systematize gender mainstreaming in the transport sector.

The three-fold approach will be implemented across all activities as part of the Component 2 and its associated activities which will provide technical assistance to enable efficient financial assistance and to create a (gender-responsive) policy and business framework conducive for massive deployment of EVs. Activities at project level will promote gender equality by means of designing investment plans that are complementary of different endeavours. In addition, through the implementation of some activities as part of the sub-projects (e.g., women's skills training), this three-fold approach will reach all phases of the sub-projects and a wide profile of stakeholders.

Accordingly, the program will promote gender equality and women's empowerment at the project and sub-project level and support the use of gender-disaggregated indicators. The investments and sub-projects will be designed to ensure that they are complementary in terms of promoting women's mobility, promote safety and access to jobs. By establishing actions to increase women's mobility, safety, and access to jobs, the E-Motion Program will ensure women's agency in mobility if mainstreaming gender equality in its sub-projects becomes systematic.

In turn the proposed Action Plan, which organizes this approach in particular sub-activities aggregated by outputs, guarantees that general recommendations presented in this report are gender-responsive. The Action Plan also includes all the specific actions included in the E-Motion Program and project levels. Additionally, the Action Plan ensures the specific actions are measured and adequately monitored, and adjusted when needed.

[EU Women, 2020]

**Page 177**

It’s also important to highlight that all the activities outlined in the proposed Gender Action Plan emerge from and also provide order to socioeconomic, legal, institutional and mobility diagnosis developed in this Gender Assessment (particularly the four interrelated topics: mobility, safety and security, income and employment). These are presented as general recommendations. Below there are presented the three detailed outputs that structure the Gender Action Plan, closely related with the Components of the Program:

- Sector specific country-level gender assessments undertaken as part of the development of local gender equality strategies (national level)
- Successful knowledge exchange and collaboration on gender aspects in transportation (regional level)
- Gender components integrated in the sub-projects financed by the Program.

## Methodology

In this section is presented the methodology that has been used to come up with the Gender Assessment:

A desk-based research of aspects related to women in the region of Latin America and particularly in the three countries included in the Program (Panama, Paraguay, Uruguay) has been undertaken. This diagnosis aims at identifying gender equality gaps in general terms as well as the gender equality and women empowerment in urban transportation.

### Gender equality in the region of Latin America

As part of the first aspect, secondary and quantitative data has been used to analyze women situation regarding the following indicators: political representation, health education, employment, domestic violence, mobility, safety and security. The results show that a significant gender gap was found in all hag countries studies on gender or data sources from reliable international entities.

This chapter also includes the review of legal and institutional framework addressing gender-based violence at national level. Other public policy documents were reviewed as well as the main international agreements ratified to part of the national legislation.

### Assessment of gender equality and women empowerment in urban transportation

Mainly secondary and quantitative data has been used to analyze each of the three interrelated dimensions: mobility, safety and security, income and employment. The indicators analyzed are (i) mobility (ii) safety and security, and (iii) jobs. The data and analyses was found in ad hoc country studies on gender or data sources from reliable international entities.

Consistent with some studies that consider the availability of data in the sector, it is observed that in the last years there is more official and public information in the Region referring to women as transport users than as workers in the sector, although in some cases (e.g. Panama) it is not yet possible to have a clear picture on this issue. In other countries where more reliable information is available, it usually refers to the main cities or urban areas of each country. Consequently, the analysis presented here and mentioned below are based, as well as in other hag countries. So this report has relied on secondary review, data and analysis available for gender and transportation sector in general.

30


---
**Page 178**
---

There was also identified an information gap about safety and security in urban transport. This kind of sensitive data needs to be collected through qualitative strategies which imply specific professional abilities to be produced and added. In addition, it is important to highlight that initiatives should be implemented with the participation of women’s organizations or women organizations with the aim of making visible a gender problem that has not been well addressed. Which could be an economic constraint for the data to be systematically produced. In the few years in which this study was carried out, there were no specific studies on this topic and there have already started to work on generating primary data. The study about safety and security in urban transport is a first step towards understanding these issues.

Specific consultations or in-depth interviews with key social actors were not carried out during the preparation of this gender assessment, however it is foreseen to implement a brief qualitative fieldwork as part of the Action Plan in order to complement the diagnosis in each country.


### 6. Key areas of intervention and recommendations to strengthen gender aspects in the e-mobility programme

In the previous countries, gender equality has been improving in recent years, but the COVID-19 pandemic has worsened it. In light of the country snapshots on gender and the assessment of (i) mobility, (ii) safety and security, and (iii) jobs, this report concludes with the following recommendations to strengthen gender mainstreaming into the 4-Motion Subprogramme components:

The review of public policies at the country level will integrate gender aspects, this means that the technical assistance will help find obstacles and risks in the policies being reviewed that may impede women from accessing e-mobility. The three main areas of intervention (i) assessment (mobility, safety and security, and jobs), constitute a guide that the technical assistance will follow to identify the gender aspects of each policy. In addition, the sectoral investment plans will receive technical assistance to systematically include a gender assessment and gender action plan with concrete measures, activities and products that will ensure that women benefit equally from e-mobility.

Specific actions to guarantee women feel safe when using EV transportation, as workers and as users, are imperative in the region. Gender-based violence is endemic in these countries and underpinnings of this problem have been exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic. The COVID-19 pandemic is exacerbating the numbers. The urban transport system represents an environment where harassment and sexual abuse occur regularly in the regular trips women do every day. Incidents are increasing at night, in isolated vehicles and unattended places, but crowded areas also represent a risk for women who use charging stations.

The recent study “Ella se mueve segura” (She’s moving safely) provides a timely and adequate guide from the perspective of gender equality to address these issues. The study was carried out at speed thanks to online training. Successful ongoing initiatives such as “Bajate al acoso” in Quito, will be shared within the training. This initiative stands from others because it put together different institutions that work on this issue and has been able to reduce sexual harassment and gender-based harassment in the buses in Quito, which in the end, can change social norms towards respect for women’s rights.

Moreover, efforts should include, for instance, to enhance accessibility of jobs related to the e-mobility programme for women (advertise that all the vacancies are open for women, i.e. job descriptions and recruitment processes should be adapted to attract female candidates), increasing their visibility in the sector by creating awareness campaigns to raise awareness and prevent sexual harassment in the public transport sector.

## Key issue identified in gender assessment

### Mobility: Inadequate transportation infrastructure to reach services (work, education, training)

- **Description of issue or risk**: Limited number of services and facilities are accessible for women incorporating women's specific needs.
- **Actions to address issues**:
  - Identify opportunities to work with private sector companies that support women in the workforce (such as school services etc.), or working with the counterparts to provide transport services.
  - Women-specific requirements not adequately incorporated into project design.
    - Upfront gender assessment to identify opportunities to optimise the placement of charging points for EVs by women wherever applicable (e.g., residential areas, public areas).
- **Women's specific requirements not adequately incorporated into project design**:
  - Opportunities to work with women's groups (e.g., women drivers associations), self-help groups, and other women from low-income backgrounds. NGOs or government agencies (for employment) to identify opportunities and resources.
- **Limited sex-disaggregated data to improve gender analysis**: Opportunities for collaboration with women's groups (e.g., women drivers associations), self-help groups, and other women from low-income backgrounds. NGOs or government agencies (for employment) to identify opportunities and resources.

When implementing the SET document, the commitments of all stakeholders will be stated as well as their expected roles in establishing clearly roles and responsibilities. ### Institutionalization of gender equality in CAF and its partners

CAF undertook organizational restructuring from late 2001 to mid-2002 that has seen its commitment and strengthening of its Gender Unit. This Unit went up a level on the organizational ladder from being part of the Human Resources Department to becoming part of the Corporate Inclusion and Diversity Management, directly dependent of the Corporate Vice President of Strategic Programmes. The Gender Action Plan is another requisitly in this more explicit. Regarding its human resources, it currently engages four specialists.

There are three documents that frame CAF's approach towards gender equality: the Gender Equality Strategy, the Gender Equality Guidelines and a specific Gender Equity Safeguard (09) among the social and environmental safeguards. These instruments aim to reduce, mitigate and/or compensate for the unintended negative impacts of development projects.

CAF’s 2012-2015 Gender Equality Strategy aims to facilitate women's access to financial resources, dignified job opportunities, basic and care services, and productive development. As well, it will contribute to reducing gender inequality in CAF’s operations by promoting the reduction of teen pregnancy and to promote and strengthen the capacities, skills and competence for women’s leadership and their participation in decision-making spaces.

The Gender Equality Guidelines are an instrument aimed at guiding the implementation of actions that promote gender equality, rights and empowerment of women in the region. They are implemented through three guidelines: (i) direct action, to develop activities whose main objective is to reduce inequality; (ii) indirect action, to ensure that CAF’s operations are conducted from a gender perspective in its CAFs’ actions; and (iii) preventive action, whose purpose is to avoid the deepening of gaps or inequality that may result from CAF's operations.

The Gender Equity Safeguard in force since 2016 applies to all projects and operations financed by CAF. It is an instrument aimed at ensuring compliance with the Gender Equality Guidelines. Its applicability is determined during all phases of CAF’s credit cycle. The Safeguard seeks to ensure that both women and men have equal opportunities for access, participation and benefit from development activities equitably in the design and execution. It also seeks to prevent the projects and programs from deepening pre-existing gender gaps or producing adverse impacts.

Regarding its partners, it must be noted that GIZ, the German Agency for International Cooperation, approved a Gender Equality Strategy in 2013. This strategy is considered as an internal and external positioning of the organization. The Strategy aims to ensure that all activities, strategies and processes are gender-sensitive and contribute towards the promotion of gender equality and the elimination of discrimination and disadvantages based on gender.


---
**Page 183**
---

A GAP implementation team of four will be appointed: a coordinator and three country-based specialists. It will belong to the PMU.

### A Grievance Redress Mechanism (GRM) gender sensitive

It is expected that as part of compliance with the Environmental and Social Safeguards of CAF, the respective GRMs will be defined for each sub-project. This requirement will be part of the TOR or the technical specifications included as part of the procurement documents.

However, the request to prepare the GRM must ensure that they are adapted in order to cover, among other things, the specific risks and vulnerabilities arising from complaints about gender violence (SEA/SH) that which could come from the influx of large numbers of refugees and population.

The process of adaptation of the GRM to be gender sensitive will include significant emphasis on clarifying concepts of gender base violence and specifying what are the risks of gender base violence in the context of the project. It is also important to clarify how the GRM will adopt a different approach, the principles of confidentiality and anonymity, informed consent, secure data recording, referral pathways.

Even if the GRM for a sub-project is planned to be articulated with a government mechanism in place (national or local), it still needs to be needed to detail how it works (roles, responsibilities, complaints form, channels available, the procedures for grievance management, communication strategy, etc.).

This kind of systems must be serviced by trained personnel who can receive complaints with empathy and professionalism. In the case the GRM include a Community Grievance Focal Person it is recommended that this person should be part of the team responsible for the implementation of the sub-project, based on that, that must receive training, particularly on how to handle SEA/SH complaints.

# Gender Assessment and the E-Mobility Program

The E-Mobility and Low Carbon Transportation Program in Latin America aims to enable a large-scale regional transition towards low-carbon mobility in Latin America focusing on intensive use vehicle leading to reduced fossil fuel consumption, greenhouse gas emissions and air pollution. E-Mob will be implemented through two Funding Proposals: one by AFD [Sub-Program 1] as an Accredited Entity to cover 8 countries in Latin America, and one by CAF [Sub-Program 2]. The E-Mob Program is designed to have a comprehensive approach to establish an e-mobility conducive ecosystem at the local, national and regional levels, including the development of charging infrastructure, vehicle electrification, and the creation of gender-sensitive capacity building (all of which gender mainstreamed); to ensure a favorable e-mobility environment combined with financial instruments to ensure deployment of large-scale fleets thereby de-risking future investments into e-mobility of investors. The E-Mob Program will be implemented through two funding proposals containing combined with the implementation of business models and a policy framework conducive to EV deployment.

To ensure that the E-Mob Program is mainstreaming a gender perspective into its investment decisions, a Gender Assessment Report has been conducted (Annex B of the GEF Funding Proposal). The Gender Assessment Report evaluates the potential impact of the E-Mob Program on women’s access to and control over resources as well as the impact potential of the Program to enhance mobility, safety and access to jobs for women in urban transportation. Based on its findings, the report states specific recommendations on including a gender perspective for the Program implementation. These recommendations are the basis for the development of the following Gender Action Plan of the E-Mob Program.

For the E-Mob Program, the contribution to reducing the gender gap in e-mobility and low-carbon transportation and preventing and minimizing the harm of lacking gender equality and women’s rights is three-folded. First, the transport paradigm shift will provide new opportunities for women to access and control resources and investments against violence against women and girls, by providing visibility to women working in non-traditional roles in the (electric vehicle) industry. Second, this new transport paradigm will lower women’s access to green jobs in traditionally segregated occupations or improve access to investment loans for female entrepreneurs. Third, the capacity building, gender mainstreaming and awareness raising activities of the E-Mob Program will systematically mainstream gender in the transport sector. This three-folded approach will be mainly addressed as part of the Component 1 and its associated activities which will provide technical assistance to enable effective financial instruments and policy frameworks conducive to EV deployment. The other two components of the mission deployment of EVs. Activities will be coordinated and realized together with national authorities to ensure complementarity between them, and in addition to the mainstreaming of some activities as part of the Components 2, 3 and 4 this three-fold approach will reach all phases of the sub-projects and a wide profile of stakeholders.

The activities outlined in the present Gender Action Plan result in the following three outputs which will be specified below, including activities, indicators and targets, timeline, responsibilities and costs:

1. Sector specific country level gender assessments undertaken as part of the development of low-carbon electric vehicle (LCEV) projects at national and regional levels
2. Successful knowledge exchange and collaboration on gender aspects in transportation (regional level)
3. Gender components integrated in the sub-projects financed by the Program.

### Footnote:
1 The mobility of care refers to travel implications of daily tasks performed by individuals with care responsibilities, which are mainly performed by women. For more information see (De Madariaga, 2021).
